Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields I: equidistribution for nilsequences Thanks: The author was partially supported by the NSF Grant DMS-2247331
Wenbo Sun

Abstract
. This paper is the first part of the series Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields , aiming to develop the higher order Fourier analysis method along spheres over finite fields, and to solve the geometric Ramsey conjecture in the finite field setting. In this paper, we prove a quantitative equidistribution theorem for polynomial sequences in a nilmanifold, where the average is taken along spheres instead of cubes. To be more precise, let Ω ⊆ ℤ d \Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d} be the preimage of a sphere 𝔽 p d \mathbb{F}_{p}^{d} under the natural embedding from ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} to 𝔽 p d \mathbb{F}_{p}^{d} . We showed that if a rational polynomial sequence ( g ⁡ ( n ) ​ Γ ) n ∈ Ω (g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega} is not equidistributed on a nilmanifold G / Γ G/\Gamma , then there exists a nontrivial horizontal character η \eta of G / Γ G/\Gamma such that η ∘ g mod ℤ \eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z} vanishes on Ω \Omega . We also prove quantitative equidistribution and factorization theorems for more general sets arising from quadratic forms defined over 𝔽 p d \mathbb{F}_{p}^{d} . These results will serve as fundamental tools in later parts of the series to proof the spherical Gowers inverse theorem and the geometric Ramsey conjecture.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11T99, 37A99

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Spherical higher order Fourier analysis

Originated from

[
2
,
4
,
12
]
, higher order Fourier analysis is a theory that uses modern analytic methods to study problems in combinatorics. The area of higher order Fourier analysis grew rapidly in the last decade, and it was quickly seen to have much in common with areas of theoretical computer science related to polynomiality testing, as well as the asymptotics for various linear patterns in the prime numbers
[
5
,
7
]
.

The theory of higher order Fourier analysis is especially powerful in the study of Szemerédi type theorems concerning averages of linear forms of the form

\frac{1}{p^{d}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}f_{1}(L_{1}(n))\cdot\dots\cdot f_{k}(L_{k}(n)),

where
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\mathbb{C}
are bounded functions, and
L_{1},\dots,L_{k}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
are linear transformations.
However, nowadays very little is known for averages of linear forms along spheres:

(1.1)

\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{n\in\Omega}f_{1}(L_{1}(n))\cdot\dots\cdot f_{k}(L_{k}(n)),

where
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is set of zeros of a quadratic polynomial (for example, one can take
\Omega
to be the set of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
n\cdot n=r
for some fixed radius
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
). This pattern pertains to many important questions in combinatorics, including the geometric Ramsey conjecture (we postpone the details of this topic to
[
19
]
).

In this paper as well as in the series
[
17
,
18
,
19
]
, we develop the theory of higher order Fourier analysis in the “spherical setting”, which enables us to study patterns of the form (
1.1
) and to provide an affirmative answer to the geometric Ramsey conjecture in the finite field setting.

1.2.
Equidistribution for spherical averages on nilmanifolds

This paper is the first part of the series
Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields

[
17
,
18
,
19
]
.
The purpose of this paper is to provide equidistribution theorems we need for the study of spherical higher order Fourier analysis. In the last decade, it has come to be appreciated that nilmanifolds and nilsequences play a fundamental role in combinatorial number theory, ergodic theory and additive combinatorics (see for example
[
5
,
7
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
).
We begin with recalling some standard terminologies in this area:

Definition

(Filtered group)
.

Let
G
be a group. An
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
on
G
is a collection
G_{\mathbb{N}}=(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subgroups of
G
indexed by
\mathbb{N}
such that the following holds:

(1)

for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
i\leq j
, we have that
G_{i}\supseteq G_{j}
;

(2)

for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
[G_{i},G_{j}]\subseteq G_{i+j}
.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
G
is an
(
\mathbb{N}
-filtered) nilpotent group
of
degree
at most
s
(or of
degree

\leq s
) with respect to some
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
if
G_{i}
is trivial whenever
i>s
.

Definition

(Nilmanifold)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a discrete and cocompact subgroup of a connected, simply-connected nilpotent Lie group
G
with filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}=(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\Gamma_{i}:=\Gamma\cap G_{i}
is a cocompact subgroup of
G_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then we say that
G/\Gamma
is an
(
\mathbb{N}
-filtered) nilmanifold
, and we use
(G/\Gamma)_{\mathbb{N}}
to denote the collection
(G_{i}/\Gamma_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
(which is called the
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
of
G/\Gamma
). We say that
G/\Gamma
has degree
\leq s
with respect to
(G/\Gamma)_{\mathbb{N}}
if
G
has degree
\leq s
with respect to
G_{\mathbb{N}}
.

Definition

(Polynomial sequences)
.

Let
d,\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
G
be a connected simply-connected nilpotent Lie group and
(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtration of
G
.
Denote
\Delta_{h}g(n):=g(n+h)g(n)^{-1}

for all
n,h\in G
.
A map
g\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G
is an
(
\mathbb{N}
-filtered) polynomial sequence
if

\Delta_{h_{m}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)\in G_{m}

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
n,h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. The set of all
\mathbb{N}
-filtered polynomial sequences is denoted by
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.

A fundamental question in higher order Fourier analysis is to study the equidistribution property of polynomial sequences.
Let
\Omega
be a non-empty subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold. A sequence
\mathcal{O}\colon\Omega\to G/\Gamma
is
\delta
-equidistributed
on
G/\Gamma
if for all Lipschitz function
F\colon G/\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
(with respect to the metric
d_{G/\Gamma}
to be defined in Section
3
), we have that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\Bigl|\frac{1}{|\Omega\cap[N]^{d}|}\sum_{n\in\Omega\cap[N]^{d}}F(\mathcal{O}(n))-\int_{G/\Gamma}F\,dm_{G/\Gamma}\Bigr|\leq\delta\|F\|_{\text{Lip}},

where
m_{G/\Gamma}
is the Haar measure of
G/\Gamma
and the
Lipschitz norm
is defined as

\|F\|_{\text{Lip}}:=\sup_{x\in G/\Gamma}|F(x)|+\sup_{x,y\in G/\Gamma,x\neq y}\frac{|F(x)-F(y)|}{d_{G/\Gamma}(x,y)}.

We say that
\mathcal{O}
is
\delta
-totally equidistributed
on
G/\Gamma
if for all
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r<\delta^{-1}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, the sequence
(\mathcal{O}(n))_{n\in\Omega\cap(r\mathbb{Z}^{d}+m)}
is
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
.

It is known that the equidistribution property of polynomial sequences is connected to the
horizontal characters
of
G/\Gamma
, i.e. group homomorphisms
\eta\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
with
\eta(\Gamma)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
.
It was proved by Green and Tao
[
8
,
9
]
that for any
\delta>0
and any polynomial sequence
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
, either
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
or there exists a nontrivial horizontal character
\eta
of
G/\Gamma
whose complexity is comparable with
G/\Gamma
(we will define complexity formally in Section
3
) such that
\eta\circ g
is a slowly varying function. This is a quantitative generalization of a result of Leibman
[
15
]
.

In this paper, we study the quantitative equidistribution property for sequences of the form
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
, where
\Omega=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon n\cdot n\equiv r\mod p\mathbb{Z}\}
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
(see
[
14
,
21
]
for the study of related averages).
The following is the main result of this paper (we refer the readers to Section
3
for definitions):

Theorem 1.1

(Equidistribution for polynomial sequence along spheres)
.

Let
0<\delta<1/2,C>0,d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq s+13
and
p\gg_{C,d}\delta^{-O_{C,d}(1)}
be a prime. Denote
\Omega=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon n\cdot n\equiv r\mod p\mathbb{Z}\}
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of complexity at most
C
and
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
be a rational polynomial sequence.
Then either
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
is
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
, or
there exists a nontrivial horizontal character
\eta
of complexity at most
O_{\delta,d}(1)
such that
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega
.

In fact, we will prove a stronger version of Theorem
1.1
, which is Theorem
5.1
in this paper.

Remark 1.2
.

Note that in Theorem
1.1
, it is not possible to require
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
to be constant on the entire space
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. For example, let
G/\Gamma=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
,
g(n)=(n\cdot n)/p
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Since
g(n)\equiv r/p\mod\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\Omega
, it is not hard to see that
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
. On the other hand, since any nontrivial horizontal character of
G/\Gamma
is of the form
\eta(x):=kx
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
, we have that
\eta\circ g(n)\mod\mathbb{Z}=k(n\cdot n)/p\mod\mathbb{Z}
can not be a constant.

It is worth noting that in order for Theorem
1.1
to be useful in
[
18
,
19
]
, we must prove this result for rational polynomials instead of just for
p
-periodic ones. See Remark
5.2
for more details.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\{0,\dots,p-1\}^{d}
denote the natural embedding of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
By taking
g
to be a polynomial from
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to
\mathbb{Z}/p
,
G/\Gamma=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and
F(x)=\exp(x):=e^{2\pi ix}
, we have the following immediate corollary for exponential sums along spheres, which is of independent interest in number theory.

Corollary 1.3

(Weyl’s equisidtribution theorem along spheres over finite field)
.

Let
0<\delta<1/2,d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq s+13
and
p
be a prime. Let
g\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}
be an integer valued polynomial of degree
s
. Denote
\Omega=\{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon n\cdot n=r\}
for some
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
If
p\gg_{d}\delta^{-O_{d}(1)}
, then either

\Bigl|\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{n\in\Omega}\exp(g\circ\tau(n)/p)\Bigr|<\delta

or

g\circ\tau(n)/p\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega
.
1
1

1

By Corollary
7.3
, we may further conclude that
g(n)=(n\cdot n-\tau(r))g_{1}(n)+pg_{2}(n)
for some integer valued polynomials
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.

Motivated by
[
1
,
8
,
9
]
,
it is natural to ask whether Theorem
1.1
implies a factorization theorem, i.e. whether one can decompose
g
as the product of a constant
\epsilon
, a polynomial sequence
g^{\prime}
being equidistributed on a subnilmanifold of
G/\Gamma
, and a polynomial sequence
\gamma
having strong periodic properties. This is indeed the case (we refer the readers to Section
3
for definitions):

Theorem 1.4

(Strong factorization property along spheres)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq s+13
,
C>0
,
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a growth function,
2
2

2

A
growth function

\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a non-decreasing function with
\mathcal{F}(n)\geq n
for all
n\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
and
p\gg_{C,d,\mathcal{F}}1
be a prime. Denote
\Omega=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon n\cdot n\equiv r\mod p\mathbb{Z}\}
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
n_{\ast}\in\Omega
.
Let
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of complexity at most
C
, and let
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
be a rational polynomial sequence on
\Omega
. There exist some
C\leq C^{\prime}\leq O_{C,d,\mathcal{F}}(1)
,
a proper subgroup
G^{\prime}
of
G
which is
C^{\prime}
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
, and
a factorization

g(n)=\epsilon g^{\prime}(n)\gamma(n)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

such that

\epsilon\in G
is of complexity
O_{C^{\prime}}(1)
,

g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})
is rational and
(g^{\prime}(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
is
\mathcal{F}(C^{\prime})^{-1}
-totally equidistributed on
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}\cap\Gamma
, and that
\gamma\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
is
partially
r
-rational with base point
n_{\ast}
and partially
r
-periodic on
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)

for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\leq O_{C,d,s}(C^{\prime})
.

In fact, we will prove a stronger version of Theorem
1.4
, which is Theorem
11.5
in this paper.

It is natural to ask whether one can extend Theorems
1.1
and
1.4
to more general sets
\Omega
. In fact, in the series
[
17
,
18
,
19
]
, we will frequent work with those
\Omega
which is the set of solutions of a system of quadratic equations (whereas the set
\Omega
defined in Theorems
1.1
and
1.4
is the set of solution for a single quadratic equation). In this paper, we provide such generalizations of Theorems
1.1
and
1.4
in Theorems
10.6
and
11.5
respectively (see also Theorem
11.2
for a variation of Theorem
11.5
).

1.3.
Organization of the paper

We first provide the background material for polynomials, nilmanifolds and quadratic forms in Sections
2
(as well as Appendix
A
),
3
and
4
respectively. Then in Section
5
,
we state the main equidistribution result of the paper (i.e. Theorem
5.1
, which is a generalization of Theorem
1.1
) and explain the outline of its proof.

Sections
6
,
7
and
8
are devoted to the proof of Theorem
5.1
.
In Section
6
, we provide the solutions to some relevant algebraic equations for
p
-periodic polynomials.
Among other
results, we solve a polynomial equation of the form (
6.29
), which plays a central role in the proof of Theorem
1.1
for
p
-periodic polynomial sequences. Then in Section
7
, we extend all the results in Section
6
to the non-periodic setting. Finally, in Section
8
, we combine the results from Sections
6
and
7
with the approach of Green and Tao
[
8
,
9
]
to complete the proof of Theorem
5.1
. We refer the readers to Section
5.2
for a more detailed explanation of this method.

For the study in
[
18
,
19
]
, we need to extend Theorem
1.1
to a more general setting. In Section
9
, we defined a large family of sets arising from quadratic equations (call the
M
-sets
), and provide some irreducible properties for such sets. In Section
10
, we extend Theorem
1.1
to a large class of
M
-sets (Theorem
10.6
). For convenience, we introduce a concept called the
Leibman dichotomy
which characterizes the phenomenon described by Theorem
1.1
.
In Sections
11
, we provide two factorization theorems (Theorems
11.2
and
11.5
) for large class of
M
-sets, which will play important roles in
[
18
,
19
]
.

Unfortunately the proofs of many results in Sections
9
and
10
are rather involved. In order for the readers to follow the ideas of the paper more easily, we postpone the proofs of some very technical (but also very important) results in Appendices
B
,
C
,
D
, and
E
.

In Section
12
, we collect some interesting open questions.

We thank James Leng for helpful discussions.

1.4.
Definitions and notations

Convention 1.5
.

Throughout this paper, we use

\tau\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\{0,\dots,p-1\}
to denote the natural bijective embedding, and use
\iota
to denote the map from
\mathbb{Z}
(or
\mathbb{Z}_{K}
for any
K
divisible by
p
) to
\mathbb{F}_{p}
given by
\iota(n):=\tau^{-1}(n\mod p\mathbb{Z})
.
We also use

\tau
to denote the map from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
to
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
(or
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{k}
) given by
\tau(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=(\tau(x_{1}),\dots,\tau(x_{k}))
,
and

\iota
to denote the map from
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
(or
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{k}
) to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
given by
\iota(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=(\iota(x_{1}),\dots,

\iota(x_{k}))
.
When there is no confusion, we will not state the domain and range of
\tau
and
\iota
explicitly.

We may also extend the domain of
\iota
to all the rational numbers of the form
x/y
with
(x,y)=1,x\in\mathbb{Z},y\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash p\mathbb{Z}
by setting
\iota(x/y):=\iota(xy^{\ast})
, where
y^{\ast}
is any integer with
yy^{\ast}\equiv 1\mod p\mathbb{Z}
.

Below are the notations we use in this paper:

•

Let
\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{+},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}+,\mathbb{C}
denote the set of non-negative integers, positive integers, integers, rational numbers, real numbers, positive real numbers, and complex numbers, respectively. Denote
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\mathbb{F}_{p}
denote the finite field with
p
elements. Let
\mathbb{Z}_{K}
denote the cyclic group with
K
elements.

•

Throughout this paper,
d
is a fixed positive integer and
p
is a prime number.

•

Throughout this paper, unless otherwise stated, all vectors are assumed to be horizontal vectors.

•

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a collection of parameters and
A,B,c\in\mathbb{R}
. We write
A\gg_{\mathcal{C}}B
if
|A|\geq K|B|
and
A=O_{\mathcal{C}}(B)
if
|A|\leq K|B|
for some
K>0
depending only on the parameters in
\mathcal{C}
.
In the above definitions, we allow the set
\mathcal{C}
to be empty. In this case
K
will be a universal constant.

•

Let
[N]
denote the set
\{0,\dots,N-1\}
.

•

For
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, denote
|i|:=|i_{1}|+\dots+|i_{k}|
.
For
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, denote
n^{i}:=n_{1}^{i_{1}}\dots n_{k}^{i_{k}}
and
i!:=i_{1}!\dots i_{k}!
.
For
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, denote
\binom{n}{i}:=\binom{n_{1}}{i_{1}}\dots\binom{n_{k}}{i_{k}}
.

•

For any set
F
, let
F[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}]
denote the set of all polynomials in the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
whose coefficients are from
F
.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\lfloor x\rfloor
denote the largest integer which is not larger than
x
, and
\lceil x\rceil
denote the smallest integer which is not smaller than
x
. Denote
\{x\}:=x-\lfloor x\rfloor
.

•

Let
X
be a finite set and
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
be a function. Denote
\mathbb{E}_{x\in X}f(x):=\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}f(x)
, the average of
f
on
X
.

•

We say that a set
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is
Q
-periodic
if
\Omega=\Omega+Q\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

•

If
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is a
Q
-periodic set and
f\colon\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{C}
is such that
f(n)=f(n+Qm)
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
n,n+Qm\in\Omega
. Then we denote
\mathbb{E}_{x\in\Omega}f(x):=\mathbb{E}_{x\in\Omega\cap[Q]^{k}}f(x)
.

•

For
F=\mathbb{Z}^{k}
or
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, and
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}),y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})\in F
, let
x\cdot y\in\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{F}_{p}
denote the dot product given by

x\cdot y:=x_{1}y_{1}+\dots+x_{k}y_{k}.

•

Let
\exp\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
denote the function
\exp(x):=e^{2\pi ix}
.

•

If
G
is a connected, simply connected Lie group, then we use
\log G
to denote its Lie algebra. Let
\exp\colon\log G\to G
be the exponential map, and
\log\colon G\to\log G
be the logarithm map. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
g\in G
, denote
g^{t}:=\exp(t\log g)
.

•

If
f\colon H\to G
is a function from an abelian group
H=(H,+)
to some group
(G,\cdot)
, denote
\Delta_{h}f(n):=f(n+h)\cdot f(n)^{-1}
for all
n,h\in H
.

•

We write affine subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
as
V+c
, where
V
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
passing through
\mathbb{0}
, and
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

Let
D,D^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
C>0
.
Here are some basic notions of complexities:

•

Real and complex numbers:
a number
r\in\mathbb{R}
is of
complexity
at most
C
if
r=a/b
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with
-C\leq a,b\leq C
. If
r\notin\mathbb{Q}
, then we say that the complexity of
r
is infinity. A complex number is of
complexity
at most
C
if both its real and imaginary parts are of complexity at most
C
.

•

Vectors and matrices:
a vector or matrix is of
complexity
at most
C
if all of its entries are of complexity at most
C
.

•

Subspaces:
a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
is of
complexity
at most
C
if it is the null space of a matrix of complexity at most
C
.

•

Linear transformations:
let
L\colon\mathbb{C}^{D}\to\mathbb{C}^{D^{\prime}}
be a linear transformation. Then
L
is associated with an
D\times D^{\prime}
matrix
A
in
\mathbb{C}
. We say that
L
is of
complexity
at most
C
if
A
is of complexity at most
C
.

•

Lipschitz functions:
a Lipschitz function is of
complexity
at most
C
if its Lipschitz norm is at most
C
.

2.
Background material for polynomials

Definition 2.1

(Polynomials in finite field)
.

Let
\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}^{d^{\prime}}_{p})
be the collection of all functions
f\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
of the form

(2.1)

f(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=\sum_{0\leq a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\leq p-1,a_{i}\in\mathbb{N}}C_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}n^{a_{1}}_{1}\dots n^{a_{d-1}}_{d}

for some
C_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}}
.
Let
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}^{d^{\prime}}_{p})
be a function which is not constant zero.
The
degree of
f
(denoted as
\deg(f)
) is the largest
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
C_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}\neq\mathbb{0}
for some
a_{1}+\dots+a_{d}=r
. We say that
f
is
homogeneous of degree
r
if
C_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}\neq\mathbb{0}
implies that
a_{1}+\dots+a_{d}=r
. We say that
f
is a
linear transformation
if
f
is homogeneous of degree 1.

Convention 2.2
.

For convenience, the
degree
of the constant zero function is allowed to take any integer value. For example, 0 can be regarded as a homogeneous polynomial of degree 10, as a linear transformation, or as a polynomial of degree -1. Here we also adopt the convention that the only polynomial of negative degree is 0.

In this paper, we will work with polynomials which satisfy various types of periodicity properties. To this end, we introduce the following notations:

Definition 2.3

(Partially
p
-periodic polynomials)
.

For any subset
R
of
\mathbb{R}
,
let
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to R)
be the collection of all polynomials in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
taking values in
R
. For any prime
p
, subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and sets
R^{\prime}\subseteq R\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, let
\text{poly}(\Omega\to R|R^{\prime})
denote the set of all
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to R)
such that
f(n)\in R
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and that
f(n)\in R^{\prime}
for all
n\in\Omega
.
Let
\text{poly}_{Q}(\Omega\to R|R^{\prime})
denote the set of all
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to R)
such that
f(n)\in R
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and that
f(n+Qm)-f(n)\in R^{\prime}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
n,n+Qm\in\Omega
.

A polynomial
f
in
\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{Z})
is called a
partially
p
-periodic polynomial on
\Omega
.

We use some examples to illustrate the meanings of these notions:

Example 2.4
.

The set

\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to R|R^{\prime})
is simply the set of all
R^{\prime}
-valued polynomials. In other words,
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to R|R^{\prime})=\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to R^{\prime})
.

Example 2.5
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
be a quadratic form, i.e.
M(n)=((nA)\cdot n+v\cdot n+b)/p
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for some
d\times d
integer matrix
A
,
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
V_{p}(M)
denote the set of
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
M(n+pm)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
(which is the same as the set of
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
M(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
). For any set
F
,
\text{poly}(V_{p}(M)\to F|\mathbb{Z})
is the set of all
F
-valued polynomials
f\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to F
such that
f(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
M(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\text{poly}_{p}(V_{p}(M)\to F|\mathbb{Z})
is the set of all
F
-valued polynomials
f\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
such that
f(n+pm)-f(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
M(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Example 2.6
.

A polynomial
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
belongs to
\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p|\mathbb{Z})
if
f
is
p
-periodic
, meaning that
f(n+pm)-f(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Clearly every polynomial with
\mathbb{Z}/p
-coefficients belongs to
\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p|\mathbb{Z})
, and
\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p|\mathbb{Z})
is contained in
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
.
On the other hand,
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
is not contained in
\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p|\mathbb{Z})

(consider for example
f(n)=n^{p}/p^{2},d=1
).
However, the following lemma shows that polynomials in
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
with degree less than
p
always belong to
\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p|\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Every polynomial
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
of degree smaller than
p
belongs to
f\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p|\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Let
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
with
\deg(f)<p
. Since
p
is a prime, by the multivariate polynomial
interpolation, we may write
f(n)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|i|\leq s}\frac{a_{i}}{Qp}n^{i}
for some
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
Q\in\mathbb{Z},p\nmid Q
. Then for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
f(n+pm)-f(n)\in\mathbb{Z}/Q
. On the other hand, since
f
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}/p
, we have that
f(n+pm)-f(n)\in(\mathbb{Z}/Q)\cap(\mathbb{Z}/p)=\mathbb{Z}
, and thus
f\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p|\mathbb{Z})
.
∎

We provide another simple lemma for
p
-periodic polynomials which we will use in later sections.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime, and
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z})
be an integer valued polynomial of degree smaller than
p
. There exist integer valued polynomial
f_{1}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z})
and integer coefficient polynomial
f_{2}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z})
both having degrees at most
\deg(f)
such that
\frac{1}{p}f=f_{1}+\frac{1}{p}f_{2}
.

Proof.

Denote
s:=\deg(f)<p
.
By the multivariate polynomial interpolation, we may write

f(n)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|i|\leq s}\frac{a_{i}}{Q}n^{i}

for some
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
Q\in\mathbb{Z},p\nmid Q
. Let
Q^{\ast}\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
Q^{\ast}Q\equiv 1\mod p\mathbb{Z}
and set

f_{2}(n):=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|i|\leq s}Q^{\ast}a_{i}n^{i}.

Then
f_{2}
is an integer coefficient polynomial of degree at most
s
. Note that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\frac{1}{p}(f(n)-f_{2}(n))=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|i|\leq s}\frac{a_{i}}{Q}\frac{1-Q^{\ast}Q}{p}n^{i},

the left hand side of which belongs to
\mathbb{Z}/p
and the right hand side of which belongs to
\mathbb{Z}/Q
. So
f_{1}(n):=\frac{1}{p}(f(n)-f_{2}(n))
takes values in
(\mathbb{Z}/Q)\cap(\mathbb{Z}/p)=\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

There is a natural correspondence between polynomials taking values in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and polynomials taking values in
\mathbb{Z}/p
. Let
F\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}})
and
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
be polynomials of degree at most
s
for some
s<p
. If
F=\iota\circ pf\circ\tau
, then we say that
F
is
induced
by
f
and
f
is a
lifting
of
F
.
3
3

3

Note that since
pf\circ\tau
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}^{d^{\prime}}
, the coefficients of
pf\circ\tau
belongs to
\mathbb{Z}^{d^{\prime}}/s!
, and thus
\iota\circ pf\circ\tau
is well defined by Convention
1.5
.

We say that
f
is a
regular lifting
of
F
if in addition
f
has the same degree as
F
and
f
has
\{0,\frac{1}{p},\dots,\frac{p-1}{p}\}
-coefficients. We refer the readers to Appendix
A
for the basic properties of liftings.

3.
Background material for nilmanifolds

3.1.
Nilmanifolds, filtrations and polynomial sequences

We start with some basic definitions on nilmanifolds.
Let
G
be a group and
g,h\in G
. Denote
[g,h]:=g^{-1}h^{-1}gh
. For subgroups
H,H^{\prime}
of
G
, let
[H,H^{\prime}]
denote the group generated by
[h,h^{\prime}]
for all
h\in H
and
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
.

Definition 3.1

(Filtered group)
.

Let
G
be a group. An
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
on
G
is a collection
G_{\mathbb{N}}=(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subgroups of
G
indexed by
\mathbb{N}
with
G_{0}=G
such that the following holds:

(1)

for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
i\leq j
, we have that
G_{i}\supseteq G_{j}
;

(2)

for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
[G_{i},G_{j}]\subseteq G_{i+j}
.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
G
is an
(
\mathbb{N}
-filtered) nilpotent group
of
degree
at most
s
(or of
degree

\leq s
) with respect to some
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
if
G_{i}
is trivial whenever
i>s
.

Example 3.2
.

The
lower central series
of a group
G
is the
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
(G^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
given by
G^{0}:=G
and
G^{i+1}=[G^{i},G]
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
G
is a
nilpotent group
of step (or degree) at most
s
(with respect to the lower central series) if
G^{i}
is trivial for all
i\geq s+1
.

Definition 3.3

(Nilmanifold)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a discrete and cocompact subgroup of a connected, simply-connected nilpotent Lie group
G
with filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}=(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\Gamma_{i}:=\Gamma\cap G_{i}
is a cocompact subgroup of
G_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
4
4

4

In some papers, such
\Gamma_{i}
is called a
rational
subgroup of
G
.

Then we say that
G/\Gamma
is an
(
\mathbb{N}
-filtered) nilmanifold
, and we use
(G/\Gamma)_{\mathbb{N}}
to denote the collection
(G_{i}/\Gamma_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
(which is called the
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
of
G/\Gamma
). We say that
G/\Gamma
has degree
\leq s
with respect to
(G/\Gamma)_{\mathbb{N}}
if
G
has degree
\leq s
with respect to
G_{\mathbb{N}}
.
5
5

5

Unlike the convention in literature, for our convenience, we do not require
G_{0}=G
to be the same as
G_{1}
.

We also need to define some special types of nilmanifolds.

Definition 3.4

(Sub-nilmanifold)
.

Let
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of degree
\leq s
with filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
and
H
be a rational subgroup of
G
. Then
H/(H\cap\Gamma)
is also an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of degree
\leq s
with the filtration
H_{\mathbb{N}}
given by
H_{i}:=G_{i}\cap H
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
(see Example 6.14 of
[
11
]
). We say that
H/(H\cap\Gamma)
is a
sub-nilmanifold
of
G/\Gamma
,
H_{\mathbb{N}}
(or
(H/(H\cap\Gamma))_{\mathbb{N}}
) is the filtration
induced by

G_{\mathbb{N}}
(or
(G/\Gamma)_{\mathbb{N}}
).

Definition 3.5

(Quotient nilmanifold)
.

Let
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of degree
\leq s
with filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
and
H
be a normal subgroup of
G
. Then
(G/H)/(\Gamma/(\Gamma\cap H))
is also an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of most
\leq s
with the filtration
(G/H)_{\mathbb{N}}
given by
(G/H)_{i}:=G_{i}/(H\cap G_{i})
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
(G/H)/(\Gamma/(\Gamma\cap H))
is the
quotient nilmanifold
of
G/\Gamma
by
H
and that
(G/H)_{\mathbb{N}}
is the filtration
induced by

G_{\mathbb{N}}
.

Definition 3.6

(Product nilmanifold)
.

Let
G/\Gamma
and
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
be
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifolds of degree
\leq s
with filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
and
G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}}
. Then
G\times G^{\prime}/\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}
is also an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of most
\subseteq J
with the filtration
(G\times G^{\prime}/\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime})_{\mathbb{N}}
given by
(G\times G^{\prime}/\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime})_{i}:=G_{i}\times G^{\prime}_{i}/\Gamma_{i}\times\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
G\times G^{\prime}/\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}
is the
product nilmanifold
of
G/\Gamma
and
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
and that
(G\times G^{\prime}/\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime})_{\mathbb{N}}
is the filtration
induced by

G_{\mathbb{N}}
and
G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}}
.

We remark that although we used the same terminology “induce” in Definitions
3.4
,
3.5
and
3.6
, this will not cause any confusion in the paper as the meaning of “induce” will be clear from the context.

Definition 3.7

(Filtered homomorphism)
.

An
(
\mathbb{N}
-filtered) homomorphism

\phi\colon G/\Gamma\to G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
between two
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifolds is a group homomorphism
\phi\colon G\to G^{\prime}
which maps
\Gamma
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
and maps
G_{i}
to
G^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Every nilmanifold has an explicit algebraic description by using the Mal’cev basis:

Definition 3.8

(Mal’cev basis)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold of step at most
s
with the
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Let
\dim(G)=m
and
\dim(G_{i})=m_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq s
. A basis
\mathcal{X}:=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\}
for the Lie algebra
\log G
of
G
(over
\mathbb{R}
) is a
Mal’cev basis
for
G/\Gamma
adapted to the filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
if

•

for all
0\leq j\leq m-1
,
\log H_{j}:=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\xi_{j+1},\dots,\xi_{m}\}
is a Lie algebra ideal of
\log G
and so
H_{j}:=\exp(\log H_{j})
is a normal Lie subgroup of
G;

•

G_{i}=H_{m-m_{i}}
for all
0\leq i\leq s
;

•

the map
\psi^{-1}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to G
given by

\psi^{-1}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})=\exp(t_{1}X_{1})\dots\exp(t_{m}X_{m})

is a bijection;

•

\Gamma=\psi^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
.

We call
\psi
the
Mal’cev coordinate map
with respect to the Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
.
If
g=\psi^{-1}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
, we say that
(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
are the
Mal’cev coordinates
of
g
with respect to
\mathcal{X}
.

We say that the Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
is
C
-rational
(or of
complexity
at most
C
) if all the structure constants
c_{i,j,k}
in the relations

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k}c_{i,j,k}X_{k}

are rational with complexity at most
C
.

For any
h\in G
, there is a unique way to write
h
as

h=\{h\}[h]

such that
\psi(\{h\})\in[0,1)^{m}
and
[h]\in\Gamma
. We adopt this notation throughout this paper.

It is known that for every filtration
G_{\bullet}
which is rational for
\Gamma
, there exists a Mal’cev basis adapted to it. See for example the discussion on pages 11–12 of
[
8
]
.

We use the following quantities to describe the complexities of the objected defined above.

Definition 3.9

(Notions of complexities for nilmanifolds)
.

Let
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold with an
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
and a Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{D}\}
adapted to it.
We say that
G/\Gamma
is of
complexity
at most
C
if the Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
is
C
-rational and
\dim(G)\leq C
.

An element
g\in G
is of
complexity
at most
C
(with respect to the Mal’cev coordinate map
\psi\colon G/\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
) if
\psi(g)\in[-C,C]^{m}
.

Let
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
be a nilmanifold endowed with the Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{X^{\prime}_{1},\dots,X^{\prime}_{D^{\prime}}\}
respectively. Let
\phi\colon G/\Gamma\to G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
be a filtered homomorphism, we say that
\phi
is of
complexity
at most
C
if the map
X_{i}\to\sum_{j}a_{i,j}X^{\prime}_{j}
induced by
\phi
is such that all
a_{i,j}
are of complexity at most
C
.

Let
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
be a closed connected subgroup. We say that
G^{\prime}
is
C
-rational
(or of
complexity
at most
C
) relative to
\mathcal{X}
if the Lie algebra
\log G
has a basis consisting of linear combinations
\sum_{i}a_{i}X_{i}
such that
a_{i}
are rational numbers of complexity at most
C
.

Convention 3.10
.

In the rest of the paper, all nilmanifolds are assumed to have a fixed filtration, Mal’cev basis and a smooth Riemannian metric induced by the Mal’cev basis. Therefore, we will simply say that a nilmanifold, Lipschitz function, sub-nilmanifold etc. is of complexity
C
without mentioning the reference filtration and Mal’cev basis.

Definition 3.11

(Polynomial sequences)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
G
be a connected simply-connected nilpotent Lie group. Let
(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtration of
G
. A map
g\colon\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G
is a
(
\mathbb{N}
-filtered) polynomial sequence
if

\Delta_{h_{m}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)\in G_{m}

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
n,h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
6
6

6

Recall that
\Delta_{h}g(n):=g(n+h)g(n)^{-1}

for all
n,h\in H
.

The set of all
\mathbb{N}
-filtered polynomial sequences is denoted by
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.

By Corollary B.4 of
[
11
]
,
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
is a group with respect to the pointwise multiplicative operation.
We refer the readers to Appendix B of
[
11
]
for more properties for polynomial sequences.

3.2.
Different notions of polynomial sequences

We start with some conventional definitions for polynomial sequences.

Definition 3.12

(null, rational and periodic polynomial sequences)
.

Let
k,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold of step at most
s
, and
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
. We say that
g
is

•

null
if
g(n)\in\Gamma
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
;

•

rational
if
g(Qn)\in\Gamma
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
for some
Q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, in which case we also say that
g
is
Q
-rational
;

•

periodic
if
g(n+Qm)^{-1}g(n)\in\Gamma
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
for some
Q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, in which case we also say that
g
is
Q
-periodic
.

We refer the readers to Appendix A of
[
8
]
for properties of rational/periodic polynomial sequences.

In this paper, since we will work with polynomial sequences which are null, rational or periodic on certain subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we introduce the following notations:

Definition 3.13

(Partially null, rational and periodic polynomial sequences)
.

Let
k,Q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold,
\Omega
be a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, and
n_{\ast}\in\Omega
.

•

We use
\text{poly}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
to denote the set of all
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
which is
partially null on
\Omega
,
meaning that
g(n)\in\Gamma
for all
n\in\Omega
.

•

We use
\text{poly}_{\approx Q,n_{\ast}}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
to denote the set of all
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
which is
partially
Q
-rational on
\Omega
with base point
n_{\ast}
meaning that
g(n_{\ast}+Qm)\in\Gamma
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
n_{\ast}+Qm\in\Omega
.

•

We use
\text{poly}_{Q}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
to denote the set of all
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
which is
partially
Q
-periodic on
\Omega
,
meaning that
g(n+Qm)^{-1}g(n)\in\Gamma
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
n,n+Qm\in\Omega
.

Example 3.14
.

The set

\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
is simply the set of all
\Gamma
-valued (i.e. null) polynomials. In other words,
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{N}})=\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. The set
\text{poly}_{\approx Q,\mathbb{0}}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
is the set of
Q
-rational polynomial sequences in
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
. The set
\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
is the set of
p
-periodic polynomial sequences in
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.

Example 3.15
.

Let
(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{N}}
be the nilmanifold with the filtration given by
G_{0}=\dots=G_{s}=\mathbb{R}
and
G_{i}=\{id_{G}\}
for
i>s
. Then
\text{poly}(\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{N}}|\mathbb{Z})
is the collection of all polynomials in
\text{poly}(\Omega\to\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{Z})
(defined in Definition
2.3
) of degree at most
s
, and
\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{N}}|\mathbb{Z})
is the collection of all polynomials in
\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{Z})
(defined in Definition
2.3
) of degree at most
s
.

Example 3.16
.

By Corollary B.4 of
[
11
]
,
it is not hard to see that
\text{poly}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
and
\text{poly}_{\approx Q,n_{\ast}}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
are groups.
However, we caution the readers that
\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
is not necessarily a group. Let
k=1
and
G=(\mathbb{R}^{3},\ast)
be the Heisenberg group with the group action given by

(x,y,z)\ast(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}):=(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime},z+z^{\prime}+xy^{\prime})

with the lower central series as its
\mathbb{N}
-filtration, meaning that
G_{0}=G_{1}=G
,
G_{2}=\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
and
G_{s}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
for
s\geq 3
.
Indeed, let
d=k=1
,
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}^{3}
,
g_{1},g_{2}\in\Gamma
and
f_{i}(n)=g_{i}^{n/p}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1,2
. Then
f_{1},f_{2}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. However,

f_{2}(n+p)^{-1}f_{1}(n+p)^{-1}f_{1}(n)f_{2}(n)=g_{2}^{-1-\frac{n}{p}}g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}^{\frac{n}{p}}=g_{2}^{-1}[g_{2}^{\frac{n}{p}},g_{1}]g_{1}^{-1}=[g_{2},g_{1}]^{\frac{n}{p}}g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}^{-1}.

In general,
[g_{2},g_{1}]^{\frac{n}{p}}
does not always belong to
\Gamma
if we choose
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
properly. This means that
f_{1}f_{2}
does not need to belong to
\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

Although
\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
is not closed under multiplication, we have the following:

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
k,Q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold, and
\Omega
be a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. For all
f\in\text{poly}_{Q}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
and
g\in\text{poly}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, we have that
fg\in\text{poly}_{Q}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

Proof.

By Corollary B.4 of
[
11
]
,
\text{poly}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
is a group. So
fg\in\text{poly}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
. So it suffices to show that for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
n,n+Qm\in\Omega
, we have that

(f(n+Qm)g(n+Qm))^{-1}(f(n)g(n))=g(n+Qm)^{-1}(f(n+Qm)^{-1}f(n))g(n)

belongs to
\Gamma
. Since
f\in\text{poly}_{Q}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, we have that
f(n+Qm)^{-1}f(n)\in\Gamma
. Since
g\in\text{poly}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, we have that
g(n+Qm)^{-1},g(n)\in\Gamma
. We are done.
∎

The following lemma allows one to convert a partially periodic sequence to a rational one.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
k,Q,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k}
be a non-empty
Q
-periodic set,
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold of step at most
s
, and
g\in\text{poly}_{Q}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.
Then for any
n_{\ast}\in\Omega
, the map
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
given by

g^{\prime}(n):=\{g(n_{\ast})\}^{-1}g(n)[g(n_{\ast})]^{-1}

is a
(s!)^{k}Q^{s}
-rational map in
\text{poly}_{Q}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

Proof.

It is easy to check that
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{Q}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. For all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we have that
g^{\prime}(n_{\ast}+Qm)^{-1}g^{\prime}(n_{\ast})\in\Gamma
. Since
g^{\prime}(n_{\ast})=id_{G}
, we have that
g^{\prime}(n_{\ast}+Qm)\in\Gamma
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
Let
\psi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}^{m}
be the Mal’cev coordinate map. By interpolation, this implies that all the coefficients of
\psi(g^{\prime}(n_{\ast}+Q\cdot))
are in
\mathbb{Z}^{k}/(s!)^{k}
, and so all the coefficients of
\psi\circ g^{\prime}
are in
\mathbb{Z}^{k}/(s!)^{k}Q^{s}
, which implies that
g^{\prime}
is
(s!)^{k}Q^{s}
-rational.
∎

In this paper, we also need the following proposition for partially rational polynomial sequences.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
k,Q,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p\gg_{k,Q,s}1
be a prime,
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k}
be a non-empty
p
-periodic set,
n_{\ast}\in\Omega
,
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold of step at most
s
and complexity at most
Q
, and
g\in\text{poly}_{\approx Q,n_{\ast}}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. Let
\psi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the Mal’cev coordinate map. Then there exists
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
Q^{\prime}\ll_{k,s}Q^{O_{k,s}(1)}
such that
g
belongs to
\text{poly}_{Q^{\prime}}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

In order to prove Proposition
3.19
, we need the following result:

Proposition 3.20
.

Let
k,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\delta>0
and
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Q})
be a rational polynomial of degree at most
s
. Suppose that
P
is
K
-periodic for some
K\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and suppose that there exists a subset
W
of
[K]^{k}
of cardinality at least
\delta K^{k}
such that
P(W)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
. Then the coefficients of
P
belong to
\mathbb{Z}/r
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\ll_{k,s}\delta^{-O_{k,s}(1)}
.

Proof.

Let

F\colon\mathbb{T}\to[0,1]
be a Lipschitz function supported on
(-\delta/10,\delta/10)
with
F(0)=1
and with Lipschitz norm at most
100\delta^{-1}
.
Then
\mathbb{E}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}F(P(n)\mod\mathbb{Z})\geq\delta
, and
\int_{\mathbb{T}}F\,dm\leq\delta/2
(where
m
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}
). So

\frac{|\mathbb{E}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}F(P(n)\mod\mathbb{Z})-\int_{\mathbb{T}}F\,dm|}{\|F\|_{\text{Lip}}}\geq\delta^{2}/200

and thus the sequence
(P(n)\mod\mathbb{Z})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
is not
\delta^{2}/200
-equidistributed in
\mathbb{T}
. By Theorem 8.6 of
[
8
]
(see also Theorem
10.2
), there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
with
0<|k|\ll_{k,s}\delta^{-O_{k,s}(1)}
such that
kP(n)\equiv a\mod\mathbb{Z}
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. Since
W
is non-empty, we may take
a=0
. So by interpolation, the coefficients of
P
belong to
\mathbb{Z}/r
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\ll_{k,s}\delta^{-O_{k,s}(1)}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.19
.

Let
\psi=(\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{m})\colon G\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the Mal’cev coordinate map of
\psi
. Then each
\psi_{i}(g(n))
is a rational polynomial of degree at most
s
.
For convenience denote
f_{i}:=\psi_{i}\circ g
.
Since
p\gg_{Q}1
, for any
x\in\Omega
, there exists
v_{x},u_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
n_{\ast}+Qv_{x}+pu_{x}=x
.
Then for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we have that
n_{\ast}+Qv_{x}+pQy=n_{\ast}+Q(v_{x}+py)\in\Omega
and thus

f_{i}(n_{\ast}+Qv_{x}+pQy)\in\mathbb{Z}
. By interpolation, all the coefficients of the polynomial
f_{i}(n_{\ast}+Qv_{x}+pQ\cdot)
belong to
\mathbb{Z}/(s!)^{k}
and thus all the coefficients of the polynomial
f_{i}(n_{\ast}+Qv_{x}+p\cdot)
belong to
\mathbb{Z}/Q^{s}(s!)^{k}
. So we have that

(3.1)

\begin{split}f_{i}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}/Q^{s}(s!)^{k}\text{ for all }x\in\Omega.\end{split}

For all
n^{\prime},n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, by Lemma A.3 of
[
8
]
, for all
1\leq i\leq m
, it is not hard to see that we may write
\psi_{i}(g(n^{\prime}+qn)^{-1}g(n^{\prime}))
in the form

\begin{split}\psi_{i}(g(n^{\prime}+qn)^{-1}g(n^{\prime}))=P_{i}(\mathbb{f}(n^{\prime}))+\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i}(f_{i^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}+qn)-f_{i^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}))P_{i,i^{\prime}}(\mathbb{f}(n^{\prime}+qn),\mathbb{f}(n^{\prime})),\end{split}

where
\mathbb{f}(n):=(f_{1}(n),\dots,f_{i}(n))
and
P_{i},P_{i,i^{\prime}}
are some polynomials of degree
O_{k,s}(1)
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}/q_{1}
for some
q_{1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
q_{1}\ll_{k,s}Q^{O_{k,s}(1)}
.
Taking
n=\mathbb{0}
, we see that
P_{i}(\mathbb{f}(\cdot))\equiv 0
. So

(3.2)

\begin{split}\psi_{i}(g(n^{\prime}+qn)^{-1}g(n^{\prime}))=\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i}(f_{i^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}+qn)-f_{i^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}))P_{i,i^{\prime}}(\mathbb{f}(n^{\prime}+qn),\mathbb{f}(n^{\prime})).\end{split}

Since
g
is partially
Q
-rational with base point
n_{\ast}
on
\Omega
,
we have that
f_{i}(n_{\ast}+Qn)\in\mathbb{Z}
whenever
n_{\ast}+Qn\in\Omega
.
Since
\Omega
is
p
-periodic, we have that
f_{i}(n_{\ast}+pQn)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
since
\Omega
is
p
-periodic. Since
f_{i}
is rational and thus is periodic,
by Proposition
3.20
, the coefficients of the polynomial
f_{i}(n_{\ast}+pQ\cdot)
belong to
\mathbb{Z}/rp^{O_{d,s}(1)}
for some
r\ll_{d,s}Q^{O_{d,s}(1)}
.
So by interpolation, all the coefficients of the polynomial
f_{i}
belong to
\mathbb{Z}/Q^{s}rp^{O_{k,s}(1)}
.

Moreover, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, since all the coefficients of the polynomial
f_{i}
belong to
\mathbb{Z}/Q^{s}rp^{O_{k,s}(1)}
, we have that
f_{i}(x+Q^{\prime}Q^{s}ry)-f_{i}(x)\in Q^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}/p^{O_{k,s}(1)}
. If in addition
x,x+Q^{\prime}Q^{s}ry\in\Omega
, then it follows from (
3.1
) that

f_{i}(x+Q^{\prime}Q^{s}ry)-f_{i}(x)\in(\mathbb{Z}/Q^{s}(s!)^{k})\cap(Q^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}/p^{O_{k,s}(1)})=Q^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}.

Therefore, it follows from (
3.2
) that there exists some
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
Q^{\prime}\ll_{k,s}Q^{O_{k,s}(1)}
such that

\psi_{i}(g(n^{\prime}+Q^{\prime}Q^{s}rn)^{-1}g(n^{\prime}))\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
n^{\prime},n^{\prime}+Q^{\prime}Q^{s}rn\in\Omega
. This means that
g
is partially
Q^{\prime}Q^{s}r
-periodic on
\Omega
. This completes the proof.
∎

Since
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is
p
-periodic, as a consequence of Proposition
3.19
, we recover Lemma A.12 of
[
8
]
:

Corollary 3.21

(Lemma A.12 of
[
8
]
)
.

Let
k,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold of step at most
s
. Then every rational polynomial sequence in
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
is periodic.

3.3.
Polynomial sequences over finite fields

One can also extend the domain of polynomial sequences from
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
.

Definition 3.22

(Polynomial sequences in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
)
.

Let
k,Q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold, and
p
be a prime. Let

\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
denote the set of all functions of the form
f\circ\tau
for some
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.
For a subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, let

\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
denote the set of all functions of the form
f\circ\tau
for some
f\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma).
We define
\text{poly}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
similarly.
We call functions in
\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)

partially
p
-periodic polynomial sequences on
\Omega
.

Example 3.23
.

By Corollary B.4 of
[
11
]
, it is not hard to show that
\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
is a group. However, unlike the conclusion of Corollary B.5 of
[
11
]
,
\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
is not closed under translations and transformations. To see this, let
k=1
and
G/\Gamma=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
be the torus with
G_{1}=G
and
G_{2}=\{id_{G}\}
. Let
f(n)=n/p
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
g:=f\circ\tau\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. However,
g^{\prime}:=g(\cdot+1)
does not belong to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

To see this, suppose on the contrary that
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. Then we may write
g^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\circ\tau
for some
f^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.
By the construction of the filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
, we must have
f^{\prime}(n)=an+b
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then

(3.3)

a\tau(n)+b=f^{\prime}\circ\tau(n)=g^{\prime}(n)=g(n+1)=f\circ\tau(n+1)=\tau(n+1)/p

for all
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Setting
n=0
and 1 in (
3.3
), we deduce that
a=b=1/p
. Setting
n=p-1
in (
3.3
), we have that
1=a(p-1)+b=0
, a contradiction.

As is explained in Example
3.23
, partially
p
-periodic polynomial sequences are not closed under translations and transformations. However, the following proposition asserts that partially
p
-periodic are indeed closed under translations and transformations modulo
\Gamma
.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold,
p
be a prime,
\Omega
be a subset of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, and
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

(1)

For any
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, there exists
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}((\Omega-h)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
such that
g(n+h)\Gamma=g^{\prime}(n)\Gamma
for all
n\in\Omega-h
.

(2)

For any
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and linear transformation
L\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, there exists
g^{\prime\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(L^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
such that
g(L(n))\Gamma=g^{\prime\prime}(n)\Gamma
for all
n\in L^{-1}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Assume that
g=f\circ\tau
for some
f\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{I}|\Gamma)
.
We first prove Part (i).
Let
f^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G
be the function given by
f^{\prime}:=f(\cdot+\tau(h))
.
Since
f
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
, so does
f^{\prime}
by Corollary B.5 of
[
11
]
(or by definition).
Note that
\tau(n+h)\equiv\tau(n)+\tau(h)\mod p\mathbb{Z}
.
For all
n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega-h)=\iota^{-1}(\Omega)-\tau(h)
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, since
n+\tau(h),n+\tau(h)+pm\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
, we have that

f^{\prime}(n+pm)^{-1}f^{\prime}(n)=f(n+pm+\tau(h))^{-1}f(n+\tau(h))\in\Gamma.
So
f^{\prime}
belongs to
\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega-h)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
and
g^{\prime}:=f^{\prime}\circ\tau
belongs to
\text{poly}_{p}((\Omega-h)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.
On the other hand, for all
n+h\in\Omega
, since
\tau(n+h),\tau(n)+\tau(h)\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
, we have that

g(n+h)=f(\tau(n+h))\equiv f(\tau(n)+\tau(h))=f^{\prime}\circ\tau(n)=g^{\prime}(n)\mod\Gamma.

We now prove part (ii).
It is not hard to see that there exists a linear transformation
L^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
L^{\prime}\circ\tau(n)\equiv\tau\circ L(n)\mod p\mathbb{Z}^{k}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}}
.
Let
f^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}}\to G
be the function given by
f^{\prime\prime}=f\circ L^{\prime}
. Since
f
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
, it is not hard to see that
f^{\prime\prime}
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
by Corollary B.5 of
[
11
]
(or by definition).
For all
n\in\tau(L^{-1}(\Omega))
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}}
, we may write
n=\tau(n^{\prime})
for some
n^{\prime}\in L^{-1}(\Omega)
. So

(3.4)

\begin{split}&\quad f^{\prime\prime}(n+pm)^{-1}f^{\prime\prime}(n)=f(L^{\prime}(\tau(n^{\prime}))+L^{\prime}(pm))^{-1}f(L^{\prime}(\tau(n^{\prime})))\\
&=\Bigl(f(L^{\prime}(\tau(n^{\prime}))+L^{\prime}(pm))^{-1}f(\tau(L(n^{\prime}))+L^{\prime}(pm))\Bigr)\\
&\qquad\cdot\Bigl(f(\tau(L(n^{\prime}))+L^{\prime}(pm))^{-1}f(\tau(L(n^{\prime})))\Bigr)\cdot\Bigl(f(\tau(L(n^{\prime})))^{-1}f(L^{\prime}(\tau(n^{\prime})))\Bigr).\end{split}

Note that
L^{\prime}(\tau(n^{\prime}))\equiv\tau(L(n^{\prime}))\mod p\mathbb{Z}^{k}
,
L^{\prime}(pm)=pL^{\prime}(m)\in p\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
\tau(L(n^{\prime}))\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
. It follows from the fact
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{I}|\Gamma)
that (
3.4
) belongs to
\Gamma
.
Since
\iota^{-1}(L^{-1}(\Omega))=\tau(L^{-1}(\Omega))+p\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}}
, we have that

f^{\prime\prime}
belongs to
\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(L^{-1}(\Omega))\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
and
g^{\prime\prime}:=f^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau
belongs to
\text{poly}_{p}(L^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.
On the other hand, for all
n\in L^{-1}(\Omega)
, since
\tau\circ L(n),L^{\prime}\circ\tau(n)\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
, we have that

g\circ L(n)=f\circ\tau\circ L(n)\equiv f\circ L^{\prime}\circ\tau(n)=f^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau(n)=g^{\prime\prime}(n)\mod\Gamma.

∎

3.4.
The Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula

The material of this section comes from Appendix C of
[
6
]
. We write it down for completeness.

Let
G
be a group,
t\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
g_{1},\dots,g_{t}\in G
. The
iterated commutator
of
g_{1}
is defined to be
g_{1}
itself. Iteratively, we define an
iterated commutator
of
g_{1},\dots,g_{t}
to be an element of the form
[w,w^{\prime}]
, where
w
is an iterated commutator of
g_{i_{1}},\dots,g_{i_{r}}
,
w^{\prime}
is an iterated commutator of
g_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\dots,g_{i^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}
for some
1\leq r,r^{\prime}\leq t-1
with
r+r^{\prime}=t
and
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}\cup\{i^{\prime}_{1},\dots,i^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}\}=\{1,\dots,t\}
.

Similarly, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{t}
be elements of a Lie algebra. The
iterated Lie bracket
of
X_{1}
is defined to be
X_{1}
itself. Iteratively, we define an
iterated Lie bracket
of
X_{1},\dots,X_{t}
to be an element of the form
[w,w^{\prime}]
, where
w
is an iterated Lie bracket of
X_{i_{1}},\dots,X_{i_{r}}
,
w^{\prime}
is an iterated Lie bracket of
X_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\dots,X_{i^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}
for some
1\leq r,r^{\prime}\leq t-1
with
r+r^{\prime}=t
and
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}\cup\{i^{\prime}_{1},\dots,i^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}\}=\{1,\dots,t\}
.

Let
G
be a connected and simply connected nilpotent Lie group. The
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula
asserts that for all
X_{1},X_{2}\in\log G
, we have

\exp(X_{1})\exp(X_{2})=\exp\Bigl(X_{1}+X_{2}+\frac{1}{2}[X_{1},X_{2}]+\prod_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}\Bigr),

where
\alpha
is a finite set of labels,
c_{\alpha}
are real constants, and
X_{\alpha}
are iterated Lie brackets with
k_{1,\alpha}
copies of
X_{1}
and
k_{2,\alpha}
copies of
X_{2}
for some
k_{1,\alpha},k_{2,\alpha}\geq 1
and
k_{1,\alpha}+k_{2,\alpha}\geq 3
. One may use this formula to show that for all
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

(g_{1}g_{2})^{x}=g_{1}^{x}g_{2}^{x}\prod_{\alpha}g_{\alpha}^{Q_{\alpha}(x)},

where
\alpha
is a finite set of labels,
g_{\alpha}
are iterated commutators with
k_{1,\alpha}
copies of
g_{1}
and
k_{2,\alpha}
copies of
X_{2}
for some
k_{1,\alpha},k_{2,\alpha}\geq 1
, and
Q_{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are polynomials of degrees at most
k_{1,\alpha}+k_{2,\alpha}
without constant terms.

Similarly, one can show that for any
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

[g_{1}^{x_{1}},g_{2}^{x_{2}}]=[g_{1},g_{2}]^{x_{1}x_{2}}\prod_{\alpha}g_{\alpha}^{P_{\alpha}(x_{1},x_{2})},

where
\alpha
is a finite set of labels,
g_{\alpha}
are iterated commutators with
k_{1,\alpha}
copies of
g_{1}
and
g_{2,\alpha}
copies of
X_{2}
for some
k_{1,\alpha},k_{2,\alpha}\geq 1
,
k_{1,\alpha}+k_{2,\alpha}\geq 3
, and
P_{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
are polynomials of degrees at most
k_{1,\alpha}
in
x_{1}
and at most
k_{2,\alpha}
in
x_{2}
which vanishes when
x_{1}x_{2}=0
.

3.5.
Type-I horizontal torus and character

Horizontal torus and character are important terminologies which characterize the equidistribution properties of nilsequences. In literature, there are at least three different types of horizontal toruses and characters. In this paper as well as the series of work
[
17
,
18
,
19
]
, we refer to them as
type-I
,
type-II
and
type-III
respectively. In this paper, we use the type-I horizontal torus and character defined below:
7
7

7

We will define the type-II horizontal torus and character in
[
18
]
, and the type-III horizontal torus and character in
[
19
]
.

Definition 3.25

(Type-I horizontal torus and character)
.

Let
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold endowed with a Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
. The
type-I horizontal torus
of
G/\Gamma
is
G/[G,G]\Gamma
.
A
type-I horizontal character
is a continuous homomorphism
\eta\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\eta(\Gamma)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
.
When written in the coordinates relative to
\mathcal{X}
, we may write
\eta(g)=k\cdot\psi(g)
for some unique
k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, where
\psi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the coordinate map with respect to the Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
. We call the quantity
\|\eta\|:=|k|=|k_{1}|+\dots+|k_{m}|
the
complexity
of
\eta
(with respect to
\mathcal{X}
).

It is not hard to see that any type-I horizontal character mod
\mathbb{Z}
vanishes on
[G,G]\Gamma
and thus descent to a continuous homomorphism between the type-I horizontal torus
G/[G,G]\Gamma
and
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,
\eta\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a well defined map from
G/\Gamma
to
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.

Type-I horizontal torus and character are used to characterize whether a nisequence is equidistributed on a nilmanifold
[
8
,
15
]
. We provide some properties for later uses.
The proof of the following lemma is similar to that of Lemma 6.7 of
[
8
]
, Lemma B.9 of
[
11
]
and Lemma 2.8 of
[
1
]
. We omit the details.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}

and
G
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered group of degree at most
s
. A function
g\colon\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
if and only if
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
with
|i|\leq s
,
there exists
X_{i}\in\log(G_{|i|})
such that

g(n)=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{k},|i|\leq s}\exp\Bigl(\binom{n}{i}X_{i}\Bigr).

Moreover, if
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
, then the choice of
X_{i}
are unique.

Furthermore, if
G^{\prime}
is a subgroup of
G
and
g
takes values in
G^{\prime}
, then
X_{i}\in\log(G^{\prime})
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.

An equivalent way of saying that a function
g\colon\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
is that

(3.5)

g(n)=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{k},|i|\leq s}g_{i}^{\binom{n}{i}}

for some
g_{i}\in G_{|i|}
(with any fixed order in
\mathbb{N}^{k}
).
We call
g_{i}
the
(
i
-th) type-I Taylor coefficient
of
g
, and (
3.5
) the
type-I Taylor expansion
of
g
.

The following lemma is obvious and we omit the proof:

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime,
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and
\eta
be a type-I horizontal character on some
\mathbb{N}
-filtered degree
\leq s
nilmanifold
G/\Gamma
. If
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, then
\eta\circ g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{Z})
and is of degree at most
s
.

We can say more about the coefficients of
\eta\circ g
for some partially
p
-periodic polynomial sequence
g
on
\Omega
.

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p\gg_{d,s}1
be a prime,
\Omega
be a non-empty
p
-periodic subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and
\eta
be a type-I horizontal character on some
\mathbb{N}
-filtered degree
\leq s
nilmanifold
G/\Gamma
.
Then for all
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
,
p^{s}\eta\circ g(n)
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}+C
for some
C\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Since
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, we have that
\eta\circ g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{Z})
and is of degree at most
s
by Lemma
3.27
.
Since
\Omega
is
p
-periodic, for all
n\in\Omega
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have that

\eta\circ g(n+pm)-\eta\circ g(n)\in\mathbb{Z}.

Since
\Omega
is non-empty, by interpolation, it is not hard to see that
we may write

Q\eta\circ g(m)-C=\frac{1}{p^{s}}f(m)

for some
C\in\mathbb{R}
,
Q\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\nmid Q
, and some integer valued polynomial
f
. It remains to show that
Q|f(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Fix any
n_{0}\in\Omega
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
n_{0}+pm\equiv n\mod s!Q\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Since
f
is integer valued, we have that

f(n)\equiv f(n_{0}+pm)\mod Q\mathbb{Z}
. On the other hand, we have

\eta\circ(n_{0}+pm)-\eta\circ(n_{0})=(f(n_{0}+pm)-f(n_{0}))/Qp^{s}\in\mathbb{Z}.

So
f(n)\equiv f(n_{0}+pm)\equiv f(n_{0})\mod Q\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Replacing
C
by
C+\frac{1}{p^{s}}f(n_{0})
if necessary, we have that
Q|f(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

We end this section with a technical lemma to be used in later sections.

Proposition 3.29
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime, and
\Omega
be a non-empty
p
-periodic subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Let
G/\Gamma
be a degree
\leq s
nilmanifold of complexity at most
C
with a filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
and a Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\eta
be a type-I horizontal character of
G
of complexity at most
C
and
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. Suppose that
\eta\circ g
is the sum of an integer valued polynomial and a polynomial of degree
s^{\prime}
for some
0\leq s^{\prime}\leq s
. If
p\gg_{C,s}1
, then there exists
\gamma\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)

with
\gamma(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
such that
\eta\circ g-\eta\circ\gamma
is a polynomial of degree at most
s^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
G/\Gamma
is of complexity at most
C
, it is not hard to see that there exists a Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
of
G/[G,G]
such that
(G/[G,G])/(\Gamma/(\Gamma\cap[G,G]))
is an abelian nilmanifold (endowed with the quotient filtration) of complexity
O_{C}(1)
with respect to
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, and that
\eta
induces a type-I horizontal character of
G/[G,G]
of complexity
O_{C}(1)
.
Note that
\eta
annihilates
[G,G]
.
Moreover, we have that
g\mod[G,G]
belongs to
\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to(G/[G,G])_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma/(\Gamma\cap[G,G]))
.
So by the discussion above, it suffices to prove this proposition under the assumption that
G
is abelian. So from now on we assume that
G
is abelian.

Denote
m_{i}=\dim(G_{i}),0\leq i\leq s
and
m=\dim(G)
. It is convenient to work with the additive notation and write

g(n)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0\leq|i|\leq s}g_{i}\binom{n}{i}

for some
g_{i}\in\{0\}^{m-m_{|i|}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{|i|}}
. Assume that
\eta(g)=k\cdot g
for all
g\in G
for some
k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
with
|k|\leq C
.
Fix any
i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},s^{\prime}<|i|\leq s
.
Since
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is the sum of an integer valued polynomial and a polynomial of degree
s^{\prime}
, we have that

\eta(g_{i})=k\cdot g_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}.

Since
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, by Lemma
3.28
, we have that
p^{s}g_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. Letting
c_{i}
denote the greatest common divisor of
k_{m-m_{|i|}+1},\dots,k_{m}
, we have that

p^{s}\eta(g_{i})=k\cdot(p^{s}g_{i})\in k\cdot(\{0\}^{m-m_{|i|}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{m_{|i|}})\subseteq c_{i}\mathbb{Z}.

On the other hand,

p^{s}\eta(g_{i})\in p^{s}\mathbb{Z}
. So if
p\gg_{C,s}1
, then

p^{s}\eta(g_{i})\in(c_{i}\mathbb{Z})\cap(p^{s}\mathbb{Z})=p^{s}c_{i}\mathbb{Z},

which implies that
\eta(g_{i})\in c_{i}\mathbb{Z}
. By the definition of
c_{i}
, there exists
\gamma_{i}\in\{0\}^{m-m_{|i|}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{m_{|i|}}
such that
\eta(g_{i})=\eta(\gamma_{i})
.
Denote

\gamma(n):=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},s^{\prime}<|i|\leq s}\gamma_{i}\binom{n}{i},

we have that
\gamma
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
and takes values in
\Gamma
, that
\gamma(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
, and that
\eta\circ g(n)-\eta\circ\gamma(n)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0\leq|i|\leq s^{\prime}}\eta(g_{i})\binom{n}{i}
is a polynomial of degree at most
s^{\prime}
.
∎

3.6.
Vertical torus and character

The vertical torus is an important concept which allows us to do Fourier decompositions on nilmanifolds.

Definition 3.30

(Vertical torus and character)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold with an
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of degree
\leq s
with a Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
adapted to it. Then
G_{s}
lies in the center of
G
. The
vertical torus
of
G/\Gamma
is the set
G_{s}/(\Gamma\cap G_{s})
.
A
vertical character
of
G/\Gamma
(with respect to the filtration
(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
) is a continuous homomorphism
\xi\colon G_{s}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\xi(\Gamma\cap G_{s})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
(in particular,
\xi
descents to a continuous homomorphism from the vertical torus
G_{s}/(\Gamma\cap G_{s})
to
\mathbb{T}
). Let
\psi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the Mal’cev coordinate map and denote
m_{s}=\dim(G_{s})
. Then there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{m_{s}}
such that
\xi(g_{s})=(\mathbb{0},k)\cdot\psi(g_{s})
for all
g_{s}\in G_{s}
(note that the first
\dim(G)-m_{s}
coordinates of
\psi(g_{s})
are all zero). We call the quantity
\|\xi\|:=|k|
the
complexity
of
\xi
(with respect to
\mathcal{X}
).

The following lemma is useful in the study of equidistribution properties on nilmanifolds.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let

\Omega
be a non-empty subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold with an
\mathbb{N}
-filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
of degree
s
. Let
m_{s}=\dim(G_{s})
and
0<\delta<1/2
. If a sequence
\mathcal{O}\colon\Omega\to G/\Gamma
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
, then there exists a Lipschitz function
F\in\text{Lip}(G/\Gamma\to\mathbb{C})
with
a vertical character of complicity at most
O_{m_{s}}(\delta^{-O_{m_{s}}(1)})
such that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{n\in\Omega\cap[N]^{d}}F(\mathcal{O}(n))-\int_{G/\Gamma}F\,dm_{G/\Gamma}\Bigr|\gg_{m_{s}}\delta^{O_{m_{s}}(1)}\|F\|_{\text{Lip}},

where
m_{G/\Gamma}
is the Haar measure of
G/\Gamma
.

The proof of Lemma
3.31
is a straightforward extension of Lemma 3.7 of
[
8
]
, we omit the details.

4.
Background materials for quadratic forms

4.1.
Basic properties for quadratic forms

Definition 4.1

(Quadratic forms on
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
)
.

We say that a function
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a
quadratic form
if

M(n)=(nA)\cdot n+n\cdot u+v

for some
d\times d
symmetric matrix
A
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, some
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
We say that
A
is the matrix
associated to

M
.
8
8

8

Sometimes in literature a quadratic form is defined to be a map
B\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
B(x,y)=(xA)\cdot y
for some symmetric matrix
A
. We define quadratic forms differently for the convenience of this paper.

We say that
M
is
pure
if
u=\mathbb{0}
.
We say that
M
is
homogeneous
if
u=\mathbb{0}
and
v=0
. We say that
M
is
non-degenerate
if
M
is of rank
d
, or equivalently,
\det(A)\neq 0
.

A quadratic form we are particularly interested in is the one induced by the dot product, namely
M(n):=n\cdot n
, which is associated with the identity matrix.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form.
We use
\text{rank}(M):=\text{rank}(A)
to denote the
rank
of
M
.
One can also define quadratic forms whose domain is an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Let
V+c
be an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
r
. There exists a (not necessarily unique) bijective linear transformation
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}\to V
.
We say that a function
M\colon V+c\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a
quadratic form
if there exists a quadratic form
M^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
and a bijective linear transformation
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}\to V
such that
M(n)=M^{\prime}(\phi^{-1}(n-c))
for all
n\in V+c
(or equivalently,
M^{\prime}(m)=M(\phi(m)+c)
for all
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}
). We define the
rank

\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})
of
M
restricted to
V+c
as the rank of
M^{\prime}
. Note that if
M(n)=M^{\prime}(\phi^{-1}(n-c))=M^{\prime\prime}({\phi^{\prime}}^{-1}(n-c))
for some quadratic forms
M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}
associated to the matrices
A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime}
, then
A^{\prime}=BA^{\prime\prime}{B}^{T}
, where
B
is the unique
r\times r
invertible matrix such that
\phi^{-1}\circ\phi(m)=mB
for all
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}
.
So
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})
is independent of the choice of
M^{\prime}
and
\phi^{\prime}
.

The following is a straightforward property for quadratic forms, which we record for later uses.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
, and
x,y,z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Suppose that
M(x)=M(x+y)=M(x+z)=0
. Then
M(x+y+z)=0
if and only if
(yA)\cdot z=0
.

Proof.

The conclusion follows from the equality

M(x+y+z)-M(x+y)-M(x+z)+M(x)=2(yA)\cdot z.

∎

The next lemma says that using translations and linear transformations, any quadratic form can be transformed into a “standard” form:

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
p
be a prime.
For any quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
of rank
d^{\prime}
, there exist
c,c^{\prime},\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
c\neq 0
and a
d\times d
invertible matrix
R
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
such that writing
M^{\prime}(n):=M(nR+v)
, we have that

(4.1)

M^{\prime}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=cn^{2}_{1}+n_{2}^{2}+\dots+n^{2}_{d^{\prime}}+c^{\prime}n_{d^{\prime}+1}-\lambda.

Moreover, if
M
is homogeneous, then we may further require
c^{\prime}=\lambda=0
in (
4.1
); if
M
is pure, then we we may further require
v=\mathbb{0}
; if
M
is non-degenerate, then we may further require that
d^{\prime}=d
.

Remark 4.4
.

Throughout this paper, whenever we write a quadratic form
M^{\prime}
in the form (
4.1
), if
d^{\prime}=d
, then we consider the term
c^{\prime}n_{d^{\prime}+1}
as non-existing.

Proof.

Similar to the real valued quadratic forms, by a suitable substitution
n\mapsto nR
for some upper triangular invertible matrix
R
and some
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, it suffices to consider the case when the matrix
A
associated to
M
is diagonal.
Since
\text{rank}(M)=d^{\prime}
, we may assume without loss of generality that the first
d^{\prime}
entries of the diagonal of
A
is non-zero.
Let
c\in\{2,\dots,p-1\}
be the smallest element such that
c\neq x^{2}
for all
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
(such an element obviously exists).
Under a suitable change of variable
(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\mapsto(c_{1}n_{1},\dots,c_{d^{\prime}}n_{d^{\prime}},n_{d^{\prime}+1},\dots,n_{d})
for some
c_{1},\dots,c_{d^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}
and a change of the orders of
n_{1},\dots,n_{d^{\prime}}
, we may reduce the problem to the case when

M(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=n_{1}^{2}+\dots+n_{k}^{2}+cn_{k+1}^{2}+\dots+cn_{d^{\prime}}^{2}+a_{1}n_{1}+\dots+a_{d}n_{d}-\lambda

for some
0\leq k\leq d^{\prime}
.
By a suitable substitution
n\mapsto n+m
for some
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
,
we may reduce the problem to the case when

M(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=n_{1}^{2}+\dots+n_{k}^{2}+cn_{k+1}^{2}+\dots+cn_{d^{\prime}}^{2}+a_{d^{\prime}+1}n_{d^{\prime}+1}+\dots+a_{d}n_{d}-\lambda.

By another change of variable,
we may reduce the problem to the case when

M(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=n_{1}^{2}+\dots+n_{k}^{2}+cn_{k+1}^{2}+\dots+cn_{d^{\prime}}^{2}+c^{\prime}n_{d^{\prime}+1}-\lambda.

By the minimality of
c
, we have that
a^{2}+1\equiv c\mod p\mathbb{Z}
for some
1\leq a\leq p-1
. If
k\leq d-2
, then

cn_{k+1}^{2}+cn_{k+2}^{2}=(an_{k+1}+n_{k+2})^{2}+(n_{k+1}-an_{k+2})^{2}.

So if
k+2\leq d^{\prime}
, then under another change of variable, we may use another change of variable to replace
cn_{k+1}^{2}+cn_{k+2}^{2}
by
n_{k+1}^{2}+n_{k+2}^{2}
in the expression of
M
. So inductively, we are reduced to the case
k=d^{\prime}-1
or
d^{\prime}
. So (
4.1
) holds by switching the position of
n_{1}
and
n_{d^{\prime}}
.

The “moreover” part follows immediately through the proof.
∎

4.2.
M
-isotropic subspaces

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
and
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Let
V^{\perp_{M}}
denote the set of
\{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon(mA)\cdot n=0\text{ for all }m\in V\}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
and
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

(1)

V^{\perp_{M}}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, and
(V^{\perp_{M}})^{\perp_{M}}=V
.

(2)

\text{rank}(M)=d
if and only if
\det(A)\neq 0
.

(3)

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a bijective linear transformation,
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Let
M^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be the quadratic form given by
M^{\prime}(n):=M(\phi(n)+v)+c
.
We have that
\text{rank}(M)=\text{rank}(M^{\prime})
and
\phi(V^{\perp_{M^{\prime}}})=(\phi(V))^{\perp_{M}}
.

(4)

\text{rank}(M)
equals to
d-\dim((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{\perp_{M}})
.

Proof.

Part (i) is easy to check by definition and the symmetry of
A
.
Part (ii) follows from the fact that
\text{rank}(M)=d\Leftrightarrow\text{rank}(A)=d\Leftrightarrow\det(A)\neq 0
. For Part (iii), assume that
\phi(x)=xB
for some
d\times d
invertible matrix
B
, then
\text{rank}(M)=\text{rank}(A)=\text{rank}(BAB^{T})=\text{rank}(M\circ\phi)
. So
\text{rank}(M)=\text{rank}(M\circ\phi)=\text{rank}(M(\phi(n)+v))
since shifting by
v
does not affect the rank of
a quadratic form. The part
\phi(V^{\perp_{M^{\prime}}})=(\phi(V))^{\perp_{M}}
is easy to check by definition.

We now prove Part (iv).
Let
r=\text{rank}(M)
.
By Lemma
4.3
, we may assume that
M(n)=M^{\prime}(\phi(n)+v)

for some bijective linear transformation
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
,
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
,
c,c^{\prime},\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{p},c\neq 0
, and some quadratic form
M^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
of the form

M^{\prime}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=cn^{2}_{1}+n_{2}^{2}+\dots+n^{2}_{r}+c^{\prime}n_{r+1}-\lambda.

It is clear that
\text{rank}(M^{\prime})=d-\dim((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{\perp_{M^{\prime}}})=r=\text{rank}(M)
. By Part (iii),
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{\perp_{M}}=\phi^{-1}((\phi(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}))^{\perp_{M^{\prime}}})=\phi^{-1}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{\perp_{M^{\prime}}})
. So
\dim((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{\perp_{M}})=\dim((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{\perp_{M^{\prime}}})
and thus
\text{rank}(M)=d-\dim((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{\perp_{M}})
.
∎

For
n\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the dot product induces the Euclidean norm
|n|^{2}:=n\cdot n
. However, in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, the dot product (as well as general quadratic forms) does not induce a norm in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, due to the existence of isotropic subspaces.

Definition 4.6

(
M
-isotropic subspaces)
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
.
A subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
M
-isotropic
if
V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}\neq\{\mathbb{0}\}
.
We say that a tuple
(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})
of vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
M
-isotropic

if the span of
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
is an
M
-isotropic subspace.
We say that a subspace or tuple of vectors is
M
-non-isotropic
if it is not
M
-isotropic.

Let
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
be a basis of a subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
A
be the matrix associated to
M
. It is not hard to see that
V
is
M
-isotropic if and only if the determinant of the matrix
((h_{i}A)\cdot h_{j})_{1\leq i,j\leq k}
is 0.

The next lemma ensures the existence of
M
-non-isotropic subspaces:

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form. Then for all
0\leq k\leq\text{rank}(M)
, there exists a
M
-non-isotropic subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
k
.

Proof.

Since
\{\mathbb{0}\}
is
M
-non-isotropic, it suffices to consider the case
k\geq 1
. Let
d^{\prime}=\text{rank}(M)
.
By Lemma
4.3
, under a change of variable, it suffices to consider the case when the associated matrix
A
of
M
is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal can be written as
(a_{1},\dots,a_{d^{\prime}},0,\dots,0)
for some
a_{1},\dots,a_{d^{\prime}}\neq 0
. Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{d}
denote the standard unit vectors. Clearly, for
1\leq k\leq d^{\prime}
, the matrix
((e_{i}A)\cdot e_{j})_{1\leq i,j\leq k}
is the upper left
k\times k
block of
A
, which is of determinant
a_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot a_{k}\neq 0
. This implies that
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\}
is a
M
-non-isotropic subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
k
.
∎

We summarize some basic properties of
M
-isotropic subspaces for later uses.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form and
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension
r
, and
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

(1)

We have
\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}})\leq\min\{d-\text{rank}(M)+r,d-r\}
.

(2)

The rank of
M|_{V+c}
equals to
d-r-\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}})
(i.e.
\dim(V)-\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}})
).

(3)

The rank of
M|_{V+c}
is at most
d-r
and at least
\text{rank}(M)-2r
.

(4)

M|_{V+c}
is non-degenerate (i.e.
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})=d-r
) if and only if
V
is not an
M
-isotropic subspace.

Proof.

Part (i).
Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{d-r}
be a basis of
V
. Let
B=\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
\dots\\
v_{d-r}\end{bmatrix}
. The dimension of
\dim(V^{\perp_{M}})
equals to
d
minus the rank of the matrix
BA
. Since
\text{rank}(BA)\geq\text{rank}(B)+\text{rank}(A)-d=\text{rank}(M)-r
, we have that
\dim(V^{\perp_{M}})\leq d-\text{rank}(M)+r
.
So Part (i) holds because
\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}})\leq\min\{\dim(V),\dim(V^{\perp_{M}})\}
.

Part (ii). Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-r}\to V
be a bijective linear transformation and denote
M^{\prime}(m):=M(\phi(m)+c)
. Then the rank of
M|_{V+c}
equals to the rank of
M^{\prime}
.
Assume that
\phi(m)=mB
for some
(d-r)\times d
matrix
B
of rank
d-r
. Then the matrix associated to
M^{\prime}
is
BAB^{T}
.
Note that for all
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}
,

\begin{split}&m\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r})^{\perp_{M^{\prime}}}\Leftrightarrow((mB)A)\cdot(nB)=0\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}\\
