&\Leftrightarrow((mB)A)\cdot v=0\text{ for all }v\in V\Leftrightarrow mB\in V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}.\end{split}

By Proposition
4.5
,

\text{rank}(M^{\prime})=d-r-\dim(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r})^{\perp_{M^{\prime}}}=d-r-\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}).

This proofs Part (ii).

Parts (iii) and (iv) follows directly from Parts (i) and (ii).
∎

An annoying issue when working with subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is that the restriction of a non-degenerate quadratic form
M
to an
M
-isotropic subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is not necessarily non-degenerate. Fortunately, with the help of Proposition
4.8
(iii), we have that the nullity of
M
does not increase too much when restricted to
V
, provided that the co-dimension of
V
is small.

The following is a consequence of Proposition
4.8
, which will be used in
[
17
]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form,
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
r
, and
V^{\prime}
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
r^{\prime}
. Suppose that
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})=d-r
. Then
\text{rank}(M|_{(V+V^{\prime})^{\perp_{M}}})\geq d-r-2r^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
V^{\perp_{M}}
is of co-dimension
r
,
by Proposition
4.8
(ii),
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})=d-r
implies that
\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}})=0
.
Since
\dim(V+V^{\prime})\leq r+r^{\prime}
and
M
is non-degenerate, we have that
\dim((V+V^{\prime})^{\perp_{M}})\geq d-r-r^{\prime}
.
By Proposition
4.8
(ii),

\text{rank}(M|_{(V+V^{\prime})^{\perp_{M}}})\geq d-r-r^{\prime}-\dim((V+V^{\prime})\cap(V+V^{\prime})^{\perp_{M}})\geq d-r-r^{\prime}-\dim((V+V^{\prime})\cap V^{\perp_{M}}).

So it remains to show that
\dim((V+V^{\prime})\cap V^{\perp_{M}})\leq r^{\prime}
. To see this, note that for any
v_{1},\dots,v_{r^{\prime}+1}\in(V+V^{\prime})\cap V^{\perp_{M}}
, there exist
c_{1},\dots,c_{r^{\prime}+1}
not all equal to 0 such that
c_{1}v_{1}+\dots+c_{r^{\prime}+1}v_{r^{\prime}+1}\in V
. Since
c_{1}v_{1}+\dots+c_{r^{\prime}+1}v_{r^{\prime}+1}\in V^{\perp_{M}}
and
V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
, we have that
c_{1}v_{1}+\dots+c_{r^{\prime}+1}v_{r^{\prime}+1}=\mathbb{0}
. This means that
\dim((V+V^{\prime})\cap V^{\perp_{M}})\leq r^{\prime}
are we are done.
∎

4.3.
Counting the zeros of quadratic forms

For a polynomial
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
, let
V(P)
denote the set of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
such that
P(n)=0
.
For a family of polynomials
\mathcal{J}=\{P_{1},\dots,P_{k}\}\subseteq\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
, denote
V(\mathcal{J}):=\cap_{i=1}^{k}V(P_{i})
.
The purpose of this section is to provide estimates on the cardinality of
V(\mathcal{J})
for families of polynomials
arising from quadratic forms.

We start with some basic estimates.
The proof of the following lemma is similar to Lemma 2.4 of
[
9
]
. We omit the details.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be of degree at most
r
.
Then
|V(P)|\leq O_{d,r}(p^{d-1})
unless
P\equiv 0
.

As an application of Lemma
4.10
, we have

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
d,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime, and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form.

(1)

The number of tuples
(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
such that
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
are linearly dependent is at most
kp^{(d+1)(k-1)}
.

(2)

The number of
M
-isotropic tuples
(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
is at most
O_{d,k}(p^{kd-1})
.

Proof.

Part (i). If
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
are linearly dependent, then there exists
1\leq i\leq k
such that
h_{i}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq k,j\neq i}c_{j}h_{j}
for some
c_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. For each
j
, there are
p
choices of
c_{j}
and
p^{d}
choices of
h_{j}
. Since there are
k
choices of
i
, in total there are at most
k\cdot(p\cdot p^{d})^{k-1}=kp^{(d+1)(k-1)}

possibilities.

Part (ii). Note that
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
are
M
-isotropic if and only if
there exist
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
not all equal to zero such that
(h_{i}A)\cdot(c_{1}h_{1}+\dots+c_{k}h_{k})=0
for all
1\leq i\leq k
, which is equivalent of saying that
the determinant of the matrix
((h_{i}A)\cdot h_{j})_{1\leq i,j\leq k}
is zero. By viewing
((h_{i}A)\cdot h_{j})_{1\leq i,j\leq k}
as a polynomial in
(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})
(which is certainly not constant zero), we get the conclusion from Lemma
4.10
.
∎

A very useful application of Lemma
4.11
is as follows. Whenever we have a collections
X
of
k
-tuples
(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
of positive density
\delta>0
, if the dimension
d
is sufficiently large compared with
k
, and if
p
is sufficiently large compared with
\delta,d,k
, then by passing to a subset of
X
, we may further require all the tuples in
X
to be linearly independent/
M
-non-isotropic with
X
still having a positive density.

The following lemma was proved in
[
13
]
. We provide its details for completeness.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
p
be a prime number. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form of rank
r
. Suppose that
r\geq 3
.
Then

|V(M)|=p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-\frac{r-2}{2}})),

and for all
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
we have that

(4.2)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{n\in V(M)}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\xi}{p}\cdot n\Bigr)=\mathbb{1}_{\xi=\mathbb{0}}+O(p^{-\frac{r-2}{2}}).\end{split}

Here we slightly abuse the notation in
\frac{\xi}{p}\cdot n
by identifying
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with its embedding
\tau(n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.3
, it is easy to see we only need to prove this lemma for the case when

M(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=a_{1}n^{2}_{1}+\dots+a_{r}n^{2}_{r}+a_{r+1}n_{r+1}+\dots+a_{d}n_{d}-\lambda

for some
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\},a_{r+1},\dots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
9
9

9

Here we do not need to use the full strength of Lemma
4.3
.

Note that

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{n\in V(M)}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\xi}{p}\cdot n\Bigr)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{j\in\mathbb{F}_{p}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\xi}{p}\cdot n+\frac{j}{p}M(n)\Bigr)\\
&=p^{d-1}\mathbb{1}_{\xi=\mathbb{0}}+\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\exp(-j\lambda)\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\xi}{p}\cdot n+\frac{j}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}n^{2}_{i}+\frac{j}{p}\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}a_{i}n_{i}\Bigr).\end{split}

By a change of coordinate, we have that

\begin{split}&\quad\Bigl|\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\exp(-j\lambda)\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\xi}{p}\cdot n+\frac{j}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}n^{2}_{i}+\frac{j}{p}\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}a_{i}n_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr|\\
&\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\Bigl|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\xi}{p}\cdot n+\frac{j}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}n^{2}_{i}+\frac{j}{p}\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}a_{i}n_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr|\\
&=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\Bigl|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{j}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}n^{2}_{i}+\frac{j}{p}\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}a_{i}n_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr|\\
&=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Bigl|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}}\exp(ja_{i}n^{2}/p)\Bigr|\cdot\prod_{i=r+1}^{d}\Bigl|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}}\exp(ja_{i}n/p)\Bigr|\\
&\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}}p^{d-r}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Bigl|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}}\exp(ja_{i}n^{2}/p)\Bigr|\end{split}

By the Gauss sum equality, for all
j\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
,

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{j}{p}\cdot n^{2}\Bigr)=Q\sqrt{p}\eta(j),

where
Q=\pm 1
or
\pm i
is a constant depending on
p
, and
\eta
is the Legendre symbol of
\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}
.
So

(4.3)

\begin{split}\sum_{n\in V(M)}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\xi}{p}\cdot n\Bigr)=p^{d-1}\mathbb{1}_{\xi=\mathbb{0}}+O(p^{d-\frac{r}{2}}).\end{split}

Setting
\xi=\mathbb{0}
, we have that
|V(M)|=p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-\frac{r-2}{2}}))
. Dividing both sides of (
4.3
) by
|V(M)|
, we get (
4.2
).
∎

We remark that Lemma
4.12
fails if
r\leq 2
. For example, if
M(n_{1},n_{2})=n_{1}^{2}+an_{2}^{2}
for some
a\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
such that
x^{2}\neq-a
for all
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, then
V(M)
is an empty set.

The following is an application of Lemma
4.12
.
We say that
t
is a
quadratic root
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
if there exists
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
such that
n^{2}=t
. It is clear that if
p>2
, then the number of quadratic root of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
is
\frac{p+1}{2}
. We have

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
d,r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime number and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form of rank
r
. Then the number of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
M(n)
is a quadratic root is
\frac{1}{2}p^{d}(1+O(p^{-\frac{r-2}{2}}))
if
r\geq 3
, and
\frac{1}{2}p^{d}(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}}))
for
r=2
.

Proof.

Let
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{(p+1)/2}\}
be the set of quadratic roots of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
We first consider the case
r\geq 3
.
By Lemma
4.12
, the number of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
M(n)\in S
is

\sum_{i=1}^{(p+1)/2}|V(M-s_{i})|=(p+1/2)\cdot p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-\frac{r-2}{2}}))=\frac{1}{2}p^{d}(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})).

We now consider the case
r=2
. By Theorem 5A on page 52 of
[
16
]
, the number of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{2}
such that
M(n)=s_{i}
is
p(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
. So the total number of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
M(n)
is a quadratic root is
\frac{1}{2}p(p+1)(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
. We are done.
∎

We provide some generalizations of Lemma
4.12
.
As an immediate corollary of Lemma
4.12
, we have the following estimate on the intersection of
V(M)
with an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},r\in\mathbb{N}
and
p
be a prime number. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form and
V+c
be an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension
r
.

(1)

If
s:=\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})\geq 3
, then

|V(M)\cap(V+c)|=p^{d-r-1}(1+O(p^{-\frac{s-2}{2}})).

(2)

If
\text{rank}(M)-2r\geq 3
, then

|V(M)\cap(V+c)|=p^{d-r-1}(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})).

Proof.

We start with Part (i).
Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-r}\to V
be a bijective linear transformation and denote
M^{\prime}(m):=M(\phi(m)+c)
for all
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-r}
.
Then
\text{rank}(M^{\prime})\geq 3
.
Note that
n\in V(M)\cap(V+c)
if and only if
n=\phi(m)+c
for some
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-r}
such that
M^{\prime}(m)=0
. So by Lemma
4.12
,

|V(M)\cap(V+c)|=|V(M^{\prime})|=p^{d-r-1}(1+O(p^{-\frac{s-2}{2}})).

If
\text{rank}(M)-2r\geq 3
, then
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})\geq 3
by Proposition
4.8
(iii). So Part (ii) follows from Part (i).
∎

The following sets are studied extensively in this paper.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form. Denote

V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{r}}:=\cap_{i=1}^{r}(V(M(\cdot+h_{i}))\cap V(M).

Lemma
4.12
can also be used to study the cardinality of
V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{r}}
.

Corollary 4.16
.

Let
d,r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
p
be a prime number. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form and
h_{1},\dots,h_{r}
be linearly independent vectors.
If
d-2r\geq 3
, then

|V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{r}}|=p^{d-r-1}(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})).

Proof.

Since
h_{1},\dots,h_{r}
are linearly independent and
M
is non-degenerate, it is not hard to see that
V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{r}}
is the intersection of
V(M)
with an affine subspace
V+c
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension
r
.
The conclusion follows from Corollary
4.14
.
∎

We also need to study the following sets in this paper:

Definition 4.17

(Gowers set)
.

Let
\Omega
be a subset of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

\Box_{s}(\Omega)
denote the set of
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{s+1}
such that
n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s}\in\Omega
for all
(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{s})\in\{0,1\}^{s}
.
Here we allow
s
to be 0, in which case
\Box_{0}(\Omega)=\Omega
.
We say that
\Box_{s}(\Omega)
is the
s
-th Gowers set
of
\Omega
.

It is worth notion that the Gowers set will also be used for the study of the spherical Gowers norms in
[
18
]
.
We have the following description for Gowers sets.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
, and
V+c
be an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. For
n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, we have that
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M)\cap(V+c))
if and only if

•

n\in V+c
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\in V
;

•

n\in V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{s}}
;

•

(h_{i}A)\cdot h_{j}=0
for all
1\leq i,j\leq s,i\neq j
.

In particular, let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}\to V
be any bijective linear transformation,
M^{\prime}(m):=M(\phi(m)+c)
. We have that
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M)\cap(V+c))
if and only if

(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})=(\phi(n^{\prime})+c,\phi(h^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,\phi(h^{\prime}_{s}))

for some
(n^{\prime},h^{\prime}_{1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

If
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M)\cap(V+c))
, then clearly
n\in V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{s}}
. Since
n,n+h_{1},\dots,n+h_{s}\in V+c
, we have that
h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\in V
. Finally, the fact that
(h_{i}A)\cdot h_{j}=0
for all
1\leq i,j\leq s,i\neq j
follows from the definition of
\Box_{s}(V(M)\cap(V+c))
and Lemma
4.2
.

Conversely, if
n\in V+c
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\in V
, then
n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s}\in V+c
for all
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{s}\in\{0,1\}
. Since
n\in V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{s}}
, we have that
M(n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s})=0
whenever
|\epsilon|:=\epsilon_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}=0
or 1. Suppose we have shown that
M(n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s})=0
whenever
|\epsilon|:=\epsilon_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}\leq i
for some
1\leq i\leq s-1
, then it follows from Lemma
4.2
and the fact that
(h_{i}A)\cdot h_{j}=0
for all
1\leq i,j\leq s,i\neq j
that
M(n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s})=0
whenever
|\epsilon|:=\epsilon_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}\leq i+1
. In conclusion, we have that
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M)\cap(V+c))
.

The proof of the “in particular” part is left to the interested readers.
∎

We summarize some basic properties for Gowers sets.

Proposition 4.19

(Some preliminary properties for Gowers sets)
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
p
be a prime. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form with
\text{rank}(M)\geq s^{2}+s+3
. Then

(1)

We have
|\Box_{s}(V(M))|=p^{(s+1)d-(\frac{s(s+1)}{2}+1)}(1+O_{s}(p^{-1/2}))
;

(2)

For all but at most
s(s+1)p^{d+s-\text{rank}(M)}
many
h_{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, the set of
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s-1})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{s}
with
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))
is at most
p^{ds-(\frac{s(s+1)}{2}+1)}
.

(3)

If
s=1
, then for any function
f\colon\Box_{1}(V(M))\to\mathbb{C}
with norm bounded by 1, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{(n,h)\in\Box_{1}(V(M))}f(n,h)=\mathbb{E}_{h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{n\in V(M)^{h}}f(n,h)+O(p^{-1/2}).

Proposition
4.19
can either be proved by direct computation plus the counting properties developed in Section
4
, or
be derived by Example
9.5
and Theorem
C.3
, the Fubini’s theorem for more general set. So we omit the proof of Proposition
4.19
.

5.
The main equidistribution theorem

5.1.
Statement of the main theorem and outline of the proof

With the help of the preparation in the previous sections, we are now ready to state the main result in this paper.

The following is the main result of this paper, which is
a generalization of Theorem
1.1
:

Theorem 5.1

(Equidistribution theorem on
V(M)
)
.

Let
0<\delta<1/2,d,k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq r
, and
p
be a prime. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form and
V+c
be an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension
r
. Suppose that
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})\geq s+13
. Let
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of dimension
m
, equipped with an
\frac{1}{\delta}
-rational Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
, and that
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
be a rational polynomial sequence. If
p\gg_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
and
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(V(M)\cap(V+c))}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
,then there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
with
0<\|\eta\|\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
(independent of
k,M
and
p
) such that
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\iota^{-1}(V(M)\cap(V+c))
.
10
10

10

It follows from Corollary
4.14
that
V(M)\cap(V+c)
is non-empty.

In order to illustrate the idea for the proof of Theorem
5.1
, we present an outline of the proof of Corollary
1.3
(which is a special case of Theorem
5.1
). Assume for simplicity that
g
is
p
-periodic. Suppose we have proven Corollary
1.3
for the case
s-1
.
Denote
\Omega=V(M)
.
Assume on the contrary that

(5.1)

\Bigl|\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{n\in\Omega}\exp(g\circ\tau(n)/p)\Bigr|>\delta.

Taking squares on both sides and under a change of variables, we have that

\frac{1}{|\Omega|^{2}}\sum_{n,n+h\in\Omega}\exp((g\circ\tau(n+h)-g\circ\tau(n))/p)>\delta^{2}.

By the Funibi’s Theorem (Proposition
4.19
or Theorem
C.3
), we have that

\frac{1}{p^{d}}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}\Bigl|\frac{1}{|\Omega\cap(\Omega-h)|}\sum_{n\in\Omega\cap(\Omega-h)}\exp((g\circ\tau(n+h)-g\circ\tau(n))/p)\Bigr|>\delta^{2},

where the cardinality of the sets
\Omega\cap(\Omega-h),h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
are comparable with each other. By the Pigeonhole Principle, for “many”
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, we have that

\Bigl|\frac{1}{|\Omega\cap(\Omega-h)|}\sum_{n\in\Omega\cap(\Omega-h)}\exp((g\circ\tau(n+h)-g\circ\tau(n))/p)\Bigr|\gg_{d}1.

Informally, one can view
\Omega\cap(\Omega-h)
as a sphere whose dimension is lower than
\Omega
by 1. So we may then apply the induction hypothesis to the degree
(s-1)
polynomial sequence
g\circ\tau(n+h)-g\circ\tau(n)
to conclude that

(5.2)

(g\circ\tau(n+h)-g\circ\tau(n))/p\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega\cap(\Omega-h)
for “many”
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

For convenience denote
Q:=\iota\circ pg\circ\tau
, then
Q\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
is a polynomial of degree at most
s
. It then follows from (
5.2
) that

(5.3)

\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}Q(n)=0
for “many”
(n,h_{1},h_{2})\in\Box_{2}(\Omega)
,

where
\Box_{2}(\Omega)
is the second Gowers set of
\Omega
defined in
Definition
4.17
.

We have now reduced Corollary
1.3
to a problem of solving the polynomial equation (
5.3
).
If
\Omega
were the entire space
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, then using some basic knowledge for polynomials, it is not hard to show that (
5.3
) would imply that
Q
is of degree at most
s-1
, which would complete the proof of Corollary
1.3
by induction.
When
\Omega
is a sphere, the difficulty of the analysis of (
5.3
) increases significantly. Therefore, solving equation (
5.3
) (as well as its various generalizations) is a central topic in this paper.

5.2.
An overview for Sections
6
,
7
and
8

It turns out that equation (
5.3
) is an over simplification for our purpose. In order to prove Theorem
5.1
, following the idea of Green and Tao
[
8
,
9
]
, one can reduce the problem to solving the following generalized versions of (
5.3
) (see also (
8.18
)):

(5.4)

\Delta_{h_{3}}\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}P(n)+\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}Q(n)=0,\text{ for ``many" }(n,h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\in\Box_{3}(\Omega),

where
Q\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
comes from the composition of
g
with some type-I horizontal character of
G/\Gamma
(so
\deg(Q)\leq s
), and
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
is a polynomial of degree at most
s-1
arising from the “non-linear” part of of
g
.

The bulk of Section
6
is devoted to solving the following generalized version of (
5.4
) (see also equation (
6.29
)):

(5.5)

\Delta_{h_{s-1}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}P(n)+\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}Q(n)=0,\text{ for ``many" }(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(\Omega).

We provide a solution to (
5.5
) in Proposition
6.8
.
In the same section, we also prove some other properties for polynomial equations in
\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
for later uses.

One can then combine the solution to equation (
5.4
) together with the machinery of Green and Tao
[
8
,
9
]
to prove Theorem
5.1
for the case when
g
is
p
-periodic.
However, in order to deal with a partially
p
-periodic sequence
g
, we need to deal with the following even more general version of equation (
5.5
):

(5.6)

\Delta_{h_{s-1}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}P(n)+\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}Q(n)\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ for ``many" }(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\iota^{-1}(\Box_{s}(\Omega)),

where
P,Q\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Q})
are partially
p
-periodic polynomials on
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
. The bulk of
Section
7
is devoted to solving equation (
5.6
) (and we eventually solve equation (
5.6
) in Proposition
7.9
).
In order to solve (
5.6
),
we develop an approach called the
p
-expansion trick
, which allows us to lift the solutions to (
5.6
) from
\mathbb{Z}/p
-valued polynomials to
\mathbb{Z}/p^{s}
-valued polynomials. In the same section, we also use the same trick to extend other results in Section
6
to the partially
p
-periodic case.

Finally, in Section
8
, we combine the algebraic preparations from Sections
6
and
7
with the approach of Green and Tao
[
8
,
9
]
to complete the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Remark 5.2
.

If we are satisfied with equidistribution results for
p
-periodic polynomials, then, as is mentioned above, the entire Section
7
will be unnecessary, and the proofs in Appendix
E
can also be simplified significantly.
However, such results are less useful in
[
18
,
19
]
since we do not have the factorization theorem in the
p
-periodic setting. This is the reason why we must prove the equidistribution results for partially rational polynomials.

6.
Algebraic properties for quadratic forms in
\mathbb{F}_{p}

6.1.
Irreducible properties for quadratic forms

We begin with a dichotomy on the intersection of the sets of solutions of a polynomial and a quadratic form.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
and
p
be a prime such that
p\gg_{d,s}1
. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form of rank at least 3.
For any
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s
, either
|V(P)\cap V(M)|\leq O_{d,s}(p^{d-2})
or
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
.

Proof.

Denote
r=\text{rank}(M)
.
We first assume that
M
and
P
are homogeneous.
By Lemma
4.3
, it suffices to consider the special case when

M(n)=c_{1}n^{2}_{1}+\dots+c_{r}n_{r}^{2}

for some
c_{1},\dots,c_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}
.
Let
U:=V(P)\cap V(M)
.
By the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists
1\leq i\leq r
such that the
i
-th coordinate of at least
(|U|-p^{d-r})/r
many
n\in U
is nonzero. We may assume without loss of generality that
i=1
.
Write
n=(t,y)
, where
y\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}
and
t\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Since
\deg(f)=s\ll p
, by the long division algorithm, we may write

(6.1)

P(t,y)=M(t,y)R(t,y)+N_{1}(y)t+N_{0}(y)

for some
R\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}),N_{1},N_{0}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
such that the degrees of
R,N_{1}
and
N_{0}
are at most
s-2
,
s-1
and
s
respectively. Denote

\Delta(y):=M(0,y)N_{1}(y)^{2}+c_{1}N^{2}_{0}(y).

We first assume that
\Delta(y)
is not constant 0.
Let
U^{\prime}
be the set of
(t,y)\in U
such that
N_{1}(y)=0
. Then (
6.1
) implies that
N_{0}(y)=0
and so
\Delta(y)=0
. Since we assumed that
\Delta(y)
is not constant 0 and
\deg(\Delta(y))\leq 2s
, by Lemma
4.10
, we have that
|U^{\prime}|\leq O_{d,s}(p^{d-2})
.

Suppose that
(t,y)\in U\backslash U^{\prime}
and
t\neq 0
. Then
M(t,y)=N_{1}(y)t+N_{0}(y)=0
. So
t=-N_{0}(y)N_{1}(y)^{-1}
, and so
M(t,y)=0
implies that
M(0,y)=-c_{1}N^{2}_{0}(y)N_{1}(y)^{-2}
, or equivalently
\Delta(y)=0
. Since
\Delta(y)
is not constant 0, by Lemma
4.10
, we have that
|(U\backslash U^{\prime})\cap\{(t,y)\colon t\neq\mathbb{0}\}|\leq O_{d,s}(p^{d-2})
.
Since we assumed that
|U\cap\{(t,y)\colon t\neq\mathbb{0}\}|\geq\frac{|U|-p^{d-r}}{r}
,
we deduce that

|U|\leq O_{d,s}(p^{d-2})
since
r\geq 3
.

We now consider the case that
\Delta(y)\equiv 0
. Let
W
be the set of
y\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}
such that
-c_{1}M(0,y)
is not a square root in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Since
\text{rank}(M(0,\cdot))\geq 2
, by Lemma
4.13
,
|W|=\frac{1}{2}p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
. Since
\Delta(y)\equiv 0
, for all
y\in W
, we have that
N_{1}(y)=0
. By Lemma
4.10
,
|W|\leq O_{s}(p^{d-2})
unless
N_{1}(y)\equiv 0
. So if
p\gg_{d,s}1
, then
N_{1}(y)\equiv 0
. Since
\Delta(y)\equiv 0
, we also have that
N_{2}(y)\equiv 0
. So (
6.1
) implies that
P=MR
, which implies that
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
.

Finally, we consider the general case when
M
and
P
are not necessarily homogeneous.
Then there exist homogeneous polynomials
\tilde{M},\tilde{P}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
of degrees at most 2 and
s
respectively such that
M=\tilde{M}(\cdot,1)
and
P=\tilde{P}(\cdot,1)
. Then
\tilde{M}
is a quadratic form of rank at least 3. By the previous case, either
|V(\tilde{P})\cap V(\tilde{M})|\leq O_{d,s}(p^{d-1})
or
V(\tilde{M})\subseteq V(\tilde{P})
. In the former case, since

(6.2)

\begin{split}&\quad|V(\tilde{P})\cap V(\tilde{M})|\geq\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}|V(\tilde{P}(\cdot,i))\cap V(\tilde{M}(\cdot,i))|\\
&=(p-1)|V(\tilde{P}(\cdot,1))\cap V(\tilde{M}(\cdot,1))|=(p-1)|V(P)\cap V(M)|,\end{split}

we have that
|V(P)\cap V(M)|\leq O_{d,s}(p^{d-2})
. In the later case, we have that
V(\tilde{M}(\cdot,1))\subseteq V(\tilde{P}(\cdot,1))
, which implies that
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
.
∎

Let
P\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a polynomial with
\deg(P)<p
and
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
. A natural question to ask is whether
P
is divisible by
M
, namely
P=MQ
for some polynomial
Q
with
\deg(Q)=\deg(P)-2
. To answer this question, we start with some definitions.
Let
P\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a polynomial of degree
s
for some
s<p
and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the symmetric matrix
A=(a_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
. If
a_{1,1}\neq 0
, then viewing
a_{1,1}n_{1}^{2}
as the leading coefficient of
M
, we may use the long division algorithm to write

P(n)=M(n)Q(n)+n_{1}R_{1}(n^{\prime})+R_{0}(n^{\prime})

for some polynomials
Q
,
R_{1}
and
R_{0}
of degrees at most
s-2
,
s-1
and
s
respectively, where we write
n:=(n_{1},n^{\prime})
for some
n_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}
. We call this algorithm the
standard
long division algorithm.
It is clear that all the coefficients of
Q,R_{1}
and
R_{0}
can be written in the form
F/a_{1,1}^{s}
with
F
being a polynomial function with respect to the coefficients of
P
and
M
of degree at most
s
.

We now consider the case when
a_{1,1}
is not necessarily non-zero. In this case, we can make
a_{1,1}
to be non-zero using a change of variable. To be more precise,
for all
1\leq i,j\leq d
,
let

\phi_{i,i}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=(n_{i},n_{1},\dots,n_{i-1},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{d})

and

\phi_{i,j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=((n_{i}+n_{j})/2,(n_{i}-n_{j})/2,n_{1},\dots,n_{i-1},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{j-1},n_{j+1},\dots,n_{d})

if
i<j
.
Let
B_{i,j}
be the symmetric
d\times d
matrix induced by the bijective linear transformation
\phi_{i,j}
, namely
\phi_{i,j}(n)=nB_{i,j}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any symmetric nonzero
d\times d
matrix
A
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, there exist
1\leq i,j\leq d
such that the upper left entry of the matrix
B_{i,j}AB_{i,j}^{T}
is nonzero.

Proof.

Assume that
A=(a_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
. If
a_{i,i}\neq 0
for some
1\leq i\leq d
, then the upper left entry of the matrix
B_{i,i}AB_{i,i}^{T}
is
a_{i,i}\neq 0
. If
a_{1,1}=\dots=a_{d,d}=0
and
a_{i,j}\neq 0
for some
1\leq i<j\leq d
, then it is not hard to compute that the upper left entry of the matrix
B_{i,j}AB_{i,j}^{T}
is
a_{i,j}\neq 0
.
∎

Let
B_{i,j}
be defined as above and suppose that upper left entry of the matrix
B_{i,j}AB_{i,j}^{T}
is nonzero, where
A
is the matrix associated to
M
.
Then we may use the standard long division algorithm to write

P(nB_{i,j})=M(nB_{i,j})Q(n)+n_{1}R_{1}(n^{\prime})+R_{0}(n^{\prime}).

So

(6.3)

P(n)=M(n)Q(nB_{i,j}^{-1})+(nB_{i,j}^{-1})_{1}R_{1}((nB_{i,j}^{-1})^{\prime})+R_{0}((nB_{i,j}^{-1})^{\prime}),

where we write
nB_{i,j}^{-1}:=((nB_{i,j}^{-1})_{1},(nB_{i,j}^{-1})^{\prime})
. We call this decomposition (
6.3
) of
P
the
B_{i,j}
-standard
long division algorithm.

It is clear that all the coefficients of
Q,R_{1}
and
R_{0}
can be written in the form
F/c_{1,1}^{s}
with
F
being a polynomial function (dependent on
B_{i,j}
) with respect to the coefficients of
P
and
M
of degree at most
s
, where
c_{1,1}
is the coefficient of the
n_{1}^{2}
term of
M(nB_{i,j})
.

We say that
M

divides
P
with respect to the
B_{i,j}
-standard long division algorithm
if
R_{1}=R_{0}=0
in the
B_{i,j}
-standard long division algorithm (
6.3
). We have the following Hilbert Nullstellensatz type result for
V(M)
:

Proposition 6.3

(Hilbert Nullstellensatz for
V(M)
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\gg_{d,s}1
be a prime number,

P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be of degree at most
s
, and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form of rank at least 3 associated with the matrix
A
. If
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
, then for all
1\leq i,j\leq d
such that the upper left entry of the matrix
B_{i,j}AB_{i,j}^{T}
is nonzero,

M
divides
P
with respect the
B_{i,j}
-standard long division algorithm.
In particular,
P=MR
for some
R\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s-2
.

It is tempting to prove Proposition
6.3
by using Hilbert Nullstellensatz.
Let
F_{i}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})

:=n_{i}^{p}-n_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq d
.
By using Hilbert Nullstellensatz in the finite field (see
[
3
]
), if
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
, then one can show that

P=MR+\sum_{i=1}^{d}F_{i}R_{i}

for some polynomials
R,R_{1},\dots,R_{d}
. Unfortunately, this is not helpful for Proposition
6.3
, since there is no restriction for the degree of
R
, and it is unclear how to remove the polynomials
R_{1},\dots,R_{d}
. So we have to prove Proposition
6.3
with a different method relying on the long division algorithm.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Denote
r=\text{rank}(M)
.
Under a change of variable, it suffices to show that for any
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s
and any quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
of rank at least 3 associated with the matrix
A
whose upper left entry is nonzero. If
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
,

M
divides
P
with respect the standard long division algorithm.

We first assume that
M
and
P
are homogeneous.
Write
n=(t,y)
, where
t\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
y\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}
.
Since the coefficient of the
t^{2}
term of
M(t,y)
is nonzero,
we may apply the standard long division algorithm to write

(6.4)

P(t,y)=M(t,y)R(t,y)+N_{1}(y)t+N_{0}(y)

for some
R,N_{1},N_{0}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degrees at most
s-2,s-1
and
s
respectively. Our goal is to show that
N_{1},N_{0}\equiv 0
.
By Lemma
4.3
and a change of variable,
it suffices to show that if (
6.4
) holds for

M(n)=c_{1}n^{2}_{1}+\dots+c_{r}n_{r}^{2}

for some
c_{1},\dots,c_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}
, and that
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
, then
N_{0}=N_{1}\equiv 0
.

Let

\Delta(y):=M(0,y)N_{1}(y)^{2}+c_{1}N^{2}_{0}(y).

If
\Delta(y)
is not constant zero, then by the proof of Lemma
6.1
, the number of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
M(n)=P(n)=0
is at most
O_{d,s}(p^{d-2})
. However, since
V(M)\subseteq V(P)
, by Lemma
4.12
, the number of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
M(n)=P(n)=0
is
p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-\frac{r-2}{2}}))
, a contradiction.

So we must have that
\Delta(y)\equiv 0
. Following the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we have that
N_{1}(y)=N_{2}(y)\equiv 0
, and so
f=MR
. Since the degree of
R
is at most
s-2
, we are done.

We now consider the general case when
M
and
P
are not necessarily homogeneous.
Then there exist homogeneous polynomials
\tilde{M},\tilde{P}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
of degrees at most 2 and
s
respectively such that
M=\tilde{M}(\cdot,1)
and
P=\tilde{P}(\cdot,1)
. Since
|V(P)\cap V(M)|=|V(M)|=p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
by Lemma
4.12
,
it follows from (
6.2
) that
|V(\tilde{P})\cap V(\tilde{M})|=p^{d}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
. By Lemma
6.1
, we have that
V(\tilde{M})\subseteq V(\tilde{P})
. So by the previous case, we have that
\tilde{M}
divides
\tilde{P}
with respect the standard long division algorithm. Since the
n_{1}^{2}
term of
M
is non-zero, it is not hard to see that
M
also divides
P
with respect the standard long division algorithm.
∎

As an immediate corollary of Lemma
6.1
and Proposition
6.3
, we have (see Corollary
6.11
for a similar result):

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
d,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\gg_{d,k,s}1
be a prime number,

P\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form,
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, and
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
k
with a basis
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
. Suppose that
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
.
Then either
|V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)\cap V(P)|\leq O_{d,k,s}(p^{d-k-2})
or
V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)\subseteq V(P)
.

Moreover, if
V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)\subseteq V(P)
, then

(6.5)

P(n)=M(n)P_{0}(n)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}((h_{i}A)\cdot(n-c))P_{i}(n)

for some polynomials
P_{0},\dots,P_{k}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P_{0})\leq s-2
and
\deg(P_{i})\leq s-1,1\leq i\leq k
.

In particular, the conclusion of this corollary holds if
d\geq k+\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}})+3
, or if
d\geq 2k+3
.
12
12

12

Some properties in this paper are not used in the proof of the main results, but they will be used later in the series of work
[
17
,
18
,
19
]
. Corollary
6.4
is such an example.

Proof.

By a change of variables
n\mapsto n+m
for some
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, it suffices to consider the special case when
M(n)=(nA)\cdot n-\lambda
for some invertible symmetric matrix
A
and some
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Since
A
is invertible,
V^{\perp_{M}}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
d-k
.
Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-k}\to V^{\perp_{M}}
be any bijective linear transformation. Denote
M^{\prime}(m):=M(\phi(m)+c)
and
P^{\prime}(m):=P(\phi(m)+c)
for all
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-k}
. It is not hard to see that
V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)=\phi(V(M^{\prime}))+c
. Moreover,

V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)\cap V(P)=\phi(V(M^{\prime})\cap V(P^{\prime}))+c.

Since
\deg(M^{\prime})=\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
, by Lemma
6.1
, either
|V(M^{\prime})\cap V(P^{\prime})|\leq O_{d,k,s}(p^{d-k-2})
or
V(M^{\prime})\subseteq V(P^{\prime})
. In the former case we have that

|V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)\cap V(P)|=|\phi(V(M^{\prime})\cap V(P))|=|V(M^{\prime})\cap V(P^{\prime})|\leq O_{d,k,s}(p^{d-k-2}).

In the later case, we have that

V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)=\phi(V(M^{\prime}))+c\subseteq\phi(V(P^{\prime}))+c\subseteq V(P).

We now assume that
V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)\subseteq V(P)
and show that (
6.5
) holds. By a change of variables
n\to nB
for some invertible matrix
B
, we may assume without loss of generality that
h_{i}A=e_{d-k+i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. Then
V^{\perp_{M}}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d-k}\}
. We may then assume without loss of generality that
c=(0,\dots,0,c_{d-k+1},\dots,c_{d})
.
Then
x\in V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)
if and only if
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-k},c_{d-k+1},\dots,c_{d})\in V(M)
for some
x_{1},\dots,x_{d-k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Since
V(M)\cap(V^{\perp_{M}}+c)\subseteq V(P)
and
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
, by Lemma
6.3
,
p\gg_{d,k,s}1
, then

(6.6)

\begin{split}P(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-k},c_{d-k+1},\dots,c_{d})=M(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-k},c_{d-k+1},\dots,c_{d})Q(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-k})\end{split}

for some polynomial
Q\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(Q)\leq s-2
for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{d-k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Note that for all
1\leq i\leq k
and
x_{d-k+i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, writing
x:=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, we have that

(h_{i}A)\cdot(x-c)=x_{d-k+i}-c_{d-k+i}.

We may then use this fact to replace the terms
c_{d-k+1},\dots,c_{d}
in (
6.6
) by
x_{d-k+1},\dots,x_{d}
and get to a expression of the form (
6.5
).

Note that
V^{\perp_{M}}
is of co-dimension
k
. So if
d\geq k+\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}})+3
, or if
d\geq 2k+3
, then
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
by Proposition
4.8
(ii) and (iii).
∎

6.2.
A special anti-derivative property

Let
F,G\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be polynomials with
F=MG
for some quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Let
\partial_{i}
denote the
i
-th directional formal derivative. Then it is not hard to see that

\partial_{j}M\partial_{i}F-\partial_{i}M\partial_{j}F=M(\partial_{j}M\partial_{i}G-\partial_{i}M\partial_{j}G)

is divisible by
M
. In this section, we study the converse of this question: if
\partial_{j}M\partial_{i}F-\partial_{i}M\partial_{j}F
is divisible by
M
for all
1\leq i,j\leq d
, then is it true that
F
is also divisible by
M
? The following is an answer to this question.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq 3
,
p
be a prime, and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form of rank at least 3.
Let
Q\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with
Q(\mathbb{0})=0
such that

\partial_{1}M(n)\partial_{i}Q(n)=\partial_{i}M(n)\partial_{1}Q(n)

for all
1\leq i\leq d
and
n\in V(M)
. If
p\gg_{d,s}1
, then

Q=MQ^{\prime}
for some
Q^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s-2
.

Proof.

For convenience write
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
By Lemma
4.3
and a change of variable, we may assume without loss of generality that

M(n)=cn_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+\dots+n_{d^{\prime}}^{2}+c^{\prime}n_{d^{\prime}+1}-\lambda

for some
3\leq d^{\prime}\leq d
, and
\lambda,c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
with
c\neq 0
.
Let
A
be the (diagonal) matrix associated to
M
with
a_{i}
be the
(i,i)
-th entry of
A
. Then
a_{1}=c\neq 0
,
a_{2}=\dots=a_{d^{\prime}}=1
, and
a_{d^{\prime}+1}=\dots=a_{d}=0
.
By Proposition
6.3
,
there exist
N_{i}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
,
\deg(N_{i})\leq s-2
for
1\leq i\leq d
such that

(6.7)

\partial_{1}M\partial_{i}Q-\partial_{i}M\partial_{1}Q=2MN_{i}.

We briefly explain the idea of the first part of the proof.
Observe that if
N_{i}\equiv 0
for all
1\leq i\leq d
, then (
6.7
) indicates that
\partial_{i}Q/\partial_{i}M
should be independent of
i
. So it is reasonable to expect
\partial_{i}Q=(\partial_{i}M)F
for some polynomial
F
for all
1\leq i\leq d
. Taking into consideration of the term
MN_{i}
, one can expect that
\partial_{i}Q-(\partial_{i}M)F
is divisible by
M
for all
1\leq i\leq d
. To make this intuition precise, we have

Claim 1.
There exist
F,R_{1},\dots,R_{d}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(F)\leq s-2,\deg(R_{i})\leq s-3
such that

(6.8)

\partial_{i}Q=(\partial_{i}M)F+MR_{i}

for all
1\leq i\leq d
.

For
2\leq i\leq d^{\prime}
, we may write
N_{i}(n)
as

N_{i}(n)=-n_{i}N_{i,1}(n)+cn_{1}N_{i,2}(n)+n_{i}n_{1}N_{i,3}(n)

for some
N_{i,1},N_{i,2},N_{i,3}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
in a unique way, where
N_{i,1}(n)
is independent of
n_{1}
and
N_{i,2}(n)
is independent of
n_{i}
. Moreover,
\deg(N_{i,1}),\deg(N_{i,2})\leq s-3
and
\deg(N_{i,3})\leq s-4
. So (
6.7
) implies that

(6.9)

cn_{1}(\partial_{i}Q(n)-M(n)N_{i,2}(n))-n_{i}(a_{i}\partial_{1}Q(n)-M(n)N_{i,1}(n))=n_{i}n_{1}M(n)N_{i,3}(n)

for all
1\leq i\leq d^{\prime}
.

From (
6.9
) we see that there exists a polynomial
F_{i}
of degree at most
s-2
such that

(6.10)

n_{1}F_{i}(n):=(2c)^{-1}(a_{i}\partial_{1}Q(n)-M(n)N_{i,1}(n)),

and that

(6.11)

\partial_{i}Q(n)=2n_{i}F_{i}(n)+M(n)(N_{i,2}(n)+c^{-1}n_{i}N_{i,3}(n))

if we substitute (
6.10
) back into (
6.9
).
We may write
\partial_{1}Q(n)=Q^{\prime}(n)+n_{1}Q^{\prime\prime}(n)
for some
Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(Q^{\prime})\leq s-1
and
\deg(Q^{\prime\prime})\leq s-2
such that
Q^{\prime}(n)
is independent from
n_{1}
. Since
N_{i,1}(n)
is independent from
n_{1}
, comparing the terms in (
6.10
) which are divisible by
n_{1}
, we have that

(6.12)

a_{i}Q^{\prime}(n)=(M(n)-cn^{2}_{1})N_{i,1}(n)

and

(6.13)

F_{i}(n)=(2c)^{-1}(a_{i}Q^{\prime\prime}(n)-cn_{1}N_{i,1}(n)).

Since
a_{2}=\dots=a_{d^{\prime}}=1
, it follows from (
6.12
) that
all of
N_{2,1},\dots,N_{d^{\prime},1}
equal to a same
N_{1}
. So by (
6.13
), all of
F_{2},\dots,F_{d^{\prime}}
equals to a same
F
. So
(
6.11
) implies that

\partial_{i}Q(n)=2n_{i}F(n)+M(n)(N_{i,2}(n)+c^{-1}n_{i}N_{i,3}(n))=\partial_{i}M(n)F(n)+M(n)(N_{i,2}(n)+c^{-1}n_{i}N_{i,3}(n))

for all
2\leq i\leq d^{\prime}
. In other words, (
6.8
) holds for all
2\leq i\leq d^{\prime}
.

On the other hand, (
6.10
) implies that

(6.14)

\partial_{1}Q(n)=2cn_{1}F(n)+M(n)N_{2,1}(n)=\partial_{1}M(n)F(n)+M(n)N_{2,1}(n).

So (
6.8
) holds for
i=1
.

Now for
d^{\prime}+2\leq i\leq d
, since
a_{i}=0
,
(
6.7
) implies that
cn_{1}\partial_{i}Q(n)=M(n)N_{i}(n)
.
It is then not hard to see that
N_{i}(n)
can be written as
cn_{1}R_{i}(n)
for some polynomial
R_{i}
of degree at most
s-3
. So

\partial_{i}Q(n)=M(n)R_{i}(n)=\partial_{i}M(n)F(n)+M(n)R_{i}(n).

So (
6.8
) holds for all
d^{\prime}+2\leq i\leq d
.

Finally,
by (
6.7
) and (
6.14
), we have that

2cn_{1}(\partial_{d^{\prime}+1}Q(n)-c^{\prime}F(n))=M(n)(2N_{d^{\prime}+1}(n)+c^{\prime}N_{2,1}(n)).

It is then not hard to see that
2N_{d^{\prime}+1}(n)+c^{\prime}N_{2,1}(n)
can be written as
2cn_{1}R_{d^{\prime}+1}(n)
for some polynomial
R_{d^{\prime}+1}
of degree at most
s-3
. So

\partial_{d^{\prime}+1}Q(n)=c^{\prime}F(n)+M(n)R_{d^{\prime}+1}(n)=\partial_{d^{\prime}+1}M(n)F(n)+M(n)R_{d^{\prime}+1}(n).

So (
6.8
) holds for
i=d^{\prime}+1
.
This finishes the proof of Claim 1.

We now explain the idea of the second part of the proof.
Our strategy is to show that one can inductively expand
Q
as

Q=MW_{1}+M^{2}W_{2}+\dots
for some polynomials
W_{1},W_{2},\dots
in the sense that
Q-(MW_{1}+\dots+M^{\ell}W_{\ell})
is a polynomial of degree at most
\deg(Q)
which is divisible by
M^{\ell+1}
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. Since this procedure must terminate after finitely many steps, we conclude that
Q=MW_{1}+\dots+M^{\ell}W_{\ell}
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and so
Q
is divisible by
M
.

To be more precise, we have

Claim 2.
For all
1\leq\ell<p
, there exist
H_{1},\dots,H_{d},W_{\ell}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(W_{\ell})\leq\deg(Q)-2
,
\deg(H_{i})\leq\deg(Q)-2\ell-1
such that for all
1\leq i\leq d
,

\partial_{i}(Q-MW_{\ell})=M^{\ell}H_{i}.

Since Claim 1 holds, by (
6.8
), we have that

(6.15)

\partial_{i}(Q-MF)=M(R_{i}-\partial_{i}F).

Since
\deg(F)\leq\deg(Q)-2
,
Claim 2 holds for
\ell=1
. Now suppose that Claim 2 holds for
\ell-1
for some
2\leq\ell<p
. By induction hypothesis, there exist
H_{1},\dots,H_{d},W_{\ell-1},W^{\prime}_{\ell-1}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(W_{\ell-1})\leq\deg(Q)-2
,
\deg(H_{i})\leq\deg(Q)-2\ell+1
for all
1\leq i\leq d
such that writing
Q^{\prime}:=Q-MW_{\ell-1}
, we have that

\partial_{i}Q^{\prime}=M^{\ell-1}H_{i}

for all
1\leq i\leq d
.
Since
\deg(\partial_{i}Q^{\prime})\leq p-1
, for all
1\leq j\leq d
,

(6.16)

\partial_{j}\partial_{i}Q^{\prime}=\partial_{j}(M^{\ell-1}H_{i})=M^{\ell-2}((\ell-1)(\partial_{j}M)H_{i}+M\partial_{j}H_{i}).

By symmetry,

(6.17)

\partial_{i}\partial_{j}Q^{\prime}=M^{\ell-2}((\ell-1)(\partial_{i}M)H_{j}+M\partial_{i}H_{j}).

Combining (
6.16
) and (
6.17
), we have that for all
1\leq i,j\leq d
, there exists a polynomial
N_{i,j}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
such that

(6.18)

(\partial_{i}M)H_{j}-(\partial_{j}M)H_{i}=MN_{i,j}.

By Proposition
6.3
,
\deg(N_{i,j})\leq\deg(H_{i})-1\leq\deg(Q)-2\ell
.
For all
1\leq i,j\leq d^{\prime},i\neq j
, there exist unique polynomials
N_{1,i,j},N_{2,i,j},N_{3,i,j}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(N_{1,i,j}),\deg(N_{2,i,j})

\leq\deg(N_{i,j})-1\leq s-2\ell-1
and
\deg(N_{3,i,j})\leq\deg(N_{i,j})-2\leq s-2\ell-2
such that

(6.19)

N_{i,j}=(\partial_{i}M)N_{1,i,j}+(\partial_{j}M)N_{2,i,j}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{i}M)(\partial_{j}M)N_{3,i,j},

where

N_{1,i,j}
is independent of
n_{j}
and
N_{2,i,j}
is independent of
n_{i}
.
Since (
6.18
) implies that

(6.20)

a_{i}n_{i}H_{j}(n)-a_{j}n_{j}H_{i}(n)=M(n)N_{i,j}(n)/2

and thus

(6.21)

a_{j}n_{j}H_{i}(n)-a_{i}n_{i}H_{j}(n)=M(n)N_{j,i}(n)/2

by symmetry, it follows from (
6.20
) and (
6.21
) that
N_{i,j}+N_{j,i}=0
. So

n_{i}(N_{1,i,j}(n)+N_{2,j,i}(n))+n_{j}(N_{1,j,i}(n)+N_{2,i,j}(n))+n_{i}n_{j}(N_{3,i,j}(n)+N_{3,j,i}(n))=0.

Since
N_{1,i,j}+N_{2,j,i}
is independent of
n_{j}
and
N_{1,j,i}(n)+N_{2,i,j}(n)
is independent of
n_{i}
, we have that

(6.22)

N_{1,i,j}+N_{2,j,i}=0,N_{3,i,j}+N_{3,j,i}=0.

Combining (
6.19
), (
6.20
) and (
6.22
),
we have that

(6.23)

a_{i}n_{i}H_{j}(n)-a_{j}n_{j}H_{i}(n)=M(n)(n_{i}N_{1,i,j}(n)-n_{j}N_{1,j,i}(n)+n_{i}n_{j}N_{3,i,j})

for all distinct
1\leq i,j\leq d^{\prime}
. For all
1\leq k\leq d^{\prime},k\neq i,j
, (
6.23
) implies that

(6.24)

a_{i}n_{k}n_{i}H_{j}(n)-a_{j}n_{j}n_{k}H_{i}(n)=M(n)(n_{k}n_{i}N_{1,i,j}(n)-n_{j}n_{k}N_{1,j,i}(n)+n_{i}n_{j}n_{k}N_{3,i,j}).

By symmetry,

(6.25)

a_{j}n_{i}n_{j}H_{k}(n)-a_{k}n_{k}n_{i}H_{j}(n)=M(n)(n_{i}n_{j}N_{1,j,k}(n)-n_{k}n_{i}N_{1,k,j}(n)+n_{i}n_{j}n_{k}N_{3,j,k})

and

(6.26)

a_{k}n_{j}n_{k}H_{i}(n)-a_{i}n_{i}n_{j}H_{k}(n)=M(n)(n_{j}n_{k}N_{1,k,i}(n)-n_{i}n_{j}N_{1,i,k}(n)+n_{i}n_{j}n_{k}N_{3,k,i}).

Considering
(\ref {1:force47})\times a_{k}+(\ref {1:force48})\times a_{i}+(\ref {1:force49})\times a_{j}
, we have that

n_{k}n_{i}(a_{k}N_{1,i,j}-a_{i}N_{1,k,j})+n_{i}n_{j}(a_{i}N_{1,j,k}-a_{j}N_{1,i,k})+n_{j}n_{k}(a_{j}N_{1,k,i}-a_{k}N_{1,j,i})

is divisible by
n_{i}n_{j}n_{k}
.
In particular,
n_{k}n_{i}(a_{k}N_{1,i,j}(n)-a_{i}N_{1,k,j}(n))
is divisible by
n_{j}
.
Since
N_{1,i,j}-N_{1,k,j}
is independent from
n_{j}
, we must have that
a_{k}N_{1,i,j}=a_{i}N_{1,k,j}
for all distinct
1\leq i,j,k\leq d^{\prime}
.
This implies that
N_{1,i,j}=a_{i}N_{j}
for some
N_{j}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
\deg(Q)-2\ell-1
for all
1\leq i\leq d^{\prime},i\neq j
.
Substituting this back to (
6.23
), we have that for all
1\leq i,j\leq d^{\prime},i\neq j
,

(6.27)

a_{i}n_{i}(H_{j}(n)-M(n)N_{j}(n))-a_{j}n_{j}(H_{i}(n)-M(n)N_{i}(n))=n_{i}n_{j}M(n)N_{3,i,j}.

Setting
j=1
and
i=2
, (
6.27
) implies that

(6.28)

H_{1}(n)-M(n)N_{1}(n)=2cn_{1}G(n)=\partial_{1}M(n)G(n)

for some polynomial
G
of degree at most
\deg(Q)-2\ell
.

By (
6.18
) and (
6.28
), for all
1\leq j\leq d
,

\begin{split}(\partial_{1}M)H_{j}=MN_{1,j}+(\partial_{j}M)H_{1}=M(N_{1,j}+(\partial_{j}M)N_{1})+(\partial_{j}M)(\partial_{1}M)G,\end{split}

which implies that

\begin{split}2cn_{1}(H_{j}(n)-\partial_{j}M(n)G(n))=M(n)(N_{1,j}(n)+\partial_{j}M(n)N_{1}(n)).\end{split}

It is not hard to see that
N_{1,j}(n)+\partial_{j}M(n)N_{1}(n)=2cn_{1}F_{j}(n)
for some polynomial
F_{j}
of degree at most
\deg(Q)-2\ell-1
. Therefore,

\begin{split}H_{j}-(\partial_{j}M)G=MF_{j}.\end{split}

So for all
1\leq i\leq d
,

\begin{split}&\quad\partial_{i}(Q-M(W_{\ell-1}+\ell^{-1}M^{\ell-1}G))=M^{\ell-1}H_{i}-\partial_{i}(\ell^{-1}M^{\ell}G)\\
&=M^{\ell-1}H_{i}-(\partial_{i}M)M^{\ell-1}G-\ell^{-1}M^{\ell}\partial_{i}G=M^{\ell}(F_{i}-\ell^{-1}\partial_{i}G).\end{split}

Since
\deg(W_{\ell-1}+\ell^{-1}M^{\ell-1}G)\leq\max\{\deg(W_{\ell-1}),\deg(M^{\ell-1}G)\}\leq\deg(Q)-2
,
the claim follows for
\ell
by taking
W_{\ell}=W_{\ell-1}+\ell^{-1}M^{\ell-1}G
. This finishes the proof of Claim 2.

Taking
\ell=\lfloor\deg(Q)/2\rfloor+1
in Claim 2, we have that exists a polynomial
W
of degree at most
s-2
such that
\partial_{i}(Q-MW)=0
for all
1\leq i\leq d
.
This implies that
Q=MW+a
for some
a\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Since
Q(\mathbb{0})=0
, we have that
Q=MW
and we are done.
∎

6.3.
Solution to a special polynomial equation

In this section, we study the solutions to polynomial equations of the form

(6.29)

\Delta_{h_{s-1}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}P(n)+\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}Q(n)=0,\text{ for all/many }(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))

for some quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
, which will be used crucially later in the paper.
We begin with a preparatory result:

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
\delta>0
,
d,d^{\prime},s\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq d^{\prime}\geq 4
,
p\gg_{d,s}\delta^{-O_{d,s}(1)}
be a prime number,

P,Q\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be polynomials of degrees at most
s
and
s+1
respectively with
Q(\mathbb{0})=0
,
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}
, and
H\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
|H|>\delta p^{d}
.
Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form given by

M(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=cn^{2}_{1}+n^{2}_{2}+\dots+n_{d^{\prime}}^{2}.

Suppose that for all
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
h=(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})\in H
with
M(n)=(nA)\cdot h=0
(where
A
is the matrix associated to
M
), we have that
P(n)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}\partial_{i}Q(n)=0
. Then there exist
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degrees at most
s-2
and
s-1
respectively such that
P=MP^{\prime}
and
Q=MQ^{\prime}
.

Note that if
h_{1}\neq 0
, then
(nA)\cdot h=0
if and only if

n=(-(h_{2}z_{2}+\dots+h_{d^{\prime}}z_{d^{\prime}}),ch_{1}z_{2},\dots,ch_{1}z_{d})

for some
z_{2},\dots,z_{d}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}
. On the other hand, the set of
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
h_{1}=0
is of cardinality
p^{d-1}
. Combining this observation with
Proposition
6.5
, we may reduce Proposition
6.6
to the following proposition:

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\delta>0
,
d,d^{\prime},s\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq d^{\prime}\geq 4
,
p\gg_{d,s}\delta^{-O_{d,s}(1)}
be a prime number,

P,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{d}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be of degrees at most
s
,
c\in\{1,\dots,p-1\}
and
H\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
|H|>\delta p^{d}
.
Let

M(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=cn^{2}_{1}+n^{2}_{2}+\dots+n_{d^{\prime}}^{2}

and

L_{h}(z_{2},\dots,z_{d}):=(-(h_{2}z_{2}+\dots+h_{d^{\prime}}z_{d^{\prime}}),ch_{1}z_{2},\dots,ch_{1}z_{d})

for
h=(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Suppose that for all
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}
and
h=(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})\in H
,
M(L_{h}(z))=0
implies that

P(L_{h}(z))+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}Q_{i}(L_{h}(z))=0.

Then for all
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
,
M(n)=0
implies that

P(n)=0
and
\partial_{j}M(n)Q_{i}(n)=\partial_{i}M(n)Q_{j}(n)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq d
.

Proof.

We assume that all the constants in the proof depends implicitly on
d
and
s
.
Let
A
be the (diagonal) matrix associated to
M
and
a_{i}
be the
(i,i)
-th entry of
A
. Passing to a subset if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
h_{1}\neq 0
for all
h\in H
.
The assumption is then equivalent of saying that for all
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
h\in H
, if
(nA)\cdot h=M(n)=0
then
P(n)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}Q_{i}(n)=0
.
Since
d^{\prime}\geq 3
, by Lemma
4.12
, the set
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
M(h)=0
is
O_{d,s}(p^{d-1})
. Passing to a subset if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
M(h)\neq 0
for all
h\in H
.

Let
Z
be the set of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
nA=\mathbb{0}
. Then
|Z|=p^{d-d^{\prime}}\leq p^{d-4}
.
Denote
V_{h}:=\{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon(nA)\cdot h=0\}=(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h\})^{\perp_{M}}
.
We say that
(n,h)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is a
good pair
if
(nA)\cdot h=M(n)=0
,
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z

and
h\in H
.
For each
h\in H
,
V_{h}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension 1 and
V_{h}\cap(V_{h})^{\perp_{M}}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
(since
M(h)\neq 0
). By Proposition
4.8
,
\text{rank}(M|_{V_{h}})\geq d^{\prime}-1\geq 3
.
By Corollary
4.14
and the fact that
|Z|\leq p^{d-4}
, there exist
p^{d-2}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
many
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
(n,h)
is a good pair.
So there are in total at least
\delta p^{2d-2}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
many good pairs. On the other hand, for each
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z
with
M(n)=0
, since
nA\neq\mathbb{0}
, there exist
p^{d-1}
many
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
(nA)\cdot h=0
.
By Lemma
6.1
, the number of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z
with
M(n)=0
is
p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
.
From this it is easy to see that there exists a subset
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z
with
|X|>\frac{\delta}{2}p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
such that for all
n\in X
,
M(n)=0
, and there exists a subset
H_{n}\subseteq H
with
|H_{n}|>\frac{\delta}{2}p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
such that
(n,h)
is a good pair for all
h\in H_{n}
.

For all
n\in X
, let
W_{n}
be the subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1}
spanned by
(1,h),h\in H_{n}
. We claim that
W_{n}=\mathbb{F}_{p}\times V_{n}
. Clearly,
W_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}\times V_{n}
, so it suffices to show that
W_{n}
is of dimension
d
. Suppose on the contrary that
W_{n}
is of dimension at most
d-1
. Let
v_{i}=(v_{i,0},\dots,v_{i,d})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1},1\leq i\leq L
be a basis of
W_{n}
. If
(1,h)=a_{1}v_{1}+\dots+a_{L}v_{L}
, we must have that
a_{1}v_{i,0}+\dots+a_{L}v_{L,0}=1
.
So there are at most
p^{L-1}\leq p^{d-2}
many
(1,h)
belonging to
W_{n}
, meaning that
|H_{n}|\leq p^{d-2}
, a contradiction since
|H_{n}|>\frac{\delta}{2}p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
and
p\gg\delta^{-O(1)}
. So
W_{n}=\mathbb{F}_{p}\times V_{n}
.
This proves the claim.

Since
P(n)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}Q_{i}(n)=0
for all
n\in X
and
h\in H_{n}
, we have that

(P(n),Q_{1}(n),\dots,Q_{d}(n))\cdot(1,h)=0

for all
h\in H_{n}
. By linearity,

(P(n),Q_{1}(n),\dots,Q_{d}(n))\cdot v=0

for all
v\in W_{n}
. Since by the claim
W_{n}=\mathbb{F}_{p}\times V_{n}
, we have that
P(n)=0
and that

(nA)\cdot h=0\Rightarrow(Q_{1}(n),\dots,Q_{d}(n))\cdot h=0

for all
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
n\in X
. This implies that

(Q_{1}(n),\dots,Q_{d}(n))=F(n)(nA)
for some
F(n)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
n\in X
.
In other words,

\partial_{j}M(n)Q_{i}(n)=2a_{i}a_{j}n_{i}n_{j}F(n)=\partial_{i}M(n)Q_{j}(n)

for all
1\leq i,j\leq d
and
n\in X
.

Finally,
since
|X|>\frac{\delta}{2}p^{d-1}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
, by Lemma
6.1
, for all
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
,
M(n)=0
implies that
P(n)=0
and
\partial_{j}M(n)Q_{i}(n)=\partial_{i}M(n)Q_{j}(n)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq d
.
∎

We are now able provide a solution to (
6.29
):

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta>0
,
p\gg_{d,k}\delta^{-O_{d,k}(1)}
be a prime, and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form.
Let
W
be a subset of
\Box_{s}(V(M))
such that either

•

W=\Box_{s}(V(M))
and
\text{rank}(M)\geq s+3
; or

•

|W|\geq\delta|\Box_{s}(V(M))|
and
\text{rank}(M)\geq s^{2}+s+3
.

Let
P,Q\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P)\leq k-1
and
\deg(Q)\leq k
be such that for all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in W
, we have that

\Delta_{h_{s-1}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}P(n)+\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}Q(n)=0

(where
\Delta_{h_{s-1}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}P(n)
is understood as
P(n)
when
s=1
). Then

P=MP_{1}+P_{2}\text{ and }Q=MQ_{1}+Q_{2}

for some
P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P_{1})\leq k-3
,
\deg(P_{2})\leq s-2
,
\deg(Q_{1})\leq k-2
,
\deg(Q_{2})\leq s-1
.

Therefore, Proposition
6.8
provides solutions to equation (
6.29
) for two cases. The first case is when (
6.29
) holds for all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))
, the second case is when (
6.29
) holds for a dense subset of
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))
(in the later case we need to impose a stronger lower bound for the dimension
d
).

13
13

13

The reason we do not obtain the lower bound
s+3
in the second case is because the dimension restriction in the Fubini’s theorem (Theorem
C.3
) is not optimal. The lower bound
s^{2}+s+3
could be improved if we obtain a finer version of Theorem
C.3
(see also Remark
C.4
). We do not pursue this improvement in this paper.

Proof of Proposition
6.8
.

Throughout the proof we assume that
p\gg_{d,k}\delta^{-O_{d,k}(1)}
.
Let
A
be the matrix associated with
M
and denote
d^{\prime}=\text{rank}(M)
. By Lemmas
4.3
and
4.18
, under a change of variable, it suffices to consider the case when
A
is a diagonal matrix with diagonal
(c,1,\dots,1,0,\dots,0)
for some
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}
, where there are
d-d^{\prime}
zero on the diagonal.
For
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, let
U_{h}:=\{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon M(n+h/2)=M(n-h/2)\}
. Then we may write
U_{h}=V_{h}+u_{h}
for some
u_{h}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, where
V_{h}:=\{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon(nA)\cdot h=0\}
.
Let
Z
be the set of
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
\dim(V_{h})\neq d-1
or
\dim(V_{h}\cap V_{h}^{\perp_{M}})\neq d-d^{\prime}
.

Claim 1.
We have that
|Z|\leq O_{d}(p^{d-1})
.

Let
Z^{\prime}
be the set of
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that either
(hA)\cdot h=0
or at least one of the coordinate of
h
is 0.
Since
\text{rank}(A)=d^{\prime}\geq 3
, by Lemma
4.12
, it is not hard to see that
|Z^{\prime}|=O_{d}(p^{d-1})
. Now fix any
h=(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z^{\prime}
. Then since
hA\neq\mathbb{0}
, we have that
\dim(V_{h})=d-1
. Note that

\{-h_{i}e_{1}+ch_{1}e_{i}\colon 2\leq i\leq d^{\prime}\}\cup\{e_{j}\colon d^{\prime}+1\leq j\leq d\}

is a basis of
V_{h}
, where
e_{i}
is the
i
-th standard unit vector. From this it is not hard to compute that

(h_{1},\dots,h_{d^{\prime}},0,\dots,0),e_{d^{\prime}+1},\dots,e_{d}

is a basis of
(V_{h})^{\perp_{M}}
. Therefore,
\dim((V_{h})^{\perp_{M}})=d-d^{\prime}+1
.
Finally, it is not hard to compute by induction that

\det\begin{bmatrix}-h_{2}&ch_{1}&&&\\
-h_{3}&&ch_{1}&&\\
\dots&&&\dots&\\
-h_{d^{\prime}}&&&&ch_{1}\\
h_{1}&\dots&\dots&\dots&h_{d^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=(-1)^{d^{\prime}+1}(ch_{1})^{d^{\prime}-2}((hA)\cdot h)\neq 0.

This implies that
V_{h}+(V_{h})^{\perp_{M}}=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. So

\dim(V_{h}\cap V_{h}^{\perp_{M}})=\dim(V_{h})+\dim(V_{h}^{\perp_{M}})-\dim(V_{h}+(V_{h})^{\perp_{M}})=(d-1)+(d-d^{\prime}+1)-d=d-d^{\prime}.

Therefore,
Z\subseteq Z^{\prime}
and thus
|Z|\leq O_{d}(p^{d-1})
.
This proves Claim 1.

By the construction of
Z
and Proposition
4.8
(ii), for all
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z
, we have that
\text{rank}(M_{h})=d^{\prime}-1
.
Let
L_{h}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}\to V_{h}
be any bijective linear transformation and denote
M_{h}(m):=M(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2)
.
By the definition of
U_{h}
, we have that
M(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2)=M(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}+h/2)
for all
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}
. From this it
is not hard to check that

(6.30)

(m,m_{1},\dots,m_{s-1})\in\Box_{s-1}(M_{h})\Leftrightarrow(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2,L_{h}(m_{1}),\dots,L_{h}(m_{s-1}),h)\in\Box_{s}(M).

For all
h_{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, let
W_{h_{s}}
be the set of
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s-1})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{s}
such that
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in W
. For all
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z
, let
W^{\prime}_{h}
be the set of
(m,m_{1},\dots,m_{s-1})
such that

(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2,L_{h}(m_{1}),\dots,L_{h}(m_{s-1}))\in W_{h}.

Since
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))
for all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s-1})\in W_{h_{s}}
,
it follows from (
6.30
) that

(6.31)

W^{\prime}_{h}\subseteq\Box_{s-1}(V(M_{h}))\text{ and }|W^{\prime}_{h}|=|W_{h}|\text{ for all }h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z.

If
|W|\geq\delta|\Box_{s}(V(M))|
and
\text{rank}(M)\geq s^{2}+s+3
, then by Proposition
4.19
, we have that

\sum_{h_{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}|W_{h_{s}}|=|W|>\delta|\Box_{s}(V(M))|=\delta p^{(s+1)d-(\frac{s(s+1)}{2}+1)}(1+O_{s}(p^{-1/2})).

Moreover, for all but at most
s(s+1)p^{d+s-\text{rank}(M)}
many
h_{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, we have that

|W_{h_{s}}|\leq p^{ds-(\frac{s(s+1)}{2}+1)}
.
Also the sum of
|W_{h_{s}}|

over those at most
s(s+1)p^{d+s-d^{\prime}}
exceptionally
h_{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is at most
s(s+1)p^{(s+1)d+s-d^{\prime}}
. It then follows from the Pigeonhole Principle that there exists
W_{\ast}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z
of cardinality at least
\delta p^{d}/4
such that

|W_{h_{s}}|\geq\delta p^{ds-(\frac{s(s+1)}{2}+1)}/4
for all
h_{s}\in W_{\ast}
.
Since
\text{rank}(M_{h})\geq(s-1)^{2}+(s-1)+3
, by Proposition
4.19
and (
6.31
), we have that

|W^{\prime}_{h}|=|W_{h}|\geq\delta p^{ds-(\frac{s(s+1)}{2}+1)}/4=\delta p^{(d-1)s-(\frac{s(s-1)}{2}+1)}/4=O(\delta)|\Box_{s-1}(V(M_{h}))|

for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
.

On the other hand, if
W=\Box_{s}(V(M))
and
\text{rank}(M)\geq s+3
, then for all
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\backslash Z
, it follows from (
6.30
) that

W^{\prime}_{h}=\Box_{s-1}(V(M_{h}))
. Therefore, in both cases,
there exists
W_{\ast}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of cardinality at least
\delta p^{d}/4
such that

(6.32)

|W^{\prime}_{h}|=|W_{h}|\geq O(\delta)|\Box_{s-1}(V(M_{h}))|

for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
.

So the assumption and (
6.30
) implies that

\Delta_{L_{h}(m_{s-1})}\dots\Delta_{L_{h}(m_{1})}P(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2)+\Delta_{L_{h}(m_{s-1})}\dots\Delta_{L_{h}(m_{1})}\Delta_{h}Q(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2)=0

for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
and
(m,m_{1},\dots,m_{s-1})\in W^{\prime}_{h}
.
Writing
P_{h}:=P(L_{h}(\cdot)+u_{h}-h/2)
and
Q_{h}:=Q(L_{h}(\cdot)+u_{h}+h/2)-Q(L_{h}(\cdot)+u_{h}-h/2)
, we have that

(6.33)

\Delta_{m_{s-1}}\dots\Delta_{m_{1}}(P_{h}(m)+Q_{h}(m))=0

for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
and
(m,m_{1},\dots,m_{s-1})\in W^{\prime}_{h}
. We remark that (
6.33
) is also valid when
s=1
, in which case (
6.33
) is understood as

(6.34)

P_{h}(m)+Q_{h}(m)=0

for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
and
m\in W^{\prime}_{h}
.

Claim 2.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
, we may write

(6.35)

P_{h}+Q_{h}=M_{h}R_{h}+R^{\prime}_{h}

for some
R_{h},R^{\prime}_{h}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(R_{h})\leq k-3
and
\deg(R^{\prime}_{h})\leq t-1
. Then

P=MP^{\prime}+P^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }Q=MQ^{\prime}+Q^{\prime\prime}

for some
P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P^{\prime})\leq k-3
,
\deg(P^{\prime\prime})\leq t-1
,
\deg(Q^{\prime})\leq k-2
and
\deg(Q^{\prime\prime})\leq t
.

If
\max\{\deg(P),\deg(Q)-1\}\leq t-1
, then Claim 2 follows by setting
P=P^{\prime\prime},Q=Q^{\prime\prime}
and
P^{\prime}=Q^{\prime}=0
.
Assume now that Claim 2 holds when
\max\{\deg(P),\deg(Q)-1\}\leq k^{\prime}-1
for some
t\leq k^{\prime}\leq k-1
and we prove that Claim 2 holds when
\max\{\deg(P),\deg(Q)-1\}\leq k^{\prime}
.
Note that (
6.35
) implies that

(6.36)

\begin{split}&\quad P(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2)+Q(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}+h/2)-Q(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2)\\
&=M(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2)R_{h}(m)+R^{\prime}_{h}(m)\end{split}

for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
. Since
\deg(R^{\prime}_{h})\leq k^{\prime}-1
, it is not hard to see that
\deg(R_{h})\leq k^{\prime}-2
.
Let
\tilde{P},\tilde{Q},\tilde{M},\tilde{R}_{h}
be the degree
k^{\prime},k^{\prime}+1,2,k^{\prime}-2
terms of
P,Q,M,R_{h}
respectively. Write
h=(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})
.
Comparing the degree
k^{\prime}
terms of (
6.36
) and notice that the degree
k^{\prime}
terms of
Q(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}+h/2)-Q(L_{h}(m)+u_{h}-h/2))
is
\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}\partial_{i}\tilde{Q}(L_{h}(m))
, we have that

\tilde{P}(L_{h}(m))+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}\partial_{i}\tilde{Q}(L_{h}(m))=\tilde{M}(L_{h}(m))\tilde{R}_{h}(m).

Therefore for all
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
with
(nA)\cdot h=M(n)=0
, there exists
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-1}
with
L_{h}(m)=n
and thus

\tilde{P}(n)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}\partial_{i}\tilde{Q}(n)=\tilde{M}(n)\tilde{R}_{h}(m)=0.

Since
|Z|\leq O_{d}(p^{d-1})
by Claim 1, we have that
|W_{\ast}\backslash Z|\geq p^{d}/8
.
Since
\text{rank}(M)\geq s+3\geq 4
,
by Proposition
6.6
, we have that
\tilde{P}=\tilde{M}P^{\prime}
and
\tilde{Q}=\tilde{M}Q^{\prime}
for some
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}-2\leq k-3
and
\deg(Q^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}-1\leq k-2
. Since
M-\tilde{M}
is of degree at most 1, we may write

P=MP^{\prime}+P^{\prime\prime},Q=MQ^{\prime}+Q^{\prime\prime}

for some
P^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P^{\prime\prime})\leq k^{\prime}-1
and
\deg(Q^{\prime\prime})\leq k^{\prime}
.
Writing
P^{\prime\prime}_{h}:=P^{\prime\prime}(L_{h}(\cdot)+u_{h}-h/2)
and
Q^{\prime\prime}_{h}:=Q^{\prime\prime}(L_{h}(\cdot)+u_{h}+h/2)-Q^{\prime\prime}(L_{h}(\cdot)+u_{h}-h/2)
,
It follows from (
6.35
) and the identity

M_{h}=M(L_{h}(\cdot)+u_{h}-h/2)=M(L_{h}(\cdot)+u_{h}+h/2)

that
for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
, we may write

(6.37)

P^{\prime\prime}_{h}+Q^{\prime\prime}_{h}=M_{h}R^{\prime\prime}_{h}+R^{\prime\prime\prime}_{h}

for some
R^{\prime\prime}_{h},R^{\prime\prime\prime}_{h}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(R^{\prime\prime}_{h})\leq k-3
and
\deg(R^{\prime\prime\prime}_{h})\leq t-1
. Since
\max\{\deg(P^{\prime\prime}),\deg(Q^{\prime\prime})-1\}\leq k^{\prime}-1
, by induction hypothesis, (
6.37
) implies that

P^{\prime\prime}=MP^{\prime\prime\prime}+P^{\prime\prime\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}=MQ^{\prime\prime\prime}+Q^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}

for some
P^{\prime\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P^{\prime\prime\prime})\leq k-3
,
\deg(P^{\prime\prime\prime\prime})\leq t-1
,
\deg(Q^{\prime\prime\prime})\leq k-2
and
\deg(Q^{\prime\prime\prime\prime})\leq t
. Then

P=M(P^{\prime}+P^{\prime\prime\prime})+P^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\text{ and }Q=M(Q^{\prime}+Q^{\prime\prime\prime})+Q^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}.

This completes the induction step and proves Claim 2.

We now complete the proof of Proposition
6.8
by induction on
s
.
If
s=1
, then since
\text{rank}(M_{h})\geq 3
, by (
6.34
) and Proposition
6.3
,
we may write

P_{h}+Q_{h}=M_{h}R_{h}

for some
R_{h}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(R_{h})\leq k-3
for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
. By Claim 2 (setting
t=0
),

P=MP_{1}\text{ and }Q=MQ_{1}+C

for some
C\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
P_{1},Q_{1}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P_{1})\leq k-3
,
\deg(Q_{1})\leq k-2
.

Suppose we have proved Proposition
6.8
for
s-1
for some
s\geq 2
. We now prove it for
s
. For all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
,
since
\text{rank}(M_{h})=\text{rank}(M)-1
and
\deg(P_{h}+\Delta_{h}Q_{h})\leq k-1
, by applying the induction hypothesis to (
6.33
), we may write

P_{h}+Q_{h}=M_{h}R_{h}+R^{\prime}_{h}

for some
R_{h},R^{\prime}_{h}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(R_{h})\leq k-3
and
\deg(R^{\prime}_{h})\leq s-2
for all
h\in W_{\ast}\backslash Z
.
By Claim 2 (setting
t=s+1
),

P=MP^{\prime}+P^{\prime\prime},Q=MQ^{\prime}+Q^{\prime\prime}

for some
P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(P^{\prime})\leq k-3
,
\deg(P^{\prime\prime})\leq s-2
,
\deg(Q^{\prime})\leq k-2
and
\deg(Q^{\prime\prime})\leq s-1
. This completes the induction step and we are done.
∎

6.4.
Intrinsic definitions for polynomials on
V(M)

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form.
Recall that we define a function
f\colon V(M)\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
to be a polynomial if
f
is the restriction to
V(M)
of some polynomial
f^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
defined in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. It is natural to ask if there is an intrinsic way to define polynomials on
V(M)
(i.e. without looking at points outside
V(M)
). A natural way to do so is to use anti-derivative properties of polynomials.
Let
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
. It is not hard to see that
g
is of degree at most
s-1
if and only if
\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)=0
for all
n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. This observation provides us a promising alternative way to define polynomials and leads to the following question:

Conjecture 6.9

(Intrinsic definition for polynomials)
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a function,
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
p\gg_{d}1
be a prime. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form with
\text{rank}(M)\gg_{s}1
. Then the followings are equivalent:

(1)

\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)=0
for all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))
;

(2)

there exists a polynomial
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s-1
such that
g(n)=g^{\prime}(n)
for all
n\in V(M)
.

If Conjecture
6.9
holds, then we may use Part (i) of Conjecture
6.9
as an intrinsic definition for polynomials on
V(M)
.
In this paper, we were unable to prove Conjecture
6.9
. Instead, we prove the following special case of Conjecture
6.9
, which is good enough for the purpose of this paper:

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p\gg_{d}1
be a prime, and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
of rank at least
s+3
. Then for any
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(g)\leq s
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

for all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))
,
we have that

\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)=0;

(2)

we have

g=Mg_{1}+g_{2}

for some
g_{1},g_{2}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(g_{1})\leq s-2
,
\deg(g_{2})\leq s-1
;

(3)

we have

g(n)=((nA)\cdot n)g_{1}(n)+g_{2}(n)

for some
g_{1},g_{2}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(g_{1})\leq s-2
,
\deg(g_{2})\leq s-1
.

Proof.

We first show that (ii) implies (i).
For all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))
, note that

\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)=\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}(M(n)g_{1}(n)).

Since
M(n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s})=0
for all
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{s}\in\{0,1\}
, we have
\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}(M(n)g_{1}(n))=0
and we are done.

The part (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) follows from Proposition
6.8
(by setting
k=s
,
P\equiv 0
and
Q=g
). Finally, (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii) since the degree of the polynomial
M(n)-(nA)\cdot n
is at most 1.
∎

As a corollary of Proposition
6.10
, we have:

Corollary 6.11
.

Let
d,k,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p\gg_{d}1
be a prime, and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
.
Let
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be linearly independent and
M
-non-isotropic vectors.
If
d\geq k+s+3
, then for any polynomial
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

for all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M)^{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}})
, we have that
\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)=0
;

(2)

we have

g(n)=M(n)g_{0}(n)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(M(n+m_{i})-M(n))g_{i}(n)+g^{\prime}(n)

for some homogeneous
g_{0},\dots,g_{k}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(g_{0})=s-2
and
\deg(g_{1})=\dots=\deg(g_{k})=s-1
, and some
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s-1
;
14
14

14

Recall that
g_{0},\dots,g_{k}
are allowed to be constant zero by Convention
2.2
.

(3)

we have

g(n)=((nA)\cdot n)g_{0}(n)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}((nA)\cdot m_{i})g_{i}(n)+g^{\prime}(n)

for some homogeneous
g_{0},\dots,g_{k}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(g_{0})=s-2
and
\deg(g_{1})=\dots=\deg(g_{k})=s-1
, and some
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s-1
.

Proof.

Clearly (ii) is equivalent to (iii). Note that for all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M)^{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}})
and
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{s}\in\{0,1\}
, we have that

M(n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s})=M(n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s}+m_{i})=0

for all
1\leq i\leq k
. So (ii) implies (i). So it suffices to show that (i) implies (ii).

By a change of variables
n\mapsto n+m
for some
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, it suffices to consider the special case when
M(n)=(nA)\cdot n-\lambda
for some invertible symmetric matrix
A
and some
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Let
V
be the span of
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}
. Since
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}
are linearly independent and
M
-non-isotropic, and since
A
is invertible, we have that
V^{\perp_{M}}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
d-k
with
V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
.
Let

W:=\{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon 2(nA)\cdot m_{i}+(m_{i}A)\cdot m_{i}=0,1\leq i\leq k\}.

Since
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}
are linearly independent and
A
is invertible, we may write
W=V^{\perp_{M}}+w
for some
w\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-k}\to V^{\perp_{M}}
be any bijective linear transformation. Denote
M^{\prime}(n^{\prime}):=M(\phi(n^{\prime})+w)
and
g^{\prime}(n^{\prime}):=g(\phi(n^{\prime})+w)
for all
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-k}
.
By Proposition
4.8
(ii),
M^{\prime}
is a non-degenerate quadratic form and thus
\text{rank}(M^{\prime})=d-k\geq s+3
.
By Lemma
4.18
, we have that
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M)^{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}})
if and only if there exists
(n^{\prime},h^{\prime}_{1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M^{\prime}))
with
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})=(\phi(n^{\prime})+w,\phi(h^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,\phi(h^{\prime}_{s}))
.
So (i) implies that
\Delta_{h^{\prime}_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h^{\prime}_{1}}g^{\prime}(n^{\prime})=0
for all
(n^{\prime},h^{\prime}_{1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M^{\prime}))
.
By Proposition
6.10
,

(6.38)

g^{\prime}(n^{\prime})=M^{\prime}(n^{\prime})g^{\prime}_{1}(n^{\prime})+g^{\prime}_{2}(n^{\prime})

for some
g^{\prime}_{1},g^{\prime}_{2}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(g^{\prime}_{1})=s-2
and
\deg(g^{\prime}_{2})\leq s-1
. We may further require
g^{\prime}_{1}
to be homogeneous.

By a change of variables
n\to nB
for some invertible matrix
B
, we may assume without loss of generality that
m_{i}A=e_{d-k+i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. Then
V^{\perp_{M}}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d-k}\}
and
W=V^{\perp_{M}}+w
for
w=(0,\dots,0,-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{1}/2,\dots,-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{k}/2)
.
Note that
n\in V(M)^{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}}
if and only if
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d-k},-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{1}/2,\dots,-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{k}/2)\in V(M)
for some
n_{1},\dots,n_{d-k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. So it follows from (
6.38
) that

(6.39)

\begin{split}&\quad g(n_{1},\dots,n_{d-k},-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{1}/2,\dots,-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{k}/2)\\
&=M(n_{1},\dots,n_{d-k},-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{1}/2,\dots,-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{k}/2)g^{\prime}_{1}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d-k})+g^{\prime}_{2}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d-k}).\end{split}

Note that for all
1\leq i\leq k
and
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, we have that

n_{d-k+i}+(m_{i}A)\cdot m_{i}/2=\frac{1}{2}(2(nA)\cdot m_{i}+(m_{i}A)\cdot m_{i})=\frac{1}{2}(M(n+m_{i})-M(n)).

So for
F=g
or
M
, the difference

F(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})-F(n_{1},\dots,n_{d-k},-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{1}/2,\dots,-(m_{1}A)\cdot m_{k}/2)

can be written in the form

\sum_{i=1}^{k}(M(n+m_{i})-M(n))Q_{i}(n)

for some
Q_{i}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(Q)_{i}\leq\deg(F)-1
.
Combining this with (
6.39
), we deduce that

g(n)=M(n)g_{0}(n)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(M(n+m_{i})-M(n))g_{i}(n)+g^{\prime}(n)

for some
g_{0},\dots,g_{k}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
\deg(g_{0})\leq s-2
and
\deg(g_{1}),\dots,\deg(g_{k})\leq s-1
, and some
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s-1
. Absorbing the lower degree terms of
g_{0},\dots,g_{k}
by
g^{\prime}
if necessary, we may further require that
g_{0},\dots,g_{k}
are homogeneous and that
\deg(g_{0})=s-2
,
\deg(g_{1})=\dots=\deg(g_{k})=s-1
. So (ii) holds and we are done.
∎

6.5.
A parallel matrix proposition

Let
A
and
B
be
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-valued
d\times d
matrices. If
B
is a multiple of
A
, then clearly
nB=\mathbb{0}
implies that
nA=\mathbb{0}
. It is natural to ask if the converse is true, namely if for many
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
,
nB=\mathbb{0}
implies that
nA=\mathbb{0}
, then is it true that
B
must be a multiple of
A
. The next proposition answers this question:

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\delta>0
,
p\gg_{d}\delta^{-O_{d}(1)}
be a prime, and
A
be a
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-valued
d\times d
matrix of rank at least 3. Let
B
be a
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-valued matrix
d\times d
matrix and
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Let
W
be a subset of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of cardinality at least
\delta p^{d}
. Suppose that for all
w\in W
and
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
(nA)\cdot w=0
, we have that

(nB+v)\cdot w=0.

Then
v=\mathbb{0}
and
B=cA
for some
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we assume that
p\gg_{d}\delta^{-O_{d}(1)}
.
Since
\text{rank}(A)\geq 1
, the set of
w\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
wA=\mathbb{0}
is of cardinality at most
p^{d-1}
. So shrinking
\delta
to
\delta/2
if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
wA\neq\mathbb{0}
for all
w\in W
.
Fix any
w\in W
. Since
wA\neq\mathbb{0}
, and since
(nA)\cdot w=0
implies that
(nB+v)\cdot w=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, it is not hard to check that we must have that
v\cdot w=0
and
wB=c_{w}wA
for some
c_{w}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Since
W
is of cardinality at least
\delta p^{d}
, By Lemma
4.11
,
there exist linearly independent
w_{1},\dots,w_{d}\in W
. So
v\cdot w_{i}=0
for
1\leq i\leq d
. This implies that
v=\mathbb{0}
.

For
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, let
M_{z}(n):=(nA)\cdot(n+z)
. Then

(6.40)

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}|V(M_{z})\cap W|=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}}|\{w\in W\colon(wA)\cdot(w+z)=0\}|=\sum_{w\in W}|\{z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon(wA)\cdot(w+z)=0\}|.

Since
wA\neq\mathbb{0}
, we have
|\{z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon(wA)\cdot(w+z)=0\}|=p^{d-1}
. So (
6.40
) is at least

|W|\cdot p^{d-1}\geq\delta p^{2d-1}
.
By the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
|V(M_{z})\cap W|\gg\delta p^{d-1}
.

Since
wB=c_{w}wA
for some
c_{w}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
w\in W
, we have that

(wB)\cdot(w+z)=c_{w}(wA)\cdot(w+z)=0

for all
w\in V(M_{z})\cap W
. Since
|V(M_{z})\cap W|\gg\delta p^{d-1}
, by Lemma
6.1
and Proposition
6.3
, we may write

(wB)\cdot(w+z)=r_{z}M_{z}(w)=r_{z}(wA)\cdot(w+z)

for some
r_{z}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
w\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Viewing both sides as polynomials in the variable
w
and comparing all of their degree 2 terms, we must have that
B=r_{z}A
. We are done.
∎

7.
Algebraic properties for quadratic forms in
\mathbb{Z}/p

In this section, we extend the results in Section
6
to
\mathbb{Q}
-valued polynomials.

7.1.
Quadratic forms in
\mathbb{Z}/p

By using the correspondence between polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we may lift all the definitions pertaining to quadratic forms in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
to the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
setting in the natural way.

Definition 7.1

(Quadratic forms in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
)
.

We say that a function
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
is a
quadratic form
if

M(n)=\frac{1}{p}((nA)\cdot n+n\cdot u+v)

for some
d\times d
symmetric matrix
A
in
\mathbb{Z}
, some
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
v\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We say that
A
is the matrix
associated to

M
.

It is more rigorous to name
M
as a
p
-quadratic form
. However, since the quantity
p
is alway clear in this paper, we simply call
M
to be a quadratic form for short.
Whenever we define a quadratic form in this paper, we will write either
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
or
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
to specify to domain and range of
M
, so that the term quadratic form will not cause confusions.

By Lemma
A.1
,
any quadratic form
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
associated with the matrix
\tilde{A}
induces a quadratic form
M:=\iota\circ p\tilde{M}\circ\tau\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
associated with the matrix
\iota(\tilde{A})
. Conversely, any quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
associated with the matrix
A
admits a regular lifting
