\theta_{w}\cdot(\gamma\circ\tilde{L}_{w}(z))\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
(or equivalently,
\theta_{w}\cdot\gamma(h)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
h\in V_{w}
).

By the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists a subset
W^{\prime}
of
W
of cardinality
\gg_{d,m}\delta^{O_{d,m}(1)}p^{d}
such that either there exists
r\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0<r\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
such that

rq\sum_{i=1}^{d}w^{\prime}_{i}\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
for all
w^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(W^{\prime})
, or there exists
\theta\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
with
0<|\theta|\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
such that
\theta\cdot\gamma(h)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
h\in\tilde{V}_{w}:=\tilde{L}_{w}(\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})=\text{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\tau(L_{w}(e_{1})),\dots,\tau(L_{w}(e_{d-1}))\}
.

If it is the former case, then since
\iota^{-1}(W^{\prime})=\tau(W^{\prime})+p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we must have that
rq\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}/p
for all
1\leq i\leq d
. Since
p\gg_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
, there exist
w_{1},\dots,w_{d}\in W^{\prime}
such that the determinant
Q
of the matrix
(w_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
is not divisible by
p
, where
w_{j}:=(w_{j,1},\dots,w_{j,d})
. Then the fact that
rq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\tau(w_{j,i})\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
for
1\leq j\leq d
implies that
rq\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}/Q
. So
rq\xi_{i}\in\frac{1}{Q}\mathbb{Z}^{m}\cap\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{Z}^{m}=\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and thus
rq\xi\in(\mathbb{Z}^{m})^{d}
(recall that
q\ll_{d}\delta^{-O_{d}(1)}
).

If it is the later case, then it is clear that there exist two
w,w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
such that
\tilde{V}_{w}\neq\tilde{V}_{w^{\prime}}
and that
\theta\cdot\gamma(h)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
h\in\tilde{V}_{w}\cup\tilde{V}_{w}
. By linearity,
\theta\cdot\gamma(h)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
h\in\tilde{V}_{w}+\tilde{V}_{w^{\prime}}
. Since
\tilde{V}_{w}
and
\tilde{V}_{w^{\prime}}
are distinct subspaces of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
of co-dimension 1 and
d\geq 2
, we have that
\tilde{V}_{w}+\tilde{V}_{w^{\prime}}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Equivalently, this means that
\theta\cdot\gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leq i\leq d
.
∎

8.5.
Completion of the proof

We will use Proposition
8.4
to complete the proof of Theorem
5.1
.
Suppose first that
there exists
\theta\in\mathbb{Z}^{m_{\text{lin}}}\times\{0\}^{m-m_{\text{lin}}}
with
0<|\theta|\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
such that
\theta\cdot\gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leq i\leq d
. Consider the map
\eta\colon G\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
defined by

\eta(x):=\theta\cdot\psi(x).

Then
\eta
is a type-I horizontal character and is of complexity at most
O_{d,m}(\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)})
. Moreover, for all
n_{1},\dots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that

\eta(g(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}))=\eta(g(e_{1})^{n_{1}}\dots g(e_{d})^{n_{d}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}n_{i}\theta\cdot\gamma_{i}\equiv 0\mod\mathbb{Z}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We now consider the case
r\xi\in(\mathbb{Z}^{m})^{d}
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
. For
1\leq j\leq m
, let
\alpha_{j}\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
be given by
\alpha_{j}(x):=\eta_{2}([x,\exp(X_{j})])
.
Note that
\alpha_{j}
is a type-I horizontal character, annihilates
G_{2}
, and
\|\alpha_{j}\|\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
. If
\alpha_{j}
is nontrivial for some
1\leq j\leq m
, then for all
n_{1},\dots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that

r\alpha_{j}\circ g(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=r\alpha_{j}(g(e_{1})^{n_{1}}\dots g(e_{d})^{n_{d}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}n_{i}r\xi_{i}\cdot\psi(X_{j})\equiv 0\mod\mathbb{Z},

and we again proves Theorem
5.1
.

Now suppose that all of
\alpha_{j}
are trivial. In this case
\eta_{2}
annihilates
[G,G]
.
So we have that
\Lambda
is the zero matrix and that
\xi
is the zero vector. Therefore
\sigma(h)=0
.
It follows from (
8.24
) that

(8.29)

t_{h}-c_{h}(hA)=q(2h\Omega+u)-c_{h}(hA)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

for some
c_{h}\in\mathbb{Z}/p
for all
h\in J_{2}+pK\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
This implies that
t_{h}=q(2h\Omega+u)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}/p
for all
h\in J_{2}+pK\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, which further implies that
q\Omega
and
qu
takes
\mathbb{Z}/p
coefficients.
So for all
h\in J_{2}+pK\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
(nA)\cdot w\in p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, (
8.29
) implies that

q(2h\Omega+u)\cdot n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Since
J_{2}
contains
\gg_{d,m}\delta^{O_{d,m}(1)}p^{d}
residue classes mod
p\mathbb{Z}
, it follows from
Corollary
7.6
that
qu\in\mathbb{Z}
and
2q\Omega=rA+B^{\prime}
for or some
r\in\mathbb{Z}/p
and matrix
B^{\prime}
with integer entries. It then follows from (
8.19
) and (
8.20
) that (replacing
q
by
2q
if necessary)

(8.30)

\eta_{2}(g_{\text{nlin}}(n))=Q(n)=\frac{1}{q}Q_{2}(n)+T(n)

for some

Q_{2}\in\text{poly}(V_{p}(\tilde{M})\to\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{Z})
and some polynomial
T\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
of degree at most 1.

If
\eta_{2}
is trivial, then
Q\equiv 0
and
\Omega
can be taken to be the zero matrix. It follows from (
8.29
) that

qu-c_{h}(hA)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

for some
c_{h}\in\mathbb{Z}/p
for all
h\in J_{2}
.
Note that if
qu\notin\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, then the number of such
h
is at most
K^{d}\cdot p^{d+1-\text{rank}_{p}(A)}\leq K^{d}p^{d-1}<|J_{2}|
, a contradiction.
So we must have that
qu\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. So it follows from (
8.19
) that if
M(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

q\eta_{1}(g(n))=qP(n)=P_{1}(n)+(qu)\cdot n+qu_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}+qu_{0}.

Since
\eta_{0}(g^{\prime},g)=\eta_{1}(g)+\eta_{2}(g^{\prime}g^{-1})
is nontrivial and
\eta_{2}
is trivial, we have that
\eta_{1}

is nontrivial. Since
q\ll_{d}\delta^{-O_{d}(1)}
,
this completes the proof of Theorem
5.1
by setting
\eta=q\eta_{1}
.

So from now on we assume that
\eta_{2}
is not trivial.
If
m_{\ast}=0
, then
\eta_{2}
vanishes on
G_{2}
and so it is trivial, a contradiction.
We now assume that
m_{\ast}\geq 1
. Since
\eta_{2}
annihilates
[G,G]
, we may extend
\eta_{2}
to a genial type-I horizontal character
\tilde{\eta}_{2}
on
G
in an arbitrary way such that
\tilde{\eta}_{2}|_{G_{2}}=\eta_{2}
, and that the complexity of
\tilde{\eta}_{2}
is comparable to
\eta_{2}
. With a slight abuse of notation, we denote
\tilde{\eta}_{2}
by
\eta_{2}
as well and treat
\eta_{2}
as a genial type-I horizontal character on
G
.
Suppose that
\eta_{2}\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
\eta_{2}(x)=\theta\cdot\psi(x)
for some
\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
with
|\theta|\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-\mathcal{O}_{d,m}(1)}
.
Since
\eta_{2}
annihilates
[G,G]
, we have that
\theta_{i}=0
for all
i>m_{ab}
.

Denote
G^{\prime}_{0}=G^{\prime}_{1}=G
and
G^{\prime}_{i}=G_{i}\cap\ker(\eta_{2})
for
i\geq 2
.
By Lemma 7.8 of
[
8
]
,
G_{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}=(G^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a filtration of
G
with degree at most
s
and nonlinearity degree
m^{\prime}_{\ast}\leq m_{\ast}-1
. Each
G^{\prime}_{i}
is closed, connected and
O_{d,m}(\delta^{-\mathcal{O}_{d,m}(1)})
-rational with respect to the Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
on
G/\Gamma
adapted to
G_{\mathbb{N}}
. We need a special factorization proposition:

Proposition 8.5
.

Fix any
n_{\ast}\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})
.
There exists
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
such that we may write
g=g^{\prime}\gamma
for some rational
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})
and some
\gamma\in\text{poly}_{\approx r,n_{\ast}}(V_{p}(\tilde{M})\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.

We first complete the proof of Theorem
5.1
assuming Proposition
8.5
.
Let the notations be the same as Proposition
8.5
. By Proposition
3.19
, there exists
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r^{\prime}\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
such that
\gamma\in\text{poly}_{r^{\prime}}(V_{p}(\tilde{M})\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.
For all
m\in[r^{\prime}]^{d}
, there exists
\tilde{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
\tilde{m}-m\in r^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\tilde{m}-n_{\ast}\in p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Since
n_{\ast}\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})
, we have
\tilde{m}\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})
. Since
\gamma\in\text{poly}_{r^{\prime}}(V_{p}(\tilde{M})\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
, we have that

(8.31)

\text{$\gamma(\tilde{m})\Gamma=\gamma(r^{\prime}n+m)\Gamma$ for all $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}$ with $r^{\prime}n+m\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})$}.

Denote

g^{\prime}_{m}(n):=\{\gamma(\tilde{m})\}^{-1}g^{\prime}(r^{\prime}n+m)\{\gamma(\tilde{m})\}.

Since
g^{\prime}
and
\gamma
are rational, by Corollary
3.21
, there exists
K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
which is divisible by
pK
such that
g^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}_{m},m\in[r^{\prime}]^{d}
are all
K^{\prime}
-periodic.
For
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, let
M_{m}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
be the quadratic map given by
\tilde{M}_{m}(n):=\tilde{M}(r^{\prime}n+m)
. Then since
r\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
,
\tilde{M}_{m}
and
\tilde{M}
have the same
p
-rank. Note that for all
n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M}_{m})
, we have that
r^{\prime}n+m\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})
.
Then it follows from (
8.31
) and (
8.4
) that

\begin{split}&\quad\delta^{O_{d,m}(1)}\ll_{d,m}|\mathbb{E}_{n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})\cap[pK]^{d}}F(g(n)\Gamma)|=|\mathbb{E}_{n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})\cap[r^{\prime}K^{\prime}]^{d}}F(g(n)\Gamma)|\\
&=|\mathbb{E}_{m\in[r^{\prime}]^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M}_{m})\cap[K^{\prime}]^{d}}F(g(r^{\prime}n+m)\Gamma)|+O(p^{-1/2})\\
&=|\mathbb{E}_{m\in[r^{\prime}]^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M}_{m})\cap[K^{\prime}]^{d}}F(g^{\prime}(r^{\prime}n+m)\gamma(r^{\prime}n+m)\Gamma)|+O(p^{-1/2})\\
&=|\mathbb{E}_{m\in[r^{\prime}]^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M}_{m})\cap[K^{\prime}]^{d}}F(g^{\prime}(r^{\prime}n+m)\gamma(\tilde{m})\Gamma)|+O(p^{-1/2})\\
&=|\mathbb{E}_{m\in[r^{\prime}]^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M}_{m})\cap[K^{\prime}]^{d}}F(\{\gamma(\tilde{m})\}g^{\prime}_{m}(n)\Gamma)|+O(p^{-1/2}).\end{split}

So by the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists some
m\in[r^{\prime}]^{d}
such that

|\mathbb{E}_{n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M}_{m})\cap[K^{\prime}]^{d}}F^{\prime}_{m}(g^{\prime}_{m}(n)\Gamma)|\gg_{d,m}\delta^{O_{d,m}(1)}.

where
F^{\prime}_{m}:=F(\{\gamma(\tilde{m})\}\cdot)
. By the construction of
K^{\prime}
,
g^{\prime}_{m}
is a rational and
K^{\prime}
-periodic polynomial in
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})

and
\|F^{\prime}\|_{\text{Lip}}=O(1)
. We may now invoke the induction hypothesis to deduce that there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
of complexity at most
O_{d,m}(\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)})
such that

\eta\circ g^{\prime}_{m}\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
V_{p}(\tilde{M}_{m})
.
Since

\eta\circ g^{\prime}_{m}(n)=\eta\circ g^{\prime}(r^{\prime}n+m)=\eta\circ g(r^{\prime}n+m)-\eta\circ\gamma(r^{\prime}n+m)\equiv\eta\circ g(r^{\prime}n+m)-\eta\circ\gamma(m)\mod\mathbb{Z}

for all
n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M}_{m})
, we have that
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
V_{p}(\tilde{M})
. This finishes the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

So it remains to prove Proposition
8.5
.

Proof of Proposition
8.5
.

Let
0\leq s^{\prime}\leq s
be the largest integer such that at least one of the entries
\theta_{m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1},\dots,\theta_{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}
is nonzero (where we denote
m_{0}:=0
).
Since

\eta_{2}
is nontrivial, such an
s^{\prime}
always exists and
s^{\prime}\geq 2
. Denote
m^{\prime}:=m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}
.
Then we may write
\theta=(\theta^{\prime},\mathbb{0})
for some
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m^{\prime}}
.

Assume that

\psi(g(n))=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0\leq|i|\leq s}w_{i}\binom{n}{i},

for some
w_{i}\in\{0\}^{m-m_{|i|}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{|i|}}
.
Since
Q(\mathbb{0})=Q(e_{i})=0
, we may write

\psi(g_{\text{nlin}}(n))=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{d},2\leq|i|\leq s}t_{i}\binom{n}{i}

for some
t_{i}\in\{0\}^{m-m_{|i|}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{|i|}}
. Write
w_{i}=(w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime\prime}_{i}),t_{i}=(t^{\prime}_{i},t^{\prime\prime}_{i})
for some
t^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}
and
t^{\prime\prime}_{i},w^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-m^{\prime}}
.
Since the first
m-m_{|i|}
entries of
t_{i}
are zero, and the last
m_{s^{\prime}+1}
entries of
\theta
are zero, we have that
\theta\cdot t_{i}=0
if
|i|\geq s^{\prime}+1
.
By (
8.30
),

\frac{1}{q}Q_{2}(n)+T(n)=\eta_{2}(g_{\text{nlin}}(n))=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},2\leq|i|\leq s}(\theta\cdot t_{i})\binom{n}{i}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},2\leq|i|\leq s^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime}_{i})\binom{n}{i}.

In particular, we have that
\deg(Q_{2})\leq s^{\prime}
.

Since
\theta
is of complexity
\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-\mathcal{O}_{d,m}(1)}
, there exists
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+},q^{\prime}\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-\mathcal{O}_{d,m}(1)}
and
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
whose only nonzero entries are
u_{m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1},\dots,u_{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}
such that

\theta\cdot u=q^{\prime}.
Denote

\gamma(n):=\prod_{i=m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1}^{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{qq^{\prime}}u_{i}(Q_{2}(n)-Q_{2}(n_{\ast}))X_{i}\Bigr).

Since
\deg(Q_{2})\leq s^{\prime}
, it follows from Corollary B.4 of
[
11
]
that
\gamma\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(G_{2})_{\mathbb{N}})
.

We now show that there exists
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-\mathcal{O}_{d,m}(1)}
such that
\gamma
belongs to
\text{poly}_{\approx r,n_{\ast}}(V_{p}(\tilde{M})\to(G_{2})_{\mathbb{N}}|(\Gamma\cap G_{2})).

In fact, it suffices to show that
Q_{2}(n_{\ast}+rn)-Q_{2}(n_{\ast})\in qq^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
n_{\ast}+rn\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})
. By interpolation, all the coefficients of the polynomial
Q_{2}
belongs to
\mathbb{Z}/p^{s^{\prime}}q^{\prime\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}=O_{d,s}(1)
and
q^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
q^{\prime\prime}\leq O_{d,s}(1)
. So for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have that
Q_{2}(n_{\ast}+rn)-Q_{2}(n_{\ast})\in qq^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}/p^{s^{\prime}}
, where
r:=qq^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}
. On the other hand, if
n_{\ast}+rn,n_{\ast}\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})
, then
Q_{2}(n_{\ast}+rn)-Q_{2}(n_{\ast})\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
qq^{\prime}
is not divisible by
p
, this implies that
Q_{2}(n_{\ast}+rn)-Q_{2}(n_{\ast})\in qq^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}
. So

\gamma\in\text{poly}_{\approx r,n_{\ast}}(V_{p}(\tilde{M})\to(G_{2})_{\mathbb{N}}|(\Gamma\cap G_{2})).

Writing
h:=g\gamma^{-1}
, we have that
h\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(G_{2})_{\mathbb{N}}|(\Gamma\cap G_{2}))
.
Note that for all
n,h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

(8.32)

\begin{split}&\quad\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}(\eta_{2}\circ g(n))=\eta_{2}\circ(\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}g)(n)=\eta_{2}\circ(\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}g_{\text{nlin}})(n)\\
&=\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}(\eta_{2}\circ g_{\text{nlin}})(n)=\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}(\frac{1}{q}Q_{2}(n)+T(n))=\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}(\frac{1}{q}Q_{2}(n)).\end{split}

So (
8.32
) implies that

(8.33)

\begin{split}&\quad\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}(\eta_{2}\circ h(n))=\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}(\eta_{2}\circ g(n))-\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}(\eta_{2}\circ\gamma(n))=0.\end{split}

Set
g^{\prime}:=g\gamma^{-1}=g_{\text{nlin}}g_{\text{lin}}\gamma^{-1}
. It is clear that
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.
Moreover, by (
8.33
),

\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}(\eta_{2}\circ g^{\prime})(n)=\eta_{2}(\Delta_{h_{2}}\Delta_{h_{1}}g^{\prime}(n))=0

for all
n,h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
So it is not hard to see that
g^{\prime}
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})
.
Finally, since
g^{\prime}=g\gamma^{-1}
, it follows from the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula that
g^{\prime}
is rational.
∎

We end this section with an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.1
, which will be used in
[
18
]
.

Corollary 8.6
.

Let
0<\delta<1/2,d,k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq r
, and
p\gg_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
be a prime. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form and
V+c
be an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension
r
. Suppose that
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})\geq s+13
. Let
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of dimension
m
, equipped with an
\frac{1}{\delta}
-rational Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
, and that
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
be a rational polynomial sequence. Let
\gamma\in G
be an element of complexity at most 1, and let
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})

be the map given by

g^{\prime}(n):=\gamma^{-1}g(n)\gamma
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, where
G^{\prime}_{i}:=\gamma^{-1}G_{i}\gamma
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Denote
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\gamma^{-1}\Gamma\gamma
.
If
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(V(M)\cap(V+c))}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
, then
(g^{\prime}(n)\Gamma^{\prime})_{n\in\iota^{-1}(V(M)\cap(V+c))}
is not
C^{-1}\delta^{C}
-equidistributed on
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
for some
C=C(d,m)\geq 1
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.1
, there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
with
0<\|\eta\|\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
such that
\eta\circ g^{\prime}=\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\iota^{-1}(V(M)\cap(V+c))
.
By Lemma A.13 of
[
8
]
,
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
is an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of dimension
m
, equipped with an
\frac{1}{\delta^{O(1)}}
-rational Mal’cev basis. Since
|\mathbb{E}_{n\in\iota^{-1}(V(M)\cap(V+c))}\exp(\eta(g^{\prime}(n)))|=1
, this implies that
(g^{\prime}(n)\Gamma^{\prime})_{n\in\iota^{-1}(V(M)\cap(V+c))}
is not
C^{-1}\delta^{C}
-equidistributed on
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
for some
C=C(d,m)\geq 1
.
∎

9.
Sets and equations generated by quadratic forms

9.1.
M
-sets

Our next goal is to extend Theorem
5.1
from
V(M)
to the intersection of the zeros of many quadratic polynomials. When working with a subset of
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
or
(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}
, it is convenient to group the variables
(n_{1},\dots,n_{dk})
into
k
groups

(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}),(n_{d+1},\dots,n_{2d}),\dots,(n_{d(k-1)+1},\dots,n_{k})

and treat each tuple
(n_{d(j-1)+1},\dots,n_{j})
as a single multi-dimensional variable. To this end, we introduce some definitions reflecting such a point of view.

We say that a linear transformation
L\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k^{\prime}}
is
d
-integral
if there exist
a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq k^{\prime}
such that

L(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})=\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i,1}n_{i},\dots,\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i,k^{\prime}}n_{i}\Bigr)

for all
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Let
L\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a
d
-integral linear transformation given by

L(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}n_{i}
for some
a_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p},1\leq i\leq k
. We say that
L
is the
d
-integral linear transformation
induced
by
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
.

Similarly, we say that a linear transformation
L\colon(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}\to(\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k^{\prime}}
is
d
-integral
if there exist
a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}/p
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq k^{\prime}
such that

L(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})=\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i,1}n_{i},\dots,\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i,k^{\prime}}n_{i}\Bigr)

for all
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

There is a natural correspondence between
d
-integral linear transformations in
\mathbb{Z}/p
and those in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
The proof of the following lemma is similar to the proof of Lemma
A.1
and we omit the details:

Lemma 9.1
.

Every
d
-integral linear transformation
\tilde{L}\colon(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}\to(\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k^{\prime}}
induces a
d
-integral linear transformation
L\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k^{\prime}}
. Conversely, every
d
-integral linear transformation
L\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k^{\prime}}
admits a regular lifting
\tilde{L}\colon(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}\to(\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k^{\prime}}
which is a
d
-integral linear transformation.

Moreover, we have that
p\tilde{L}\circ\tau\equiv\tau\circ L\mod p(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k^{\prime}}
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form with
A
being the associated matrix. We need to work with a large family of sets generated by
M
.
We say that a function
F\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is an
(M,k)
-integral quadratic function

if

(9.1)

F(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq k}b_{i,j}(n_{i}A)\cdot n_{j}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}v_{i}\cdot n_{i}+u

for some
b_{i,j},u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
v_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
We say that an
(M,k)
-integral quadratic function
F
is
pure

if
F
can be written in the form of (
9.1
) with
v_{1}=\dots=v_{k}=\mathbb{0}
.
We say that an
(M,k)
-integral quadratic function
F\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is
nice

if

F(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}}b_{i}(n_{k^{\prime}}A)\cdot n_{i}+u

for some
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
,
b_{i},u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

For
F
given in (
9.1
), denote

v_{M}(F):=(b_{k,k},b_{k,k-1},\dots,b_{k,1},v_{k},b_{k-1,k-1},\dots,b_{k-1,1},v_{k-1},\dots,b_{1,1},v_{1},u)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\binom{k+1}{2}+kd+1},

and

v^{\prime}_{M}(F):=(b_{k,k},b_{k,k-1},\dots,b_{k,1},v_{k},b_{k-1,k-1},\dots,b_{k-1,1},v_{k-1},\dots,b_{1,1},v_{1})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\binom{k+1}{2}+kd}.

Informally, we say that
b_{i,j}
is the
n_{i}n_{j}
-coefficient,
v_{i}
is the
n_{i}
-coefficient, and
u
is the constant term coefficient for these vectors.

Definition 9.2

(Different types of
(M,k)
-families)
.

An
(M,k)
-family
is a collections of
(M,k)
-integral quadratic functions.
Let
\mathcal{J}=\{F_{1},\dots,F_{r}\}
be an
(M,k)
-family.

•

We say that
\mathcal{J}
is
pure
if all of
F_{1},\dots,F_{r}
are pure.

•

We say that
\mathcal{J}
is
consistent
if
(0,\dots,0,1)
does not belong to the span of
v_{M}(F_{1}),

\dots,

v_{M}(F_{r})
, or equivalently, there is no linear combination of
F_{1},\dots,F_{r}
which is a constant nonzero function, or equivalently, for all
c_{1},\dots,c_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, we have

c_{1}v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{1})+\dots+c_{r}v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{r})=\mathbb{0}\Rightarrow c_{1}v_{M}(F_{1})+\dots+c_{r}v_{M}(F_{r})=\mathbb{0}.

•

We say that
\mathcal{J}
is
independent
if
v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{1}),\dots,v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{r})
are linearly independent, or equivalently, there is no nontrivial linear combination of
F_{1},\dots,F_{r}
which is a constant function, or equivalently, for all
c_{1},\dots,c_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, we have

c_{1}v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{1})+\dots+c_{r}v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{r})=\mathbb{0}\Rightarrow c_{1}=\dots=c_{r}=0.

•

We say that
\mathcal{J}
is
nice
if there exist some bijective
d
-integral linear transformation
L\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
and some
v\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
such that
F_{i}(L(\cdot)+v)
is nice for all
1\leq i\leq r
.

The dimension of the span of
v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{1}),\dots,v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{r})
is called the
dimension
of an
(M,k)
-family
\{F_{1},\dots,F_{r}\}
.

When there is no confusion, we call an
(M,k)
-family to be an
M
-family
for short.

We say that a subset
\Omega
of
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
is an
M
-set
if there exists an
(M,k)
-family
\{F_{i}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon 1\leq i\leq r\}

such that
\Omega=\cap_{i=1}^{r}V(F_{i})
.
We call either
\{F_{1},\dots,F_{r}\}
or the ordered set
(F_{1},\dots,F_{r})
an
M
-representation
of
\Omega
.

Let
\mathbb{P}\in\{\text{pure, nice, independent, consistent}\}
.
We say that
\Omega
is
\mathbb{P}
if one can choose the
M
-family
\{F_{1},\dots,F_{r}\}
to be
\mathbb{P}
.
We say that the
M
-representation
(F_{1},\dots,F_{r})
is
\mathbb{P}
if
\{F_{i}\colon 1\leq i\leq r\}
is
\mathbb{P}
.
We say that
r
is the
dimension
of the
M
-representation
(F_{1},\dots,F_{r})
.
The
total co-dimension
of a consistent
M
-set
\Omega
, denoted by
r_{M}(\Omega)
, is the minimum of the dimension of the independent
M
-representations of
\Omega
.
19
19

19

We will show in Proposition
C.8
that
r_{M}(\Omega)
is independent of the choice of the independent
M
-representation if
d
and
p
are sufficiently large.

We say that an independent
M
-representation
(F_{1},\dots,F_{r})
of
\Omega
is
standard
if
the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}v_{M}(F_{1})\\
\dots\\
v_{M}(F_{r})\end{bmatrix}
is in the reduced row echelon form (or equivalently, the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{1})\\
\dots\\
v^{\prime}_{M}(F_{r})\end{bmatrix}
is in the reduced row echelon form).
If
(F_{1},\dots,F_{r})
is a standard
M
-representation of
\Omega
, then we may relabeling
(F_{1},\dots,F_{r})
as

(F_{k,1},\dots,F_{k,r_{k}},F_{k-1,1},\dots,F_{k-1,r_{k-1}},\dots,F_{1,1},\dots,F_{1,r_{1}})

for some
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
F_{i,j}
is non-constant with respect to
n_{i}
and is independent of
n_{i+1},\dots,n_{k}
.
We also call
(F_{i,j}\colon 1\leq i\leq k,1\leq j\leq r_{i})
a
standard
M
-representation
of
\Omega
. The vector
(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})
is called the
dimension vector
of this representation.

Convention 9.3
.

We allow
(M,k)
-families,
M
-representations and dimension vectors to be empty. In particular,
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
is considered as a nice and consistent
M
-set with total co-dimension zero.

Example 9.4
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
, and
h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Then the set
V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{s}}
defined in Definition
4.15
is an
M
-set with a dimension vector
(s+1)
. Moreover, it is consistent if
h_{1}A,\dots,h_{s}A
are linearly independent. On the other hand,
V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{s}}
is not a nice
M
-set.

Example 9.5
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
.
It follows from Lemma
4.18
that the Gowers set
\Box_{s}(V(M))
is a consistent
M
-set with a dimension vector
(1,1,\dots,s)
, whose total co-dimension is
(s^{2}+s+2)/2
.
On the other hand, let
L(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s}):=(h_{1},\dots,h_{s},n)
. Then it follows from Lemma
4.18
that
L(\Box_{s}(V(M)))
is a consistent
M
-set with a dimension vector
(0,\dots,s-1,s+1)
, whose total co-dimension is also
(s^{2}+s+2)/2
. We will show later in Example
B.4
that these
M
-sets are nice when
M
is non-degenerate.

We refer the readers to Appendix
B
for some basic properties as well as more examples of
M
-sets, and to Appendix
C
for the Fubini’s theorem on
M
-sets (which will be used extensively in
[
18
,
19
]
).

9.2.
Irreducible properties for sets generated by quadratic forms

In Section
10
, we will boost Theorem
5.1
to cover partially
p
-periodic polynomials on a large class of
M
-sets. Before generalizing Theorem
5.1
, we need to address a technical issue on the irreducible property for
M
-sets: if
P
is a polynomial whose set of solutions has a “large” intersection with some
M
-set
\Omega
, then does it imply that all the elements in
\Omega
are solutions to
P
?
To answer this question, we start with the following definition:

Definition 9.6

(
\delta
-irreducible sets)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N},\delta>0
and
p
be a prime. We say that a subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
if for all
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s
, either
|V(P)\cap\Omega|\leq\delta|\Omega|
or
\Omega\subseteq V(P)
.

We say that a
p
-periodic subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is
weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
if for all
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
of degree at most
s
,
either
|V_{p}(P)\cap\Omega\cap[p]^{d}|\leq\delta|\Omega\cap[p]^{d}|
or

\Omega\subseteq V_{p}(P).

We say that
\Omega
is
strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
if for all
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})
of degree at most
s
, either

|V_{p}(P)\cap\Omega\cap[p]^{d}|\leq\delta|\Omega\cap[p]^{d}|
or
\Omega\subseteq V_{p}(P).

Remark 9.7
.

If
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
is a
1/2
-irreducible algebraic set, then it is not hard to see that for any
P,Q\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
, if
\Omega\subseteq V(P)\cup V(Q)
, then either
\Omega\subseteq V(P)
or
\Omega\subseteq V(Q)
. In the language of algebraic geometry, this means that
\Omega
is an
irreducible algebraic set
(or an
algebraic variety
).

The following basic proposition explains the connections between different notions of irreducibility.

Proposition 9.8

(Connections between notions of irreduciblities)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N},\delta>0
and
p
be a prime with
p>s
.

(1)

If
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then
\tau(\Omega)\subseteq[p]^{k}
is
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

(2)

Conversely, if a
p
-periodic subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then
\iota(\Omega)\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

(3)

If a
p
-periodic subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then it is also weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from the fact that for every
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
of degree at most
s
, we have
\iota\circ pP\circ\tau\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
and

V_{p}(P)\cap\tau(\Omega)=\tau(V(\iota\circ pP\circ\tau)\cap\Omega)
.
Part (ii) follows from the fact that for every
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
, there exists a regular lifting
\tilde{P}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
of
P
with
\tilde{P}=\frac{1}{p}\tau(P\circ\iota)\mod\mathbb{Z}
and
V(P)\cap\iota(\Omega)=\iota(V_{p}(\tilde{P})\cap\Omega)
.
Part (iii) follows from the inclusion
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)\subseteq\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})
.
∎

It is not hard to see that the full set
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
\delta
-irreducible. To be more precise, we have

Proposition 9.9

(
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is irreducible)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N},\delta>0
and
p
be a prime. If
p\gg_{d,s}\delta^{-O_{d,s}(1)}
, then

(1)

\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
;

(2)

\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
;

(3)

\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from Lemma
4.10
. Part (ii) follows from Part (i) and Proposition
9.8
.
We now prove Part (iii). The idea is to use Part (ii) and the
p
-expansion trick.
Let
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})
be of degree at most
s
with
|V_{p}(g)\cap[p]^{d}|>\delta p^{d}
.
By the multivariate polynomial interpolation, there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
such that

Qg=\sum_{i=0}^{s}\frac{g_{i}}{p^{i}}

for some integer valued polynomials
g_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}
of degree at most
s
.
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be the smallest integer such that there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
with

Qg=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{g_{i}}{p^{i}}

for some integer valued polynomials
g_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}
. Obviously such
k
exists and is at most
s
. Our goal is to show that
k=0
.

Suppose that

k>0
.
For all
n\in V_{p}(g)
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, by definition
g(n+pm)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, we must have that
g_{k}(n+pm)\in p\mathbb{Z}
.
So
|V_{p}(\frac{1}{p}g_{k})\cap[p]^{d}|\geq|V_{p}(g)\cap[p]^{d}|>\delta p^{d}
. By Part (ii), we have that
V_{p}(\frac{1}{p}g_{k})\supseteq[p]^{d}
and thus
\frac{1}{p}g_{k}(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Then there exists
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q^{\prime}
such that

\frac{Q^{\prime}}{p}g_{k}
and thus
Q^{\prime}(g_{k-1}+\frac{1}{p}g_{k})
is an integer valued polynomial, a contradiction to the minimality of
k
.

We have thus proved that
k=0
and thus
Qg
itself is integer valued for some
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
. Then for all
n\in[p]^{d}
, we have that
g(n)\in\mathbb{Z}/Q
. Since
g
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}/p^{s}
, we have that
g(n)\in(\mathbb{Z}/Q)\cap(\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})=\mathbb{Z}
. So
[p]^{d}
is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
6.1
, we have:

Proposition 9.10

(
V(M)
is irreducible)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N},\delta>0
and
p
be a prime. If
p\gg_{d,s}\delta^{-O_{d,s}(1)}
, then

(1)

for every quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
of rank at least 3, the set
V(M)
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
;

(2)

for every quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
of
p
-rank at least 3, the set
V_{p}(M)
is weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
;

(3)

for every quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
of
p
-rank at least 3, the set
V_{p}(M)
is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from Lemma
6.1
. Part (ii) follows from Part (i) and Proposition
9.8
.
We now prove Part (iii) using Part (ii) and the
p
-expansion trick. The proof is very similar to that of Proposition
7.7
.
Let
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})
be of degree at most
s
with
|V_{p}(g)\cap V_{p}(M)\cap[p]^{d}|>\delta|V_{p}(M)\cap[p]^{d}|
.
By the multivariate polynomial interpolation, there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
such that

Qg=\sum_{i=0}^{s}\frac{g_{i}}{p^{i}}

for some integer valued polynomials
g_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}
of degree at most
s
.
We say that a polynomial
f
is
good
if

f=\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor s/2\rfloor}M^{i}R_{i}

for some integer valued polynomial
R_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}
of degree at most
s-2i
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be the smallest integer such that there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
with

Qg=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{g_{i}}{p^{i}}

for some good polynomials
g_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}
. Obviously such
k
exists and is at most
s
. Our goal is to show that
k=0
.

Suppose that

k>0
.
For all
n\in V_{p}(g)\cap V_{p}(M)
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, by definition
g(n+pm)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, we must have that
g_{k}(n+pm)\in p\mathbb{Z}
.
Suppose that

g_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor s/2\rfloor}M^{i}R_{i}

for some integer valued polynomials
R_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}
of degree at most
s-2i
.
Assume that

M(n)=\frac{1}{p}((nA)\cdot n+u\cdot n+v)

for some symmetric integer valued
d\times d
matrix
A
, some
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and some
v\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then for all
n\in V_{p}(g)\cap V_{p}(M)
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, writing
x:=M(n)+(2(nA)+u)\cdot m
, we have

(9.2)

\begin{split}&\quad 0\equiv g_{k}(n+pm)\equiv\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor s/2\rfloor}M(n+pm)^{i}R_{i}(n)\\
&\equiv\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor s/2\rfloor}(M(n)+(2(nA)+u)\cdot m)^{i}R_{i}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor s/2\rfloor}x^{i}R_{i}(n)\mod p\mathbb{Z}.\end{split}

Note that if
2(nA)+u\notin p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, then for any
x\in[p]
, there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
M(n)+(2(nA)+u)\cdot m\equiv x\mod p\mathbb{Z}
. So viewing the right hand side of (
9.2
) as a polynomial of
x
, we have that

R_{i}(n)\in p\mathbb{Z}/Q^{\prime}
for some
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q^{\prime}
for all
0\leq i\leq\lfloor s/2\rfloor
and
n\in V_{p}(g)\cap V_{p}(M)
with
2(nA)+u\notin p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Since
\text{rank}_{p}(M)\geq 3
, it is not hard to see that the number of
n\in[p]^{d}
with

2(nA)+u\in p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is at most
p^{d-3}
.
So

|V_{p}(Q^{\prime}R_{i})\cap V_{p}(M)\cap[p]^{d}|\geq|V_{p}(g)\cap V_{p}(M)\cap[p]^{d}|-p^{d-3}>\delta|V_{p}(M)\cap[p]^{d}|/2.

By Part (ii), we have that
V_{p}(M)\subseteq V_{p}(Q^{\prime}R_{i})
for all
0\leq i\leq\lfloor s/2\rfloor
.
Let
Q^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N},Q^{\prime}|Q^{\prime\prime},p\nmid Q^{\prime\prime}
be such that
Q^{\prime\prime}R_{i}
is an integer coefficient polynomial for all
0\leq i\leq\lfloor s/2\rfloor
.
We may invoke Corollary
7.3
to conclude that

\frac{Q^{\prime\prime}}{p}R_{i}=MR^{\prime}_{i}+R^{\prime\prime}_{i}

for some integer valued polynomials
R_{i},R^{\prime\prime}_{i}
of degrees at most
s-2i-2
and
s-2i
respectively. Then

\frac{Q^{\prime\prime}}{p}g_{k}
and thus
Q^{\prime\prime}(g_{k-1}+\frac{1}{p}g_{k})
is good, a contradiction to the minimality of
k
.

We have thus proved that
k=0
and thus
Qg
itself is good for some
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
. Then for all
n\in V_{p}(M)
, we have that
g(n)\in\mathbb{Z}/Q
. Since
g
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}/p^{s}
, we have that
g(n)\in(\mathbb{Z}/Q)\cap(\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})=\mathbb{Z}
and thus
V_{p}(M)\subseteq V_{p}(g)
. So
V_{p}(M)
is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.
∎

We provide some basic propositions for the irreducible properties. Firstly, it is straightforward that the irreducible properties are preserved under translations.

Proposition 9.11

(Irreducible sets are translation invariant)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N},\delta>0
and
p>s
be a prime.

(1)

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
and
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Then
\Omega+v
is also
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

(2)

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be a
p
-periodic set which is weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
and
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Then
\Omega+v
is also weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

(3)

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be a
p
-periodic set which is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
and
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Then
\Omega+v
is also strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

Proof.

Let
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
such that
|V(P)\cap(\Omega+v)|\geq\delta|\Omega+v|=\delta|\Omega|
. Then
|V(P(\cdot-v))\cap\Omega|\geq\delta|\Omega|
. Since
\Omega
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
, we have that
V(P(\cdot-v))\subseteq\Omega
and thus
V(P)\subseteq\Omega+v
. This proves Part (i).

The proofs of Parts (ii) and (iii) are similar to Part (i) and we leave them to the interested readers.
∎

Moreover, the irreducible properties are preserved under products.

Proposition 9.12

(Products of irreducible sets are irreducible)
.

Let
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N},\delta>0
and
p>s
be a prime. Let
\Omega_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{1}}
,
\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{2}}
and
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
.

(1)

If
\Omega_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{1}}
and
\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{2}}
are
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
is
2\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

(2)

If
\Omega_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d_{1}}
and
\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d_{2}}
are
p
-periodic sets which are weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
is weakly
(2\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

(3)

If
\Omega_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d_{1}}
and
\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d_{2}}
are
p
-periodic sets which are strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
is strongly
(2\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

Proof.

Let
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
such that
|V(P)\cap(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})|\geq 2\delta|\Omega_{1}|\cdot|\Omega_{2}|
. By the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists a subset
U
of
\Omega_{2}
with
|U|\geq\delta|\Omega_{2}|
such that for all
y\in U
,
|V(P(\cdot,y))\cap\Omega_{1}|=|V(P)\cap(\Omega_{1}\times\{y\})|\geq\delta|\Omega_{1}|
. Since
\Omega_{1}
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
, we have that
\Omega_{1}\subseteq V(P(\cdot,y))
for all
y\in U
. In other words, for all
x\in\Omega_{1}
,
|V(P(x,\cdot))\cap\Omega_{2}|\geq|U|\geq\delta|\Omega_{2}|
. Since
\Omega_{2}
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
, we have that
\Omega_{2}\subseteq V(P(x,\cdot))
for all
x\in\Omega_{1}
. In other words,
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\subseteq V(P)
. This proves Part (i).

The proofs of Parts (ii) and (iii) are similar to Part (i) and we leave them to the interested readers.
∎

Finally, the irreducible properties are preserved under isomorphisms.

Proposition 9.13

(Irreducible sets are invariant under isomorphisms)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N},\delta>0
and
p>s
be a prime.
Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
and
L\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
be a bijective linear transformation.

(1)

If
\Omega
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then so is
L(\Omega)
.

(2)

If
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
is weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then so is
\iota^{-1}(L(\Omega))
.

(3)

If
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
, then so is
\iota^{-1}(L(\Omega))
.

Proof.

We first prove Part (i).
Let
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with

|V(P\circ L)\cap\Omega|=|V(P)\cap L(\Omega)|\geq\delta|L(\Omega)|=\delta|\Omega|.

Clearly
P\circ L\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a polynomial of degree at most
s
. Since
\Omega
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
, we have that
\Omega\subseteq V(P\circ L)
and so
L(\Omega)\subseteq V(P)
. So
L(\Omega)
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

We now prove Part (ii).
Let
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with
|V_{p}(P)\cap\iota^{-1}\circ L(\Omega)\cap[p]^{k}|\geq\delta|\iota^{-1}\circ L(\Omega)\cap[p]^{k}|=\delta|\Omega|
. By Lemma
9.1
, there exists a linear transformation
\tilde{L}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
\tilde{L}\circ\tau\equiv\tau\circ L\mod p\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
Since
L
is bijective,
\tilde{L}
maps different residue classes mod
p\mathbb{Z}^{k}
to different residue classes mod
p\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
So

|V_{p}(P)\cap\iota^{-1}\circ L(\Omega)\cap[p]^{k}|=|V_{p}(P)\cap\tilde{L}\circ\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\cap[p]^{k}|=|V_{p}(P\circ\tilde{L})\cap\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\cap[p]^{k}|.

It is clear that
P\circ\tilde{L}
is a polynomial belonging to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
of degree at most
s
. Since
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
is weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
, we have that
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\subseteq V_{p}(P\circ\tilde{L})
and thus
\iota^{-1}\circ L(\Omega)\subseteq V_{p}(P)
since
P\circ\tilde{L}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
. So
\iota^{-1}\circ L(\Omega)
is weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

Note that if
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})
is a polynomial of degree at most
s
, then so is
P\circ\tilde{L}
.
The rest proof of Part (iii) is almost identical to that of Part (ii) and we omit the details.
∎

We are now ready to state the main irreducible theorem for
M
-sets:

Theorem 9.14

(Nice and consistent
M
-sets are irreducible)
.

Let
d,K\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
,
p
be a prime, and
\delta>0
. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form and
\Omega\subseteq(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{K}
be a nice and consistent
M
-set. If
d\geq\max\{2r_{M}(\Omega)+1,4K-1\}
and
p\gg_{d,s}\delta^{-O_{d,s}(1)}
, then

(1)

\Omega
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
;

(2)

\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
is weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
;

(3)

\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

The proof of Theorem
9.14
is rather complicated. We postpone the proof of Theorem
9.14
to Appendix
D
to prevent the readers from being distracted by technical details.

Remark 9.15
.

The method we use to prove Theorem
9.14
can be adapted to show that all consistent
M
-sets are irreducible in all the three senses. However, we do not need such a general result in this paper.

10.
Leibman dichotomy

In this section, we extend Theorem
5.1
to all nice and consistent
M
-sets. For convenience, we introduce the following definition:

Definition 10.1

(
(\delta,K)
-Leibman dichotomy)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
,
C,\delta,K>0
,
p
be a prime, and
\Omega
be a non-empty
p
-periodic subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. We say that
\Omega
admits a
rational (resp. partially periodic)
(\delta,K,p)
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C

if for any
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold
G/\Gamma
of degree at most
s
and complexity at most
C
, and any
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
which is rational (resp. belongs to
\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
), either
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
is
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
,
or there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
with
0<\|\eta\|\leq K
such that
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega
.

We say that a non-empty subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
admits a
rational (resp. partially periodic)
(\delta,K)
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
if
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
admits a rational (resp. partially periodic)
(\delta,K,p)
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
.

We remark that for a non-empty subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, an equivalent way of saying that
\Omega
admits a partially periodic
(\delta,K)
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
is that for any any
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold
G/\Gamma
of degree at most
s
and complexity at most
C
, and any
g\in\text{poly}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
, either
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
is
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
(meaning that
(g\circ\tau(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
),
or there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
with
0<\|\eta\|\leq K
such that
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega
.

We may deduce the following Leibman dichotomy from the main result of
[
8
]
:

Theorem 10.2

(
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
admits a Leibman dichotomy)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
,
C>0
and
p
be a prime. There exists
K=O_{C,d,s}(1)
such that for all
0<\delta<1/2
,
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
admits a rational
(\delta,K\delta^{-K})
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
.

Proof.

Let
\delta>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
be rational. By Corollary
3.21
,
g
is
Q
-periodic for some
Q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
Suppose that the sequence
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
. Since
g
is
Q
-periodic, we have that
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in[QN]^{d}}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. By Theorem 8.6 of
[
8
]
, there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
with
0<\|\eta\|\leq O_{C,d,s}(\delta^{-O_{C,d,s}(1)})
such that
\|\eta\circ g\|_{C^{\infty}[QN]^{d}}\ll_{C,d,s}\delta^{-O_{C,d,s}(1)}
(see Definition 8.2 of
[
8
]
for the definition of the smoothness norm
\|\cdot\|_{C^{\infty}[N]^{d}}
). Letting
N\to\infty
, we have that
\|\eta\circ g\|_{C^{\infty}[QN]^{d}}=0
if
N
is sufficiently large and thus
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
[QN]^{d}
. Since
g
is
Q
-periodic, this implies that
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
∎

We may also rephrase Theorem
5.1
using the Leibman dichotomy as below:

Theorem 10.3

(
V(M)
admits a Leibman dichotomy)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},r,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
C>0
and
p
be a prime. There exists
K=O_{C,d}(1)
such that for any affine subspace
V+c
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension
r
, any quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
with
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})\geq s+13
, and any
0<\delta<1/2
,
if
p\gg_{C,d}\delta^{-O_{C,d}(1)}
,
then
V(M)\cap(V+c)
admits a rational
(\delta,K\delta^{-K})
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
.
20
20

20

It follows from Corollary
4.14
that
V(M)\cap(V+c)
is non-empty.

Remark 10.4
.

By Lemma
3.18
, it is not hard to see that if
\Omega
admits a rational
(\delta,K,p)
-(or
(\delta,K)
-) Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
, then
\Omega
also admits a partially periodic
(\delta,K,p)
-(or
(\delta,K)
-) Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
O(C)
. So Theorems
10.2
and
10.3
also hold for partially periodic Leibman dichotomies.

We now turn to sets which admits partially periodic Leibman dichotomies.
The following lemma is a straightforward consequence of the definition of the Leibman dichotomy and Proposition
3.24
. Its proof is left to the interested readers.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta,K>0
,
p
be a prime,
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be non-empty,
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
L\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a bijective linear transformation. If
\Omega
admits a partially periodic
(\delta,K)
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
,
then so does
L(\Omega)+v
.

We are now ready to state the following partial generalization of Theorem
5.1
for nice and consistent
M
-sets:

Theorem 10.6

(Nice and consistent
M
-sets admit Leibman dichotomies)
.

Let
d,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq\max\{4r+1,4k+3,2k+s+11\}
,
C>0
and
p
be a prime.
There exists
K:=O_{C,d}(1)
such that for any non-degenerate quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
, any nice and consistent
M
-set
\Omega\subseteq(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
of total co-dimension
r
, and any
0<\delta<1/2
, if
p\gg_{C,d}\delta^{-O_{C,d}(1)}
,
then
\Omega
admits a partially periodic
(\delta,K\delta^{-K})
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
.
21
21

21

It follows from Theorem
C.3
that
\Omega
is non-empty.

It is an interesting question to ask if Theorem
10.6
holds for rational Leibman dichotomies. However, we do not need such a strong statement in later parts of the series.

The proof of Theorem
10.6
is rather complicated. We postpone the proof of Theorem
10.6
to Appendix
E
to prevent the readers from being distracted by technical details.

11.
Factorization theorems

With all the preparations in the previous sections,
we are now ready to derive the factorization theorem for partially
p
-periodic polynomial sequences
g
on a set
\Omega
admitting a Leibman dichotomy. We show that
g
can be written as the product of a small constant term
\epsilon
, a polynomial sequence
g^{\prime}
which is sufficiently well equidistributed on a subnilmanifold, and a polynomial sequence
\gamma
which has a small period on the set
\Omega
.

We provide two sets of factorization results. The first set is provided in Section
11.1
and is designed for
[
19
]
, which deals with partially periodic polynomial sequences for a large family of nice and consistent
M
-sets
\Omega
. The second set is provided in Section
11.2
and is designed for
[
18
]
, which deals with rational polynomial sequences but for a more restricted family of sets
\Omega
.

11.1.
Factorization theorems of partially periodic sequences

To begin with, we prove a weak factorization theorem for partially periodic polynomial sequences, where
g^{\prime}
is only required to take values on a subnilmanifold.
For a nilmanifold
G/\Gamma
with filtration
(G_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, the
total dimension
of
G/\Gamma
is the quantity
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\dim(G_{i})
.

Proposition 11.1

(Weak factorization property I)
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
C,\delta,K>0
and
p\gg_{C,d,K,s}1
be a prime. Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a non-empty set admitting a partially periodic
(\delta,K)
-Leibman dichotomy up to degree
s
and complexity
C
. Let
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of complexity at most
C
, equipped with a
C
-rational Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
, and let
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. If
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
,
then we may factorize
g
as

g(n)=\epsilon g^{\prime}(n)\gamma(n)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

where
\epsilon\in G
of complexity at most 1,

g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma^{\prime})
,
g^{\prime}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
for some sub-nilmanifold
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
of
G/\Gamma
whose Mal’cev basis is
O_{C,d}(K)
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
and whose total dimension is smaller than that of
G/\Gamma
, and
\gamma\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
with
\gamma(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
.

Proof.

The proof uses ideas from Proposition 9.2 of Green and Tao’s work
[
8
]
and the work of Candela and Sisask
[
1
]
. In our case, the constructions of the sequences
g^{\prime}
and
\gamma
are more intricate, as we need them to satisfy certain partial periodic conditions.

Throughout the proof we assume that
p\gg_{C,d,K,s}1
.
Denote
m_{i}:=\dim(G_{i})
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
m:=\dim(G)
.
Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\}
be the Mal’cev basis of
G/\Gamma
.
Write
g:=\{g(\mathbb{0})\}g_{\ast}[g(\mathbb{0})]
. Then
g_{\ast}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. So replacing
g
by
g_{\ast}
if necessary,
we may assume without loss of generality that
g(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
.

If
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
, then since
\Omega
admits a partially periodic
(\delta,K)
-Leibman dichotomy, there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
with
0<\|\eta\|\leq K
such that
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
.
We may assume that
g=\tilde{g}\circ\tau
, for some
\tilde{g}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. Since
g(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
, we may use the type-I Taylor expansion to write
g
as

g(n)=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s}g_{i}^{\binom{n}{i}}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

for some
g_{i}\in G_{|i|}
. Let
G^{\prime}
be the connected component of
\ker(\eta)
. Then
G^{\prime}
is a subgroup of
G
which is
O_{C,d}(K)
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
. Write

\psi(g(n))=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s}t_{i}\binom{n}{i}

for some
t_{i}\in\{0\}^{m-m_{|i|}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{|i|}}
.
The type-I horizontal character
\eta
is given in coordinates by

\eta\circ g(n)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s}k\cdot t_{i}\binom{n}{i}

for some
k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
of complexity at most
O_{C,d}(K)
.
Let
0\leq s^{\prime}\leq s
be the largest integer such that at least one of the entries
k_{m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1},\dots,k_{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}
is nonzero (where we denote
m_{0}:=m
). Since

\eta
is nontrivial, such an
s^{\prime}
always exists.
Denote

m^{\prime}:=m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}
.
Then we may write
k=(k^{\prime},\mathbb{0})
for some
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m^{\prime}}
. Write
t_{i}=(t^{\prime}_{i},t^{\prime\prime}_{i})
for some
t^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}
and
t^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-m^{\prime}}
.
Since the first
m-m_{|i|}
entries of
t_{i}
are zero, and the last
m_{s^{\prime}+1}
entries of
k
are zero, we have that
k\cdot t_{i}=0
if
|i|\geq s^{\prime}+1
.
So

\eta\circ g(n)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s}k^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime}_{i}\binom{n}{i}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s^{\prime}}k^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime}_{i}\binom{n}{i}.

Since
k
is of complexity at most
O_{C,d}(K)
, there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N}_{+},Q\leq O_{C,\delta,d,K}(1)
and
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
whose only nonzero entries are
u_{m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1},\dots,u_{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}
such that

k\cdot u=Q.
Denote

\gamma_{0}(n):=\prod_{i=m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1}^{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}\exp(Q^{\ast}u_{i}\eta(g(n))X_{i}),

where
Q^{\ast}
is any integer such that
Q^{\ast}Q\equiv 1\mod p^{s}\mathbb{Z}
. Clearly
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
.
Since
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, we have that
\eta\circ g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{Z})
by Lemma
3.27
. This implies that

\gamma_{0}\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

Since
\Omega
is non-empty, by Lemma
3.28
, we have that
p^{s}\eta\circ g(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
On the other hand, writing
h(n):=g(n)\gamma_{0}(n)^{-1}
we have that
h\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
by Corollary B.4 of
[
11
]
and that

\eta\circ h(n)=\eta\circ g(n)-Q^{\ast}(k\cdot u)\eta\circ g(n)=(1-Q^{\ast}Q)\eta\circ g(n),

which is an integer valued polynomial. Since
\gamma_{0}\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
and
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, it follows from Lemma
3.17
that
h\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. By Proposition
3.29
,
there exists
\gamma^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
with
\gamma^{\prime}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
such that
\eta\circ h=\eta\circ\gamma^{\prime}
.

Setting
\gamma:=\gamma_{0}\gamma^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}:=g\gamma^{-1}
, it is not hard to see that
\gamma\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
and
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.
Moreover, since
g(\mathbb{0})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{0})=\gamma^{\prime}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
, we have that
g^{\prime}(\mathbb{0})=\gamma(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
.
Note that

\eta\circ g^{\prime}(n)=\eta\circ g(n)-\eta\circ\gamma_{0}(n)-\eta\circ\gamma^{\prime}(n)=\eta\circ h(n)-\eta\circ\gamma^{\prime}(n)=0.

So
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})
.
Finally, for all
n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have that

g^{\prime}(n+pm)^{-1}g^{\prime}(n)=\gamma(n+pm)g(n+pm)^{-1}g(n)\gamma(n)^{-1}.

Since all of
\gamma(n+pm)
,
g(n+pm)^{-1}g(n)
and
\gamma(n)^{-1}
belong to
\Gamma
, we have that
g^{\prime}(n+pm)^{-1}g^{\prime}(n)\in\Gamma
. Therefore, we have that
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma^{\prime})
. We are done since
g=g^{\prime}\gamma
.
∎

By repeatedly using Proposition
11.1
, we get the following strong factorization property:

Theorem 11.2

(Strong factorization property I)
.

Let
d,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},r,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
C>0
,
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a growth function,
p\gg_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}1
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form, and
\Omega\subseteq(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
satisfying one of the following assumptions:

(1)

r=0
and
\Omega=(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
;

(2)

k=1,r=0
and
\Omega=V(M)\cap(V+c)
for some affine subspace
V+c
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})\geq s+13
;

(3)

M
is non-degenerate,
d\geq\max\{4r+1,4k+3,2k+s+11\}
and
\Omega
is a nice and consistent
M
-set of total co-dimension
r
.

Let
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of complexity at most
C
, and let
g
be a polynomial sequence in
\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. There exist some
C\leq C^{\prime}\leq O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}(1)
,
a proper subgroup
G^{\prime}
of
G
which is
C^{\prime}
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
, and
a factorization

g(n)=\epsilon g^{\prime}(n)\gamma(n)\text{ for all }n\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}

such that
\epsilon\in G
is of complexity
O_{C^{\prime}}(1)
,

g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma^{\prime})
,
g^{\prime}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
and
(g^{\prime}(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is
\mathcal{F}(C^{\prime})^{-1}
-equidistributed on
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}\cap\Gamma
, and that
\gamma\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
,
\gamma(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
.

Proof.

The method is similar to the proof of Theorem 1.19 of
[
8
]
. Throughout the proof, we assume that
p\gg_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}1
. Enlarging
C
if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{F}(C)>2
.
By Theorem
10.2
,
10.3
or
10.6
,
\Omega
is a non-empty set which admits a partially periodic
(\mathcal{F}(C)^{-1},O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}(1))
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
.
So by Proposition
11.1
, either
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is
\mathcal{F}(C)^{-1}
-equidistributed, or we may write

g(n)=\epsilon_{1}g_{1}(n)\gamma_{1}(n)\text{ for all }n\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}

such that
\epsilon_{1}\in G
is of complexity at most 1,

g_{1}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to(G_{1})_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma_{1})
,
g_{1}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
for some proper subgroup
G_{1}
of
G
which is
O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}(1)
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
and whose total dimension is less than that of
G
, where
\Gamma_{1}:=G_{1}\cap\Gamma
, and that
\gamma_{1}\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
,
\gamma_{1}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
.
By Lemma A.10 of
[
8
]
,
G_{1}/(G_{1}\cap\Gamma)
admits a
C_{1}
-rational Mal’cev basis for some
C_{1}=O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}(1)
.

By Theorem
10.2
,
10.3
or
10.6
,
\Omega
admits a partially periodic
(\mathcal{F}(C_{1})^{-1},O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}(1))
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C_{1}
. So
by Proposition
11.1
, either

(g_{1}(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is
\mathcal{F}(C_{1})^{-1}
-equidistributed on
G_{1}/\Gamma_{1}
, or we may write

g_{1}(n)=\epsilon_{2}g_{2}(n)\gamma_{2}(n)\text{ for all }n\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}

such that
\epsilon_{2}\in G_{1}
is of complexity at most 1,

g_{2}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to(G_{2})_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma_{2})
,
g_{2}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
for some proper subgroup
G_{2}
of
G_{1}
which is
O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}(1)
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
and whose total dimension is less than that of
G_{1}
, where
\Gamma_{2}:=G_{2}\cap\Gamma
, and that
\gamma_{2}\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
,
\gamma_{2}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
.
Since
\epsilon_{2}\in G_{1}
is of complexity at most 1, we have that
\epsilon_{2}
is of complexity
O_{C_{1}}(1)
as an element of
G
. By Lemma A.10 of
[
8
]
,
G_{2}/(G_{2}\cap\Gamma)
admits a
C_{2}
-rational Mal’cev basis for some
C_{2}=O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}(1)
. We may also require that
C_{2}\geq C_{1}
.

We may continue this process to get a sequence
C\leq C_{1}\leq C_{2}\leq\dots
. Since this iteration will terminate after at most
O_{C}(1)
steps, we can find
1\leq t\leq O_{C}(1)
, some
C\leq C_{t}\leq O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},k,r,s}(1)
, and a factorization

g(n)=\epsilon_{1}\dots\epsilon_{t}g_{t}(n)\gamma_{1}(n)\dots\gamma_{t}(n)\text{ for all }n\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}

\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{t}\in G
are of complexities at most
O_{C_{t}}(1)
,

g_{t}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to(G_{t})_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma_{t})
,
g_{t}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
for some proper subgroup
G_{t}
of
G
which is
C_{t}
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
and
(g_{t}(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is
\mathcal{F}(C_{t})^{-1}
-equidistributed on
G_{t}/\Gamma_{t}
, where
\Gamma_{t}:=G_{t}\cap\Gamma
, and that
\gamma_{i}\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
,
\gamma_{i}(\mathbb{0})=id_{G}
for
1\leq i\leq t
. We are done by setting
C=C_{t}
,
\epsilon=\epsilon_{1}\dots\epsilon_{t}
,
g^{\prime}=g_{t}
and
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\dots\gamma_{t}
(since it is not hard to see from Lemma A.3 of
[
8
]
that
\epsilon
is of complexity
O_{t,C_{t}}(1)=O_{C_{t}}(1)
).
∎

Remark 11.3
.

It is tempting to conjecture that in Theorems
11.2
, if
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
(i.e. if
g
is
p
-periodic), then one can further require
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma^{\prime})
. However, we were unable to prove it. The difficulty is that even if we start with some
p
-periodic
g
, the polynomial sequence
g^{\prime}
obtained in Proposition
11.1
is not necessarily
p
-periodic (we can only make
g^{\prime}
to be partially
p
-periodic on
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
). This is a major reason why we need to prove most of the results in this paper for the partially
p
-periodic polynomials.

11.2.
Factorization theorems of rational sequences

In this section, we present factorization theorems for rationa polynomial sequences. Again we start with a weak factorization property:

Proposition 11.4

(Weak factorization property II)
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
C,\delta,K>0
and
p\gg_{C,d,K,s}1
be a prime. Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a non-empty set such that
k\Omega+u
admits a rational
(\delta,K)
-Leibman dichotomy up to degree
s
and complexity
C
for all
k\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}
and
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, and let
n_{\ast}\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
. Let
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of complexity at most
C
, equipped with a
C
-rational Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
, and let
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
be rational. If
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is not
\delta
-totally equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
,
then we may factorize
g
as

g(n)=\epsilon g^{\prime}(n)\gamma(n)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

where
\epsilon\in G
is of complexity at most 1,

g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})
is rational for some sub-nilmanifold
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
of
G/\Gamma
whose Mal’cev basis is
O_{C,d}(K)
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
and whose total dimension is smaller than that of
G/\Gamma
, and
\gamma\in\text{poly}_{\approx r,n_{\ast}}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-O_{d,m}(1)}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we assume that
p\gg_{C,d,K,s}1
.
Denote
m_{i}:=\dim(G_{i})
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
m:=\dim(G)
.
Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\}
be the Mal’cev basis of
G/\Gamma
.
Write
g:=\{g(n_{\ast})\}g_{\ast}[g(n_{\ast})]
. Then
g_{\ast}
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
and is rational by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula. So replacing
g
by
g_{\ast}
if necessary,
we may assume without loss of generality that
g(n_{\ast})=id_{G}
.

If
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota(\Omega)^{-1}}
is not
\delta
-totally equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
, then there exists
r_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r_{0}<\delta^{-1}
and some
u\in[r_{0}]^{d}
such that
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\cap(r_{0}\mathbb{Z}^{d}+u)}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
, or equivalently
(g(r_{0}n+u)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(r_{0}^{-1}(\Omega-u))}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
,
where we slightly abuse the notation to treat
r_{0}
and
u
as elements in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
in the expression
r_{0}^{-1}(\Omega-u)
. Since
r_{0}^{-1}(\Omega-u)
admits a rational
(\delta,K)
-Leibman dichotomy and since
g(n_{\ast})=id_{G}
, there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
with
0<\|\eta\|\leq K
and some
a\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
\eta\circ g(r_{0}n+u)+a\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\iota^{-1}(r_{0}^{-1}(\Omega-u))
.

Now for any
x\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
, there exist
v_{x},u_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
x=u+r_{0}v_{x}+pu_{x}
. Then for any
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, since
x-u\equiv r_{0}(v_{x}+py)\mod p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have that
v_{x}+py\in\iota^{-1}(r_{0}^{-1}(\Omega-u))
and thus by assumption
\eta\circ g(u+r_{0}(v_{x}+py))+a\in\mathbb{Z}
. By interpolation, all the coefficients of the polynomial
\eta\circ g(u+r_{0}(v_{x}+p\cdot))+a
belong to
\mathbb{Z}/(s!)^{d}
. Since
x=u+r_{0}(v_{x}+p\cdot(\frac{u_{x}}{r_{0}}))
, we have that
\eta\circ g(x)+a
belongs to belong to
\mathbb{Z}/(r_{0})^{s}(s!)^{d}
for all
x\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
. Since
g(n_{\ast})=id_{G}
, replacing
\eta
by
(r_{0})^{s}(s!)^{d}\eta
if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
\eta\circ g(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the connected component of
\ker(\eta)
. Then
G^{\prime}
is a subgroup of
G
which is
O_{C,d}(K)
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
.

We may use the type-I Taylor expansion to write
g
as

g(n)=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s}g_{i}^{\binom{n}{i}}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

for some
g_{i}\in G_{|i|}
. Write

\psi(g(n))=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s}t_{i}\binom{n}{i}

for some
t_{i}\in\{0\}^{m-m_{|i|}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{|i|}}
.
The type-I horizontal character
\eta
is given in coordinates by

\eta\circ g(n)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s}\theta\cdot t_{i}\binom{n}{i}

for some
\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
of complexity at most
O_{C,d}(K)
.
Let
0\leq s^{\prime}\leq s
be the largest integer such that at least one of the entries
\theta_{m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1},\dots,\theta_{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}
is nonzero (where we denote
m_{0}:=m
). Since

\eta
is nontrivial, such an
s^{\prime}
always exists.
Denote

m^{\prime}:=m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}
.
Then we may write
\theta=(\theta^{\prime},\mathbb{0})
for some
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m^{\prime}}
. Write
t_{i}=(t^{\prime}_{i},t^{\prime\prime}_{i})
for some
t^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}
and
t^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-m^{\prime}}
.
Since the first
m-m_{|i|}
entries of
t_{i}
are zero, and the last
m_{s^{\prime}+1}
entries of
\theta
are zero, we have that
\theta\cdot t_{i}=0
if
|i|\geq s^{\prime}+1
.
So

\eta\circ g(n)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s}\theta^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime}_{i}\binom{n}{i}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0<|i|\leq s^{\prime}}\theta^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime}_{i}\binom{n}{i}.

Since
\theta
is of complexity at most
O_{C,d}(K)
, there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N}_{+},Q\leq O_{C,\delta,d,K}(1)
and
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
whose only nonzero entries are
u_{m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1},\dots,u_{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}
such that

\theta\cdot u=Q.
Denote

\gamma(n):=\prod_{i=m-m_{s^{\prime}}+1}^{m-m_{s^{\prime}+1}}\exp(\frac{1}{Q}u_{i}\eta(g(n))X_{i}),

Then
\gamma
belongs to
\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
. Since
g
is rational, so is
\gamma
by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula.

We now show that there exists
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\ll_{d,m}\delta^{-\mathcal{O}_{d,m}(1)}
such that
\gamma
belongs to
\text{poly}_{\approx r,n_{\ast}}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.
In fact, it suffices to show that
\eta\circ g(n_{\ast}+rn)\in Q\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
n_{\ast}+rn\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
.
For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, since
n_{\ast}+pn\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
, we have that

\eta\circ g(n_{\ast}+pn)\in\mathbb{Z}
. By interpolation, all the coefficients of the polynomial
\eta\circ g
belongs to
\mathbb{Z}/p^{s^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}=O_{d,s}(1)
and
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
Q^{\prime}\leq O_{d,s}(1)
. So for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have that
\eta\circ g(n_{\ast}+QQ^{\prime}n)\in Q\mathbb{Z}/p^{s^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, if
n_{\ast}+QQ^{\prime}n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
, then
\eta\circ g(n_{\ast}+QQ^{\prime}n)\in\mathbb{Z}
, so we have that
\eta\circ g(n_{\ast}+QQ^{\prime}n)\in(Q\mathbb{Z}/p^{s^{\prime}})\cap\mathbb{Z}=Q\mathbb{Z}
. So
\gamma\in\text{poly}_{\approx QQ^{\prime},n_{\ast}}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.

On the other hand, writing
g^{\prime}:=g\gamma^{-1}
we have that
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
by Corollary B.4 of
[
11
]
and that
g^{\prime}
is rational by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula. Since

\eta\circ g^{\prime}(n)=\eta\circ g(n)-\frac{1}{Q}(\theta\cdot u)\eta\circ g(n)=0,

we have that
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})
. We are done since
g=g^{\prime}\gamma
.
∎

We may now repeatedly using Proposition
11.4
to deduce the following strong factorization property:

Theorem 11.5

(Strong factorization property II)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},r,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
C>0
,
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a growth function,
p\gg_{C,d,\mathcal{F},s}1
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form, and
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
satisfying one of the following assumptions:

(1)

\Omega=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
;

(2)

\Omega=V(M)\cap(V+c)
for some affine subspace
V+c
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})\geq s+13
.

Let
n_{\ast}\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
,
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of complexity at most
C
, and let
g\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
be rational. There exist some
C\leq C^{\prime}\leq O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},s}(1)
,
a proper subgroup
G^{\prime}
of
G
which is
C^{\prime}
-rational relative to
\mathcal{X}
, and
a factorization

g(n)=\epsilon g^{\prime}(n)\gamma(n)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

such that
\epsilon\in G
is of complexity
O_{C^{\prime}}(1)
,

g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G^{\prime}_{\mathbb{N}})
is rational and
(g^{\prime}(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)}
is
\mathcal{F}(C^{\prime})^{-1}
-totally equidistributed on
G^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}\cap\Gamma
, and that
\gamma
belongs to both
\text{poly}_{\approx r,n_{\ast}}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
and
\text{poly}_{r}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
.
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
r\leq O_{C,C^{\prime},d,s}(1)
.

Sketch of the proof.

By Theorem
10.2
or
10.3
combined with a change of variable, we have that
k\Omega+u
is a non-empty set which admits a rational
(\mathcal{F}(C)^{-1},O_{C,d,\mathcal{F},s}(1))
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
for all
k\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\backslash\{0\}
and
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. By the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, it is not hard to see that the products of two polynomial sequences which are partially
Q
-rational on
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
defined over a nilmanifold of complexity
C
is partially
Q^{\prime}
-rational on
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
for some
Q^{\prime}\leq O_{C,Q}(1)
.
we may obtain the conclusion of Theorem
11.5
by repeatedly using Proposition
11.4
(and then apply Proposition
3.19
at the last step). We leave the details to the interested readers.
∎

12.
Open questions

We collect some open questions in this section.
Recall that we need to require
d\geq s+13
in Theorem
1.1
. It is natural to ask:

Question 12.1
.

What is the optimal lower bound for the dimension
d
in Theorem
1.1
?

In order to improve the lower bound of
d
in Theorem
1.1
, it is natural to attempt to sharpen the tools we used in proving Theorem
1.1
. Therefore, one can ask:

Question 12.2
.

What are the optimal lower bounds for the dimension
d
in Proposition
6.8
, Theorems
9.14
,
10.6
, and
C.3
?

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form.
It is an interesting question to ask what is the connection between
p
-periodic polynomial sequences on
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and partially
p
-periodic polynomial sequences on
V(M)
. In particular, is it true that a partially
p
-periodic polynomial sequence on
V(M)
merely the restriction to
V(M)
of a
p
-periodic polynomial sequences on
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
?

Question 12.3
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form of rank at least 3, and
G_{\mathbb{N}}
be a nilpotent group of step
s
.
Let
p\gg_{d,s}1
.
For all
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(V(M)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, is there any
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
such that
g(n)\Gamma=g^{\prime}(n)\Gamma
for all
n\in V(M)
?

Another interesting question is to ask if there is an intrinsic definition for partially
p
-periodic polynomials on
V(M)
. This is Conjecture
6.9
which we restate below:

Question 12.4
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a function,
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
p\gg_{d}1
be a prime. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form with
\text{rank}(M)\gg_{s}1
. Are the followings equivalent?

(1)

\Delta_{h_{s}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)=0
for all
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M))
.

(2)

There exists a polynomial
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s-1
such that
g(n)=g^{\prime}(n)
for all
n\in V(M)
.

As is mentioned in Section
6.4
, we were only able to answer a special case of this question.

Another interesting question in algebra is that whether all the nice and consistent
M
-sets satisfy Hilbert Nullstellensatz. This is Conjecture
D.9
which we restate below:

Question 12.5
.

Let
d,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
p
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form, and
\Omega\subseteq(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
be a consistent
M
-set given by
\Omega=V(\mathcal{J})
for some consistent
(M,k)
-family
\mathcal{J}=\{f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\}
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. If
d\gg_{k,r,s}1
and
p\gg_{d}1
, then for any polynomial
P\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s
with
\Omega\subseteq V(P)
, can we write

P=\sum_{j=1}^{r}f_{j}Q_{j}

for some polynomial
Q_{j}
of degree
O_{k,r,s}(1)
?

In Appendix
D
,
We prove a weaker version of Conjecture
D.9
in Proposition
D.10
. Also it is proved in Proposition
6.3
that Conjecture
D.9
holds for
\Omega=V(M)
. But the general case remains unanswered.

Finally, it is an important question to ask whether the factorization theorems holds in the setting of
p
-periodic polynomial sequences.

Question 12.6
.

In Proposition
11.1
and Theorem
11.2
, if we assume in addition that
g
is
p
-periodic, then can we also require
g^{\prime}
to be
p
-periodic in the factorization of
g
?

If the answer to Question
12.6
is affirmative, then many arguments in this paper as well as in
[
18
,
19
]
can be greatly simplified by restricting ourselves to
p
-periodic polynomial sequences.

Appendix A
Properties for liftings of polynomials

In this appendix, we provide some basic properties for liftings of polynomials.

Lemma A.1

(Basic properties for lifting)
.

Let
d,d^{\prime},d^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
p>\max\{d,d^{\prime},d^{\prime\prime}\}
be a prime.

(1)

Every
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
of degree less than
p
induces
\iota\circ pf\circ\tau
, which is a polynomial of degree at most
\deg(f)
. Moreover, if
f
is homogeneous, then so is
\iota\circ pf\circ\tau
;

(2)

Every
F\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}^{d^{\prime}}_{p})
of degree less than
p
admits a regular lifting. Moreover, if
F
is homogeneous, we may further require the lifting to be homogeneous;

(3)

If
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
is a lifting of some
F\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}})
with both
f
and
F
having degrees at most
p-1
, then for any
f^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
of degree less than
p
,
f^{\prime}
is a lifting of
F
if and only if
f-f^{\prime}
is integer valued;

(4)

If
f,f^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
are liftings of
F,F^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}})
respectively with all of
f,f^{\prime},F
and
F^{\prime}
having degrees at most
(p-1)/2
, then
f+f^{\prime}
is a lifting of
F+F^{\prime}
and
pff^{\prime}
is a lifting of
FF^{\prime}
;

(5)

If
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
is a lifting of some
F\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}})
with both
f
and
F
having degree at most
\sqrt{p}
, and
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d^{\prime}}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime\prime}})
is a lifting of some
F\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime\prime}})
with both
f
and
F
having degrees less than
\sqrt{p}
, then
f^{\prime}\circ pf
is a lifting of
F^{\prime}\circ F
;

(6)

If
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
is a lifting of some
F\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}})
with both
f
and
F
having degrees less than
p
, then
\tau\circ F\equiv pf\circ\tau\mod\mathbb{Z}^{d^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Part (i).
Since
f
takes values in
(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}}
, by the multivariate polynomial interpolation,
there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
such that

f(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=\sum_{0\leq a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\leq p-1,a_{i}\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{C_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}}{Qp^{s}}n^{a_{1}}_{1}\dots n^{a_{d-1}}_{d}

for some
C_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d^{\prime}}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Using the property that
f(n+pm)-f(n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d^{\prime}}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, it is not hard to see that we may further write
f
as

f(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=\sum_{0\leq a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\leq p-1,a_{i}\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{C^{\prime}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}}{Qp}n^{a_{1}}_{1}\dots n^{a_{d-1}}_{d}

for some
C^{\prime}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d^{\prime}}
and
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
.
Let
C^{\prime\prime}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}^{d^{\prime}}
be such that
C^{\prime\prime}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}Q\equiv C^{\prime}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}\mod p\mathbb{Z}^{d^{\prime}}
. Denote
f^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}}
,

f^{\prime}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=\sum_{0\leq a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\leq p-1,a_{i}\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{C^{\prime\prime}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}}}{p}n^{a_{1}}_{1}\dots n^{a_{d-1}}_{d}.

and
F\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}}
,

F(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=\sum_{0\leq a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\leq p-1,a_{i}\in\mathbb{N}}\iota(C^{\prime\prime}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{d}})n^{a_{1}}_{1}\dots n^{a_{d-1}}_{d}.

Since
