O_{k,s}(1)
for all
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{k-1}
and
n_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
∎

Step 3: removing the
(G_{k,I,i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))^{s+2}
term using a parametrization trick.

We are now ready to complete the proof of Part (i) of Theorem
D.1
for
\Omega_{k}
for the case
c_{k}=1
.
Let
P\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with
|V(P)\cap\Omega_{k}|\geq\delta|\Omega_{k}|
.
We wish to show that for all
\mathbb{n}=(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})\in\Omega_{k}
, we have that
P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
.

We first consider the case when
n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1}
are linearly independent. Since
L_{k,j},2\leq j\leq r_{k}
are linearly independent,
24
24

24

Meaning that no nontrivial linear combination of
L_{k,j},2\leq j\leq r_{k}
is zero.
we have that
L_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}),2\leq j\leq r_{k}
are linearly independent.
Since
A
is invertible, the vectors
L_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})A=(S_{k,j,1}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}),\dots,S_{k,j,d}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})),2\leq j\leq r_{k}
are also linearly independent. By the knowledge of linear algebra, there exists a subset
I
of
\{1,\dots,d\}
of cardinality
r_{k}-1
such that
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\neq 0
.
By Lemma
D.3
,
there exist
i_{1},i_{2}\in I^{c},i_{1}\leq i_{2}
such that
G_{k,I,i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\neq 0
. On the other hand, by Theorem
C.3
,
|V(P)\cap\Omega_{k}|\geq\delta p^{dk-r}/2
since
d\geq 2r+1
.
Since
(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})\in\Omega_{k}
, we have that
(G_{k,I,i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))^{s+2}P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
by Proposition
D.5
. Therefore,
P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
.

We now consider the general case.
For
1\leq t\leq k-1
, we say that
Property
t
holds if for all
\mathbb{n}=(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})\in\Omega_{k}
with

n_{1},\dots,n_{t}
being linearly independent, we have that
P(\mathbb{n},n_{k})=0
. By the above discussion, Property
k-1
holds.
Assume now that we have proven Property
t+1
holds for some
0\leq t\leq k-2
. We show that Property
t
holds.
Pick any
\mathbb{n}=(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})\in\Omega_{k}
such that
n_{1},\dots,n_{t}
are linearly independent. If
n_{1},\dots,n_{t+1}
are linearly independent, then
P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
by induction hypothesis and we are done. So we assume that
n_{1},\dots,n_{t+1}
are linearly dependent.

Lemma D.6
.

There exists a subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
dimension
3
such that

•

for all
v\in V\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}
,
n_{1},\dots,n_{t},v
are linearly independent;

•

for all
v\in V
and
1\leq j\leq k
, we have that
(vA)\cdot n_{j}=0
;

•

we have that
V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
.

Proof.

Let
W_{1}
denote the set of
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
n_{1},\dots,n_{t},v
are linearly dependent.
Then
|W_{1}|=p^{t}
.
Let
W_{2}
denote the set of
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that

(vA)\cdot n_{j}=0

for all
1\leq j\leq k
.
Then
W_{2}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension at least
d-k
.
So there exists a subspace
W_{3}
of
W_{2}
with
W_{1}\cap W_{3}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
of dimension

d^{\prime}\geq d-k-t.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}}\to W_{3}
be any bijective linear transformation and let
M^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be the quadratic form given by
M^{\prime}=M\circ\phi
.
By Proposition
4.8
(iii),
\text{rank}(M^{\prime})=\text{rank}(M|_{W_{3}})\geq d-2(k+t)\geq d-2(2k-2)\geq 3
.
So by Lemma
4.7
, there exists a subspace
U
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}}
of dimension 3 which is
M^{\prime}
-non-isotropic. Then clearly
\phi(U)
is
M
-non-isotropic. Moreover,
\phi(U)
is a subspace of
W_{3}
of dimension 3. We are done by taking
V=\phi(U)
.
∎

Let
V
be given by Lemma
D.6
, and
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}\to V
be any bijective linear transformation.
Let
W
be the set of
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
such that
(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\phi(m),n_{t+2},\dots,n_{k})\in\Omega_{k}
. Note that
\mathbb{0}\in W
.
Since
(\phi(m)A)\cdot n_{j}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq k
, by (
D.4
) and (
D.5
),
for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq r_{i^{\prime}}
with either
(i^{\prime},j)\neq(t+1,1)
or
(i^{\prime},j)=(t+1,1)
and
c_{t+1}=0
,
it is not hard to see that

F_{i^{\prime},j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\phi(m),n_{t+2},\dots,n_{k})=F_{i^{\prime},j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1},n_{t+2},\dots,n_{k}).

Moreover, if
(i^{\prime},j)=(t+1,1)
and
c_{t+1}=1
, then by (
D.4
),
we have that

M^{\prime}(m):=F_{t+1,1}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\phi(m),n_{t+2},\dots,n_{k})

is a quadratic form from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
to
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. It is then not hard to see that

W=V(M^{\prime})
if
c_{t+1}=1
and
W=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
if
c_{t+1}=0
. Since
V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
, by Proposition
4.8
(ii),
\text{rank}(M^{\prime})=3
.
So by Propositions
9.9
and
9.10
,
W
is
1/2
-irreducible.

On the other hand, for all
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}
,
since
n_{1},\dots,n_{t},\phi(m)
are linearly independent and since
n_{1},\dots,n_{t+1}
are linearly dependent, we have that
n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\phi(m)
are linearly independent.
By the induction hypothesis that Property
t+1
holds, we have that

P^{\prime}(m):=P(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\phi(m),n_{t+2},\dots,n_{k})=0

for all
m\in W\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}
.
In other words,
W\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}\subseteq V(P^{\prime})
. So
|V(P^{\prime})\cap W|\geq|W|-1\geq|W|/2
.
Since
W
is
1/2
-irreducible, we have that
W\subseteq V(P^{\prime})
. In particular, since
\mathbb{0}\in W
, we have that

P^{\prime}(\mathbb{0})=P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0.

In conclusion, we have that Property
t
holds. By induction, we deduce that Property 0 holds, meaning that
\Omega_{k}\subseteq V(P)
. So
\Omega_{k}
is
\delta
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

D.4.
Proof of Part (i) of Theorem
D.1
for the degenerate case

We now consider the case when
c_{k}=0
.
In this case
r_{k}\leq k-1
. Since the proof is very similar to (actually slightly easier than) the case
c_{k}=1
, we only provide an outline for the proof of this case and leave the details to the interested readers.

Step 1: a parametrization for the last variable.

Write

L_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})A=(S_{k,j,1}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}),\dots,S_{k,j,d}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))

for some polynomials
S_{k,j,1},\dots,S_{k,j,d}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k-1}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
of degree at most 1. Since
A
is diagonal, for all
1\leq\ell\leq d
,
S_{k,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})
is dependent only on
n_{i,\ell},1\leq i\leq k-1
.
Then we may rewrite (
D.5
) as

(D.15)

F_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}n_{k,\ell}S_{k,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})+u_{k,j},

for all
1\leq j\leq r_{i}
.

For
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}
with
|I|=r_{k}>0
, let
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})
denote the determinant of the
r_{k}\times r_{k}
matrix

(S_{k,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))_{2\leq j\leq r_{k},\ell\in I}
.
Then

\Delta_{k,I}
is a polynomial from
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
to
\mathbb{F}_{p}
of degree at most
r_{k}
depending only on
n_{i,\ell},1\leq i\leq k-1,\ell\in I
.
For convenience denote
I^{c}:=\{1,\dots,d\}\backslash I
.
Fix any
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{k-1}
with
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\neq 0
. Then
L_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})A,2\leq j\leq r_{k}
are linearly independent. By (
D.15
) and the knowledge of linear algebra,
F_{k,1}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=\dots=F_{k,r_{k}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
if and only if
for all
\ell\in I
,

(D.16)

n_{k,\ell}=\frac{R_{k,I,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k,I^{c}})}{\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})},

where
R_{k,I,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k,I^{c}})
is the determinant of the
r_{k}\times r_{k}
matrix obtained by replacing the column
(S_{k,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))_{1\leq j\leq r_{k}}
from
(S_{k,j,\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))_{1\leq j\leq r_{k},\ell^{\prime}\in I}
by the column
(R_{k,I,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k,I^{c}}))_{1\leq j\leq r_{k}}
where

(D.17)

R_{k,I,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k,I^{c}})=-u_{k,j}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in I^{c}}n_{k,\ell^{\prime}}S_{k,j,\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})=-F_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in I}n_{k,\ell^{\prime}}S_{k,j,\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}).

Let
Z_{k,I}
denote the set of
(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
such that
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\neq 0
.
Similar to the proof of Lemma
D.4
, we have (we omit the details):

Lemma D.7
.

We have that
|{Z}^{c}_{k,I}\cap\Omega_{k}|\leq\delta p^{dk-r}/2
.

Step 2: a factorization for the target polynomial.

This step is carried out by the following proposition:

Proposition D.8
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq k\leq K
,
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}
with
|I|=r_{k}
and
P\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with
|V(P)\cap\Omega_{k}|\geq\delta p^{dk-r}
. Then

\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})^{s+2}P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}F_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})Q_{j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})

for some polynomial
Q_{j}
of degree at most
O_{k,s}(1)
for all
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{k-1}
and
n_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

Proof.

The proof is a simpler version of that of Proposition
D.5
.
By Lemma
D.7
, we have that
|V(P)\cap\Omega_{k}\cap Z_{k,I}|\geq\delta p^{dk-r}/2
.
By the Pigeonhole Principle and Theorem
C.3
(ii), it is not hard to see that there exist a subset
U\subseteq\Omega_{k-1}
with
|U|\gg\delta p^{d(k-1)-(r-r_{k})}
and a subset
U(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\subseteq\Omega_{k}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})
of cardinality
\gg\delta p^{d-r_{k}}
for each
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in U
such that for all
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in U
and
n_{k}\in U(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})
, we have that
P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
and
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\neq 0
.

For simplicity denote
n^{\prime}_{k}:=n_{k,I^{c}}=(n_{k,\ell})_{\ell\in I^{c}}
and set

P_{I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n^{\prime}_{k}):=P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},\phi_{k,I,\mathbb{n}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}_{k}))
. Then for all
n_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, we have that
(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})\in V(P_{I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},\cdot))\cap\Omega_{k}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})
if and only if
n_{k}=\phi_{k,I,\mathbb{n}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}_{k})
for some
n^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-r_{k}}
with
P_{I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n^{\prime}_{k})=0
. Since
U(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})
is of cardinality
\gg\delta p^{d-r_{k}}
, we have that
|V(P_{I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},\cdot))|\gg\delta p^{d-r_{k}}
.
By Lemma
4.10
, we have that

(D.18)

P_{I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n^{\prime}_{k})=0

for all
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in U
and
n^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-r_{k}}
.
We may write the left hand side of (
D.18
) as

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{d-r_{k}},|m|\leq s}C_{m}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}){n^{\prime}_{k}}^{m}

for some polynomial
C_{m}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})
of degree at most
O_{k,s}(1)
.
Then we have that
C_{m}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})=0
for all
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in U
and thus
|V(C_{m})\cap\Omega_{k-1}|\gg\delta p^{d(k-1)-(r-r_{k})}
. By induction hypothesis, we have that
\Omega_{k-1}\subseteq V(C_{m})
. So (
D.18
) holds for all
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{k-1}
and
n^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-r_{k}}
.

Let
(\phi_{k,I,\mathbb{n}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}_{k}))_{\ell}
denote the
\ell
-th entry of
\phi_{k,I,\mathbb{n}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}_{k})
.
By (
D.16
) and (
D.17
), for all
\ell\in I
,
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})((\phi_{k,I,\mathbb{n}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}_{k}))_{\ell}-n_{k,\ell})
equals to
the determinant of the
r_{k}\times r_{k}
matrix obtained by replacing the column
(S_{k,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))_{1\leq j\leq r_{k}}
from
(S_{k,j,\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))_{1\leq j\leq r_{k},\ell^{\prime}\in I}
by the column
(q_{j,\ell})_{1\leq j\leq r_{k}}
, where

q_{j,\ell}=R_{k,I,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n^{\prime}_{k})-n_{k,\ell}S_{k,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})=-F_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in I\backslash\{\ell\}}n_{k,\ell^{\prime}}S_{k,j,\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})

for all
1\leq j\leq r_{k}
, which, by linearity, equals to
the determinant of the
r_{k}\times r_{k}
matrix obtained by replacing the column
(S_{k,j,\ell}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))_{1\leq j\leq r_{k}}
from
(S_{k,j,\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))_{1\leq j\leq r_{k},\ell^{\prime}\in I}
by the column
(-F_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k}))_{1\leq j\leq r_{k}}

This means that
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})((\phi_{k,I,\mathbb{n}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}_{k}))_{\ell}-n_{k,\ell})
can be written in the form

(D.19)

\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}F_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})Q_{j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})

for some polynomial
Q_{j}\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
r_{k}
.
Therefore,

\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})^{s}(P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})-P_{I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n^{\prime}_{k}))=\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})^{s}(P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})-P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},(\phi_{k,I,\mathbb{n}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}_{k})))

can be written in the form (
D.19
) for some polynomial
Q_{j}\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
ks
. Since (
D.18
) holds for all
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{k-1}
and
n^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d-r_{k}}
, we have that

\Delta_{I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})^{s+2}P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}F_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})Q_{j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})

for some polynomial
Q_{j}
of degree at most
O_{k,s}(1)
for all
\mathbb{n}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{k-1}
and
n_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
∎

Step 3: removing the
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})^{s+2}
term using a parametrization trick.

We are now ready to complete the proof of Part (i) of Theorem
D.1
for
\Omega_{k}
for the case
c_{k}=1
.
Let
P\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with
|V(P)\cap\Omega_{k}|\geq\delta|\Omega_{k}|
.
We wish to show that for all
\mathbb{n}=(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})\in\Omega_{k}
, we have that
P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
.

We first consider the case when
n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1}
are linearly independent.
Since
L_{k,j},1\leq j\leq r_{k}
are linearly independent, we have that
L_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}),1\leq j\leq r_{k}
are linearly independent.
Since
A
is invertible, the vectors
L_{k,j}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})A=(S_{k,j,1}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}),\dots,S_{k,j,d}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})),1\leq j\leq r_{k}
are also linearly independent. By the knowledge of linear algebra, there exists a subset
I
of
\{1,\dots,d\}
of cardinality
r_{k}
such that
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})\neq 0
.
On the other hand, by Theorem
C.3
,
|V(P)\cap\Omega_{k}|\geq\delta p^{dk-r}/2
since
d\geq 2r+1
.
Since
(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})\in\Omega_{k}
, we have that
\Delta_{k,I}(\mathbb{n}^{\prime})^{s+2}P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
by Proposition
D.8
. Therefore,
P(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{k})=0
.

If
n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1}
are linearly dependent, the argument is similar to the case
c_{k}=1
. We omit the details.
This completes the proof of Part (i) of Theorem
D.1
.

D.5.
Hilbert Nullstellensatz properties for
M
-sets

Before proving Parts (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
D.1
,
we take a detour to study Hilbert Nullstellensatz properties for
M
-sets. It is natural to ask the following question:

Conjecture D.9

(Hilbert Nullstellensatz for
M
-sets)
.

Let
d,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
p
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form, and
\Omega\subseteq(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
be a consistent
M
-set given by
\Omega=V(\mathcal{J})
for some consistent
(M,k)
-family
\mathcal{J}=\{f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\}
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. If
d\gg_{k,r,s}1
and
p\gg_{d}1
, then for any polynomial
P\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
of degree at most
s
with
\Omega\subseteq V(P)
, we have that

P=\sum_{j=1}^{r}f_{j}Q_{j}

for some polynomial
Q_{j}
of degree
O_{k,r,s}(1)
.

Unfortunately
we do not know the answer to Conjecture
D.9
. However, we have a partial result which is sufficient for the purpose of this paper.
We need to introduce some notations before stating the result. Let the notations be the same as Sections
D.2
,
D.3
and
D.4
.
Assume that
\Omega
is of type
(c_{1},\dots,c_{K})
.
For all
1\leq k\leq K
, if
c_{k}=1
, pick some
I_{k}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}
with
|I_{k}|=r_{k}-1
and some
i_{k,1},i_{k,2}\in\{1,\dots,d\}\backslash I_{k},i_{k,1}\leq i_{k,2}
; if
c_{k}=0
, pick some
I_{k}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}
with
|I_{k}|=r_{k}
and set
i_{k,1}=i_{k,2}=0
. We say that
\mathfrak{I}:=(I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2})_{k=1}^{K}
is an
admissible parametrization
of
\Omega
. Define

D_{\mathfrak{I}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{K}):=\prod_{k=1}^{K}G_{k,I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1})\Delta_{k,I_{k}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1}),

where we set
\Delta_{k,I_{k}}\equiv 1
if
I_{k}=\emptyset
and set
G_{k,I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2}}\equiv 1
if either
I_{k}=\emptyset
or
i_{k,1}=i_{k,2}=0
. We have the following partial answer to Conjecture
D.9
:

Proposition D.10
.

Let the notations be the same as Sections
D.2
,
D.3
and
D.4
.
Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathfrak{I}
be an admissible parametrization of
\Omega
.
Let
P\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with
\Omega\subseteq V(P)
. Then

(D.20)

\begin{split}D_{\mathfrak{I}}^{T}P=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}F_{k,j}Q_{k,j}\end{split}

for some
T=O_{K,s}(1)\in\mathbb{N}
and some polynomial
Q_{k,j}
of degree at most
O_{K,s}(1)
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathfrak{I}:=(I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2})_{k=1}^{K}
.
For
1\leq t\leq K
, we say that
Property
t
holds if

\begin{split}&\quad\Bigl(\prod_{k=t}^{K}G_{k,I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1})\Delta_{k,I_{k}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1})\Bigr)^{T}P(n_{1},\dots,n_{K})\\
&=\sum_{k=t}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}F_{k,j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})Q_{k,j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{K})\end{split}

for some
T=O_{K,s}(1)\in\mathbb{N}
and some polynomial
Q_{k,j}
of degree at most
O_{K,s}(1)
for all
(n_{1},\dots,n_{t-1})\in\Omega_{t-1}
and
n_{t},\dots,n_{K}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

By Theorem
C.3
, Propositions
D.5
and
D.8
, we have that Property
K
holds. Suppose now that we have shown Property
(t+1)
for some
1\leq t\leq K-1
, we prove that
Property
t
holds. Suppose that

(D.21)

\begin{split}&\quad\Bigl(\prod_{k=t+1}^{K}G_{k,I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1})\Delta_{k,I_{k}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1})\Bigr)^{T}P(n_{1},\dots,n_{K})\\
&-\sum_{k=t+1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}F_{k,j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})Q_{k,j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{K})=0\end{split}

for some
T=O_{K,s}(1)\in\mathbb{N}
and some polynomial
Q_{k,j}
of degree at most
O_{k,s}(1)
for all
(n_{1},\dots,n_{t})\in\Omega_{t}
and
n_{t+1},\dots,n_{K}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Let
Q
be the polynomial on the left hand side of (
D.21
). Then
Q(n_{1},\dots,n_{K})=0
for all
(n_{1},\dots,n_{t})\in\Omega_{t}
and
n_{t+1},\dots,n_{K}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Writing

(D.22)

\begin{split}Q(n_{1},\dots,n_{K})=\sum_{i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|i_{t+1}+\dots+i_{K}|\leq O_{K,s}(1)}C_{i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t})n_{t+1}^{i_{t+1}}\dots n_{K}^{i_{K}}\end{split}

for some polynomials
C_{i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}}
of degrees at most
O_{k,s}(1)
, this implies that
C_{i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}}(n_{1},\dots,

n_{t})=0
for all
i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|i_{t+1}+\dots+i_{K}|\leq O_{K,s}(1)
and
(n_{1},\dots,n_{t})\in\Omega_{t}
.
Applying Theorem
C.3
, Propositions
D.5
and
D.8
to the set
\Omega_{t}
, we have that

(D.23)

\begin{split}&\quad(G_{t,I_{t},i_{t,1},i_{t,2}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t-1})\Delta_{t,I_{t}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t-1}))^{T^{\prime}}C_{i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t})\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{t}}F_{t,j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t})Q_{j;i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{t})\end{split}

for some
T^{\prime}=O_{K,s}(1)\in\mathbb{N}
and some polynomial
Q_{j;i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}}
of degree at most
O_{K,s}(1)
for all
(n_{1},\dots,n_{t-1})\in\Omega_{t-1}
and
n_{t}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
for all
i_{t+1},\dots,i_{K}\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|i_{t+1}+\dots+i_{K}|\leq O_{K,s}(1)
. Property
t
follows by combining (
D.21
), (
D.22
) and (
D.23
).

So by induction, we have that Property 1 holds and we are done.
∎

Let
\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}},\tilde{F}_{k,j}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
be regular liftings of
D_{\mathfrak{I}}
and
F_{k,j}
, respectively. By Lemmas
2.8
and
A.1
, we may lift Proposition
D.10
to the
\mathbb{Z}/p
setting.

Corollary D.11
.

Let the notations be the same as Sections
D.2
,
D.3
and
D.4
.
Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathfrak{I}
be an admissible parametrization of
\Omega
.
Let
P\in\text{poly}_{p}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p|\mathbb{Z})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\subseteq V_{p}(P)
. Then
there exist
T=O_{K,s}(1)\in\mathbb{N}
and
Q_{0},Q_{k,j}\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{Z}),1\leq k\leq K,0\leq j\leq r_{k}
of degree at most
O_{K,s}(1)
with
Q_{k,j}
having integer coefficients such that

\begin{split}(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T}P=Q_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}\tilde{F}_{k,j}Q_{k,j}.\end{split}

Proof.

Let
P^{\prime}\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
be the polynomials induced by
P
. Then
\Omega\subseteq V(P^{\prime})
. By
Proposition
D.10
, we have

(D.24)

\begin{split}D_{\mathfrak{I}}^{T}P^{\prime}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}F_{k,j}Q^{\prime}_{k,j}\end{split}

for some
T=O_{K,s}(1)\in\mathbb{N}
and some
Q^{\prime}_{k,j}
of degree at most
O_{K,s}(1)
.
Let
\tilde{Q}_{k,j}
be a regular lifting of
Q^{\prime}_{k,j}
.
By Lemma
A.1
(iv), we have that
(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T}P

is a lifting of

D_{\mathfrak{I}}^{T}P^{\prime},

and that
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}\tilde{F}_{k,j}p\tilde{Q}_{k,j}

is a lifting of

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}F_{k,j}Q^{\prime}_{k,j}.

So by Lemma
A.1
(iii) and (
D.24
), the difference
between
(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T}P
and
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{k}}\tilde{F}_{k,j}p\tilde{Q}_{k,j}
equals to an integer valued polynomial
Q_{0}

of degree at most
O_{K,s}(1)
.
Finally, since
\tilde{Q}_{k,j}
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}/p
,
Q_{k,j}:=p\tilde{Q}_{k,j}
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}
. Applying Lemma
2.8
to modify
Q_{k,j}
and
Q_{0}
accordingly, we may require that all of
Q_{k,j}
have integer coefficients, while
Q_{0}
still takes integer values.
We are done.
∎

D.6.
Proof of parts (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
D.1

We use the same notations as in Sections
D.2
and
D.3
, except that now
\mathbb{n},\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{i},n_{i,j}
denote elements in
\mathbb{Z}^{t}
instead of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{t}
for some appropriate
t\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and that we use
r
to denote
r_{M}(\Omega)
.
Part (ii) follows from Part (i) and Proposition
9.8
. So it remains to prove Part (iii).
Our strategy is to use the
p
-expansion trick.
Let
g\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})
be a polynomial of degree at most
s
with
|V_{p}(g)\cap\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\cap([p]^{d})^{K}|\geq\delta p^{dK-r}
. Our goal is to show that
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\subseteq V_{p}(g)
.

Since
g
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}/p^{s}
,
by the multivariate polynomial interpolation, it is not hard to see that there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\nmid Q
such that

Qg=\sum_{i=0}^{s}\frac{g^{\prime}_{i}}{p^{i}}

for some integer valued polynomials
g^{\prime}_{i}\colon(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{Z}
of degree at most
s
.

It is convenient to reindex the polynomials
F_{k,j},1\leq k\leq K,1\leq j\leq r_{k}
as
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
in an arbitrary order. Let
\tilde{f}_{j}
be a regular lifting of
f_{j}
.
We say that a polynomial
F\colon(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{R}
is
t
-good
if

F=\sum_{i:=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{r},|i|\leq s/2}F_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\tilde{f}_{j}^{i_{j}}

for some integer valued
polynomials
F_{i}\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{Z})
of degree at most
t-2|i|,0\leq|i|\leq s/2
.
For convenience denote
a_{s}=s
and let
a_{i}=O_{a_{i+1},K,s}(1)
to be chosen later for all
0\leq i\leq s-1
. Then
a_{0}=O_{K,s}(1)<p
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}=(I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2})_{k=1}^{K}
be an admissible parametrization of
\Omega
.
Then
p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}
is an integer valued polynomial of degree at most
O_{K,s}(1)
. Let
T=O_{K,s}(1)\in\mathbb{N}
to be chosen later.
Let
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
be the smallest integer such that

(D.25)

(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T(s-k^{\prime})}Qg=\sum_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}}\frac{g_{i}}{p^{i}}

for some
Q\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\nmid Q
and

a_{k^{\prime}}
-good polynomials
g_{i}
.
Obviously such
k^{\prime}
exists and is at most
s
. We first show that
k^{\prime}=0
.

Suppose that

k^{\prime}>0
.
Let
U:=V_{p}(g)\cap\tau(\Omega)
.
For all
\mathbb{n}\in U
and
\mathbb{m}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}
, since
g(\mathbb{n}+p\mathbb{m})\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that

g_{k^{\prime}}(\mathbb{n}+p\mathbb{m})\in p\mathbb{Z}.

Suppose that

(D.26)

g_{k^{\prime}}=\sum_{i:=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{r},|i|\leq s/2}R_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\tilde{f}_{j}^{i_{j}}

for some integer valued polynomials
R_{i}\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{Z})
of degree at most
a_{k^{\prime}}-2|i|
.

Claim.
There exists a subset
U^{\prime}
of
U
of cardinality at least
\delta p^{dK-r}/2
such that for all
\mathbb{n}\in U^{\prime}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}^{r},|i|\leq s/2
, we have that
\frac{1}{p}R_{i}(\mathbb{n})\in\mathbb{Z}
.

By (
D.4
) and (
D.5
), for all
1\leq j\leq r
, we may write

f_{j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{K})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq K}\frac{1}{2}c_{j,i,i}(n_{i}A)\cdot n_{i}+\sum_{1\leq i<i^{\prime}\leq K}c_{j,i,i^{\prime}}(n_{i}A)\cdot n_{i^{\prime}}+c^{\prime}_{j}

for some
c_{j,i,i^{\prime}},c^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
such that the vectors
v_{j}:=(c_{j,i,i^{\prime}})_{1\leq i\leq i^{\prime}\leq K}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\binom{K+1}{2}},1\leq j\leq r
are linearly independent. For convenience denote
c_{j,i,i^{\prime}}:=c_{j,i^{\prime},i}
for
i^{\prime}<i
. It is not hard to see that we may write

\tilde{f}_{j}(\mathbb{n}+p\mathbb{m})\equiv\tilde{f}_{j}(\mathbb{n})+T_{j}(\mathbb{n},\mathbb{m})\mod p\mathbb{Z}

for all
\mathbb{m},\mathbb{n}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}
, where

T_{j}(\mathbb{n},\mathbb{m})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}(m_{i}\tau(A))\cdot\Bigl(\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{K}\tau(c_{j,i,i^{\prime}})n_{i^{\prime}}\Bigr).

Fix any
\mathbb{n}\in U
. Then

(D.27)

0\equiv g_{k^{\prime}}(\mathbb{n}+p\mathbb{m})\equiv\sum_{i:=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{r},|i|\leq s/2}R_{i}(\mathbb{n})\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\tilde{f}_{j}(\mathbb{n})+T_{j}(\mathbb{n},\mathbb{m}))^{i_{j}}\mod p\mathbb{Z}

for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Let
U^{\prime\prime}
denote the set of
\mathbb{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{K})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{K}
such that
there exist
t_{1},\dots,t_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
not all equal to 0 with

\sum_{j=1}^{r}t_{j}\Bigl(\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{K}c_{j,i,i^{\prime}}n_{i^{\prime}}\Bigr)=\mathbb{0}\text{ for all $1\leq i\leq K$.}

Then for all
\mathbb{n}\in U^{\prime\prime}
, there exist
t_{1},\dots,t_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
such that

(D.28)

\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{K}u_{i,i^{\prime}}n_{i^{\prime}}=\mathbb{0}\text{ for all $1\leq i\leq K$,}

where
u_{i,i^{\prime}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{r}t_{j}c_{j,i,i^{\prime}}
.
Since
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
are linearly independent, at least one of
u_{i,i^{\prime}},1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq K
is non-zero. So at least one of the equations in (
D.28
) is nontrivial and thus one of
n_{1},\dots,n_{K}

is uniquely determined by the rest. So

|U^{\prime\prime}|\leq Kp^{r}\cdot p^{d(K-1)}=Kp^{d(K-1)+r}.

Let
U^{\prime}:=U\backslash\tau(U^{\prime\prime})
.
Since
|U|\geq\delta p^{dK-r}
, and
d\geq 2r+1
, if
p\gg_{d,s}\delta^{-O_{d,s}(1)}
, then
|U^{\prime}|\geq\delta p^{dK-r}/2
.

On the other hand, for all
\mathbb{n}\in U^{\prime}
, the vectors
(\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{K}c_{j,i,i^{\prime}}n_{i^{\prime}})_{i=1}^{K},1\leq j\leq r
(as elements in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{dK}
) are linearly independent. Since
A
is invertible, we have that

\mathbb{m}\mod p(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\mapsto(\tilde{f}_{j}(\mathbb{n})+T_{j}(\mathbb{n},\mathbb{m})\mod p\mathbb{Z})_{1\leq j\leq r}

is a surjection from
(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{dK}
to
(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{r}
. So it follows from (
D.27
) that

\sum_{i:=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{r},|i|\leq s/2}R_{i}(\mathbb{n})\prod_{j=1}^{r}x_{j}^{i_{j}}\equiv 0\mod p\mathbb{Z}

for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}
. By interpolation, this implies that
QR_{i}(\mathbb{n})\in p\mathbb{Z}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}^{r},|i|\leq s/2
for some
Q\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q
. So
R_{i}
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{p}{Q}\mathbb{Z}=p\mathbb{Z}
.
This completes the proof of the claim.

Let
U^{\prime}
be given by the claim. Since
\frac{1}{p}R_{i}
belongs to
\text{poly}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{Z}/p)
and
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
is weakly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible for all
\delta>0
as long as
p\gg_{d,s}\delta^{-O_{d,s}(1)}
by Part (ii), it follows from Theorem
C.3
that
\tau(\Omega)\subseteq V_{p}(R_{i})
. By Corollary
D.11
, if
T=O_{a_{k^{\prime}},K,s}(1)=O_{K,s}(1)
is sufficiently large, then
there exist
Q_{i,j}\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{Z}),0\leq j\leq r
of degree at most
O_{K,s}(1)
such that

(D.29)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{p}(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T}R_{i}=Q_{i,0}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\tilde{f}_{j}Q_{i,j}.\end{split}

By (
D.25
), (
D.26
) and (
D.29
),
we have that

(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T(s-(k^{\prime}-1))}Qg=\sum_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}-2}\frac{(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T}g_{i}}{p^{i}}+\frac{1}{p^{k^{\prime}-1}}(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T}(g_{k^{\prime}-1}+\frac{g_{k^{\prime}}}{p})

with
(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{T}(g_{k^{\prime}-1}+\frac{g_{k^{\prime}}}{p})
being an
O_{K,s}(1)
-good polynomial. We arrive at a contradiction to the minimality of
k^{\prime}
provided that
a_{k^{\prime}-1}\gg_{a_{k^{\prime}},K,s}1
.

In conclusion, we have that
k^{\prime}=0
and thus

(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}})^{Ts}Qg=g^{\prime}

for some
Q\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\nmid Q
and some

a_{0}
-good polynomial
g^{\prime}
.
Then for every
\mathfrak{I}
and every
\mathbb{n}\in\tau(\Omega)+p(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K},
we have
g^{\prime}(\mathbb{n})\in\mathbb{Z}
and thus

(D.30)

(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}(\mathbb{n}))^{Ts}Qg(\mathbb{n})\in\mathbb{Z}.

Our final step is to use a parametrization trick to get rid of the
(p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}(\mathbb{n}))^{Ts}
term in (
D.30
).
The method is very similar to step 3 of the proof of Part (i) of Theorem
D.1
.

We first pick
\mathbb{n}=(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{K})\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
such that
n_{1},\dots,n_{K-1}
are
p
-linearly independent. Fix
1\leq k\leq K
. If
c_{k}=1
, then
recall that
r_{k}\leq k
and
L_{k,j},2\leq j\leq r_{k}
are linearly independent. Since
A
is invertible, by the knowledge of linear algebra, there exists a subset
I_{k}
of
\{1,\dots,d\}
of cardinality
r_{k}-1
such that
\Delta_{k,I_{k}}(\iota(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))\neq 0
. By Lemma
D.3
,
there exist
i_{k,1},i_{k,2}\in I^{c},i_{k,1}\leq i_{k,2}
such that
G_{k,I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2}}(\iota(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))\neq 0
.
If
c_{k}=0
, then
recall that
r_{k}\leq k-1
and
L_{k,j},1\leq j\leq r_{k}
are linearly independent. Since
A
is invertible, by the knowledge of linear algebra, there exists a subset
I_{k}
of
\{1,\dots,d\}
of cardinality
r_{k}
such that
\Delta_{k,I_{k}}(\iota(\mathbb{n}^{\prime}))\neq 0
. Denote
i_{k,1}=i_{k,2}=0
.

Then
\mathfrak{I}=(I_{k},i_{k,1},i_{k,2})_{k=1}^{K}
is an admissible parametrization of
\Omega
. Moreover, since
D_{\mathfrak{I}}(\iota(n))\neq 0
, we have that
p\tilde{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}(\mathbb{n})\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash p\mathbb{Z}
. By (
D.30
), we have
g(\mathbb{n})\in\mathbb{Z}/Q^{\prime}
for some
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N},p\nmid Q^{\prime}
. On the other hand, since
g
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}/p^{s}
, we have that
g(\mathbb{n})\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We now consider the case when
n_{1},\dots,n_{K-1}
are not necessarily
p
-linearly independent.
For
1\leq t\leq K-1
, we say that
Property
t
holds if for all
\mathbb{n}=(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{K})\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
with

n_{1},\dots,n_{t}
being
p
-linearly independent, we have that
g(\mathbb{n})\in\mathbb{Z}
. By the discussion above, Property
K-1
holds.
Assume now that we have proven Property
t+1
holds for some
0\leq t\leq K-2
. We show that Property
t
holds.
Pick any
\mathbb{n}=(\mathbb{n}^{\prime},n_{K})\in\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
such that
n_{1},\dots,n_{t}
are
p
-linearly independent. If
n_{1},\dots,n_{t+1}
are
p
-linearly independent, then
g(\mathbb{n})\in\mathbb{Z}
by induction hypothesis and we are done. So we assume that
n_{1},\dots,n_{t+1}
are
p
-linearly dependent.

By Lemma
D.6
, there exists a subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
dimension
3
such that

•

for all
v\in V\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}
,
\iota(n_{1}),\dots,\iota(n_{t}),v
are linearly independent;

•

for all
v\in V
and
1\leq j\leq K
, we have that
(vA)\cdot\iota(n_{j})=0
;

•

we have that
V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}\to V
be any bijective linear transformation.
Let
W
be the set of
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
such that
(\iota(n_{1}),\dots,\iota(n_{t}),\iota(n_{t+1})+\phi(m),\iota(n_{t+2}),\dots,\iota(n_{K}))\in\Omega
. Note that
\mathbb{0}\in W
.
Since
(\phi(m)A)\cdot\iota(n_{j})=0
for all
1\leq j\leq K
, by (
D.4
) and (
D.5
),
for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq K
and
1\leq j\leq r_{i^{\prime}}
with either
(i^{\prime},j)\neq(t+1,1)
or
(i^{\prime},j)=(t+1,1)
and
c_{t+1}=0
, it is not hard to see that

F_{i^{\prime},j}(\iota(n_{1}),\dots,\iota(n_{t}),\iota(n_{t+1})+\phi(m),\iota(n_{t+2}),\dots,\iota(n_{K}))=F_{i^{\prime},j}(\iota(n_{1}),\dots,\iota(n_{K})).

Moreover, if
(i^{\prime},j)=(t+1,1)
and
c_{t+1}=1
, then by (
D.4
),
we have that

M^{\prime}(m):=F_{t+1,1}(\iota(n_{1}),\dots,\iota(n_{t}),\iota(n_{t+1})+\phi(m),\iota(n_{t+2}),\dots,\iota(n_{K}))

is a quadratic form from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
to
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. It is then not hard to see that

W=V(M^{\prime})
if
c_{t+1}=1
and
W=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
if
c_{t+1}=0
. Since
V\cap V^{\perp_{M}}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
, by Proposition
4.8
(ii),
\text{rank}(M^{\prime})=3
.
So by Propositions
9.9
and
9.10
,
\iota^{-1}(W)
is strongly
(1/2,p)
-irreducible.

On the other hand, for all
m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}
,
since
A

\iota(n_{1}),\dots,\iota(n_{t}),\phi(m)
are linearly independent and since
\iota(n_{1}),\dots,\iota(n_{t+1})
are linearly dependent, we have that
\iota(n_{1}),\dots,

\iota(n_{t}),\iota(n_{t+1})+\phi(m)
are linearly independent.
So
n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\tau\circ\phi(m)+pu
are
p
-linearly independent for all
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
By the induction hypothesis that Property
t+1
holds, we have that

(D.31)

g(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\tau\circ\phi(m)+pu,n_{t+2},\dots,n_{K})\in\mathbb{Z}

for all
m\in W\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}
and
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Let
\phi^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{3}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be the linear transformation induced by the restrictions
\phi^{\prime}(e_{j}):=\tau(\phi(e_{j}))
for
j=1,2,3
, where we slightly abuse the notation to let
e_{j}
denote the
j
-th standard vector of both
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
.
it is not hard to see that
\tau\circ\phi\equiv\phi^{\prime}\circ\tau\mod p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\phi^{\prime}(x+py)-\phi^{\prime}(x)\in p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Let
g^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
be given by

g^{\prime}(x):=g(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\phi^{\prime}(x),n_{t+2},\dots,n_{K})

for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{K-1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
. Since
g\in\text{poly}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{K}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})
, we have that
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{3}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{s})
.

On the other hand, for any
m\in W\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
,
by (
D.31
), there exist
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that

\begin{split}&\quad g^{\prime}(\tau(m)+pz)=g(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\phi^{\prime}(\tau(m)+pz),n_{t+2},\dots,n_{K})\\
&=g(n_{1},\dots,n_{t},n_{t+1}+\tau\circ\phi^{\prime}(m)+pu,n_{t+2},\dots,n_{K})\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{split}

This implies that

\tau(W)\backslash\{\mathbb{0}\}\subseteq V_{p}(g^{\prime})
. So
|V_{p}(g^{\prime})\cap\tau(W)|\geq|W|-1\geq|W|/2
.
Since
\iota^{-1}(W)
is strongly
(1/2,p)
-irreducible, we have that
\iota^{-1}(W)\subseteq V_{p}(g^{\prime})
. In particular, since
\mathbb{0}\in\tau(W)
, we have that

g(\mathbb{n})=g^{\prime}(\mathbb{0})\in\mathbb{Z}
.

In conclusion, we have that Property
t
holds. By induction, we deduce that Property 0 holds, meaning that
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\subseteq V_{p}(g)
. So
\iota^{-1}(\Omega)
is strongly
(\delta,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
. This completes the proof of Part (iii) of Theorem
D.1
.

Appendix E
Leibman dichotomies for nice and consistent
M
-sets

We present the proof of Theorem
10.6
in this appendix. We restate this theorem below for convenience.

Theorem E.1

(Nice and consistent
M
-sets admit Leibman dichotomies)
.

Let
d,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq\max\{4r+1,4k+3,2k+s+11\}
,
C>0
and
p
be a prime.
There exists
K:=O_{C,d}(1)
such that for any non-degenerate quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
, any nice and consistent
M
-set
\Omega\subseteq(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
of total co-dimension
r
, and any
0<\delta<1/2
, if
p\gg_{C,d}\delta^{-O_{C,d}(1)}
,
then
\Omega
admits a partially periodic
(\delta,K\delta^{-K})
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
.

By Proposition
B.3
(iii) and Lemma
10.5
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\Omega=V(\mathcal{J})
for some nice and consistent
(M,k)
-family
\mathcal{J}
. By Lemma
B.1
,
\mathcal{J}

admits a nice and standard
M
-representation
(F_{i,j}\colon 1\leq i\leq k,1\leq j\leq r_{i})
such that

(E.1)

F_{i,j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})=(n_{i}A)\cdot(a_{i,j,1}n_{1}+\dots+a_{i,j,i}n_{i})+u_{i,j}

for some
a_{i,j,t},u_{i,j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
1\leq i\leq k,1\leq j\leq r_{i}
, such that the matrix
\begin{bmatrix}a_{i,1,i}&\dots&a_{i,1,1}\\
\dots&\dots&\dots\\
a_{i,r_{i},i}&\dots&a_{i,r_{i},1}\end{bmatrix}

is of rank
r_{i}
and is in the reduced row echelon form, where
0\leq r_{i}\leq i
and
r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}=r
. In particular,
F_{i,j}
is independent of
n_{i+1},\dots,n_{k}
and depends nontrivially on
n_{1},\dots,n_{i}
.

Fix any
\delta>0
.
Throughout the proof, assume that
p\gg_{C,d}\delta^{-O_{C,d}(1)}
.
Let
G/\Gamma
be an
s
-step
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of complexity at most
C
and

g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
be such that
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
.

The idea of the proof is inspired by Section 3 of
[
9
]
(see also page 37 of
[
20
]
and Proposition 4.11 of
[
22
]
for similar methods), which we explain briefly. For any bijective
d
-integral linear transformation
\phi\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
, one can show that for many
\mathbb{x}\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k-1}
, the sequence
(g\circ\phi^{-1}(\mathbb{x},n)\Gamma)_{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon(\mathbb{x},n)\in\phi(\Omega)}
is not
O(\delta)
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
. On the other hand, the set
\{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon(\mathbb{x},n)\in\phi(\Omega)\}
can be viewed as the set of zeros of some quadratic form induced by
M
. We may use Theorem
5.1
to conclude that
\eta\circ g\circ\phi^{-1}(\mathbb{x},\cdot)\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant when restricted to
\Omega
for some nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta
for many
\mathbb{x}
. If we take sufficiently many different
\phi
, then we will be able to combine these informations to conclude that
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega
.

We now turn to the rigorous proof of Theorem
E.1
.

Step 1: decomposing
\Omega
using different linear transformations.

Let

N:=\lceil p^{\alpha}\rceil
for any fixed
\frac{k-2}{k-1}<\alpha<1
.
Denote
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1})\otimes n:=(a_{1}n,\dots,a_{k-1}n)\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k-1}
for
a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
For all
v\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}^{k-1}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k-1}
, let

\phi_{v}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
denote the bijective
d
-integral linear transformation given by

\phi_{v}(\mathbb{x},n):=(\mathbb{x}+v\otimes n,n)\text{ for all }\mathbb{x}\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k-1},n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}.

By Proposition
B.3
(iii),
\mathcal{J}_{v}:=\{F_{i,j}\circ\phi_{v}\colon 1\leq i\leq k^{\prime},1\leq j\leq r_{i}\}
is a nice and independent
(M,k)
-family. So
\phi_{v}^{-1}(\Omega)
is a nice and consistent
M
-set with total co-dimension
r
. By Lemma
B.1
,
\phi_{v}^{-1}(\Omega)
admits a nice and standard
M
-representation with some dimension vector
\mathbb{r}_{v}
of dimension
r
. By the Pigeonhole Principle, there exist a subset
T
of
\{0,\dots,N-1\}^{k-1}
of cardinality
\gg_{k,r}N^{k-1}
such that
\mathbb{r}_{v}
equals to the same vector
\mathbb{r}^{\prime}=(r^{\prime}_{1},\dots,r^{\prime}_{k})
with
r^{\prime}_{1}+\dots+r^{\prime}_{k}=r
for all
v\in T
. Since
\phi_{v}^{-1}(\Omega)
is nice, we have
0\leq r^{\prime}_{i}\leq i
.

For convenience denote
D:=d(k-1)-r^{\prime}_{1}-\dots-r^{\prime}_{k-1}.

Let

(\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{v},\mathcal{J}^{\prime\prime}_{v})
be an
\{n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1}\}
-decomposition of
\mathcal{J}_{v}
(recall Appendix
C
for the definition).
Let
\Omega_{v}
denote the set of
(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k-1}
such that
(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in V(\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{v})
for all
n_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, and for each
(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k-1}
, let
\Omega_{v}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1})
denote the set of
n_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})

\in V(\mathcal{J}^{\prime\prime}_{v})
.
Since
d\geq 2r+1
, by Theorem
C.3
and the Pigeonhole Principle, for any
v\in T
, there exist a subset
U_{v}
of
\Omega_{v}
of cardinality
\gg_{d}\delta p^{D}
such that
for all
\mathbb{x}\in U_{v}
,
\Omega_{v}(\mathbb{x})
has a standard
M
-representation with the one dimensional dimension vector
(r^{\prime}_{k})
, and that
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega_{v}(\mathbb{x})}
is not
O_{d}(\delta)
-equidistributed.
Let
J
denote the set of
(v,\mathbb{x})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k-1}\times T
such that
\mathbb{x}\in U_{v}
. Then
|J|\gg_{d}N^{k-1}\delta p^{D}
.

Since
\Omega_{v}(\mathbb{x})
has a standard
M
-representation with the dimension vector
(r^{\prime}_{k})
, we have that either

\Omega_{v}(\mathbb{x})
is the intersection of the zeros of an
(M,1)
-integral quadratic function and an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension
r^{\prime}_{k}-1
, or
\Omega_{v}(\mathbb{x})
is an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension
r_{k}
.
For the former case, since
d-2(r^{\prime}_{k}-1)\geq d-2(k-1)\geq s+13
,
by Proposition
4.8
and Theorem
10.3
, for all
(v,x)\in J
, there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
0<\|\eta_{v,\mathbb{x}}\|\leq O_{C,d}(1)\delta^{-O_{C,d}(1)}
such that
\eta_{v,\mathbb{x}}\circ g(\phi_{v}(\mathbb{x},\cdot))\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega_{v}(\mathbb{x})
.
For the later case, we have the same conclusion by Theorem
10.2
.

By the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists a subset
J_{1}
of
J
of cardinality at least
\gg_{C,d}N^{k-1}\delta^{O_{C,d}(1)}p^{D}
such that
\eta_{v,\mathbb{x}}
equals to the same
\eta
for all
(v,\mathbb{x})\in J_{1}
. Again by the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists a subset
J^{\prime}
of
T
of cardinality
\gg_{C,d}\delta^{O_{C,d}(1)}N^{k-1}
such that for all
v\in J^{\prime}
, the set
J^{\prime}(v):=\{\mathbb{x}\colon(\mathbb{x},v)\in J_{1}\}
is of cardinality
\gg_{C,d}\delta^{O_{C,d}(1)}p^{D}
.
Since
N:=\lceil p^{\alpha}\rceil
, we have that
|J^{\prime}|>p^{k-2}
.
Similar to the proof of Lemma
4.11
, there exist
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in J^{\prime}
such that
(1,v_{1}),\dots,(1,v_{k})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
are linearly independent.

Denote
F:=\eta\circ g
. We wish to show that
F\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega
.

Step 2: deriving an invariance property for
F
.

It is convenient to transform
\Omega
by the bijective
d
-integral linear transformation
\psi\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
given by

\psi(n_{1},\dots,n_{k}):=(v_{1}\otimes n_{1}+\dots+v_{k}\otimes n_{k},n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}),

and temporarily work with the function
F^{\prime}:=\eta\circ g\circ\psi
.
Our next step is to combine the information we get for
F
from the linear transformations
\phi_{v_{1}},\dots,\phi_{v_{k}}
.
For all
1\leq i\leq k
,
Let
\psi_{i}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
be the bijective
d
-integral linear transformation given by

\psi_{i}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k}):=\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{k}(v_{j}-v_{i})\otimes n_{j},n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}\Bigr).

Let
U_{i}\subseteq\psi^{-1}_{i}(\Omega)
be the set of
y\in\psi^{-1}_{i}(\Omega)
such that
\psi_{i}(y)=(\mathbb{x},n)
for some
\mathbb{x}\in J^{\prime}(v_{i})
and
n\in\Omega_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x})
.
For such
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})\in\psi^{-1}_{i}(\Omega)
, we have that
n=y_{1}+\dots+y_{k}
and
\mathbb{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(v_{j}-v_{i})\otimes y_{j}
. So

(E.2)

\begin{split}&\quad F^{\prime}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})=\eta\circ g(v_{1}\otimes y_{1}+\dots+v_{k}\otimes y_{k},y_{1}+\dots+y_{k})\\
&=\eta\circ g(\mathbb{x}+v_{i}\otimes n,n)=\eta\circ g\circ\phi_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x},n).\end{split}

For convenience denote
\mathbb{y}:=(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},y_{i},y_{i+1},\dots,y_{k})
,
\mathbb{y}^{\prime}:=(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},y^{\prime}_{i},y_{i+1},\dots,

y_{k})
and
\tilde{\mathbb{y}}:=(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i},y_{i+1},\dots,y_{k})
, where
y_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Let
\Omega^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}
denote the set of
\tilde{\mathbb{y}}\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}
such that
\mathbb{y},\mathbb{y}^{\prime}\in\psi^{-1}(\Omega),
and
U^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}
denote the set of
\tilde{\mathbb{y}}\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}
such that
\mathbb{y},\mathbb{y}^{\prime}\in U_{i}.

Pick any
\tilde{\mathbb{y}}\in U^{\prime}_{i}
. Assume that
\mathbb{y}=(\mathbb{x},n)
and
\mathbb{y}^{\prime}=(\mathbb{x}^{\prime},n^{\prime})
for some
\mathbb{x},\mathbb{x}^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}(v_{i})
,
n\in\Omega_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x})
and
n^{\prime}\in\Omega_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x}^{\prime})
. Then
\mathbb{x}=\mathbb{x}^{\prime}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq k,j\neq i}(v_{j}-v_{i})\otimes y_{j}
. Since
\eta\circ g(\phi_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x},\cdot))\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x})
, we have that

\eta\circ g\circ\phi_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x},n)\equiv\eta\circ g\circ\phi_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x},n^{\prime})\mod\mathbb{Z}.

So it follows from
(
E.2
) that

(E.3)

F^{\prime}(\mathbb{y})\equiv F^{\prime}(\mathbb{y}^{\prime})\mod\mathbb{Z}

for all
\tilde{\mathbb{y}}\in U^{\prime}_{i}
.
Since
\Omega=\cap_{t=1}^{k}\cap_{j=1}^{r_{t}}V(F_{t,j})
and
F_{t,j}
is independent of
n_{t+1},\dots,n_{k}
, we have that

\psi(\Omega^{\prime}_{i})=(\cap_{t=1}^{i-1}\cap_{j=1}^{r_{t}}V(F_{t,j,i}))\cap(\cap_{t=i}^{k}\cap_{j=1}^{r_{t}}(V(F_{t,j,i})\cap V(F^{\prime}_{t,j,i}))),

where
F_{t,j,i},F^{\prime}_{t,j,i}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
are the
d
-integral linear transformations given by

F_{t,j,i}(\tilde{\mathbb{y}}):=F_{t,j}(\mathbb{y})
and
F^{\prime}_{t,j,i}(\tilde{\mathbb{y}}):=F_{t,j}(\mathbb{y}^{\prime}).

By Proposition
C.8
(iv) and (v),

\Omega_{i}^{\prime}
is a consistent
M
-set of total co-dimension at most
2r
. On the other hand, since
F_{t,j}
are nice, so are
F_{t,j,i},F^{\prime}_{t,j,i}
. So
\psi(\Omega_{i}^{\prime})
and thus
\Omega_{i}^{\prime}
is nice and consistent.
Let
\kappa\gg_{C,d}\delta^{O_{C,d}(1)}
to be chosen later. By Theorem
D.1
, if
d\geq\max\{4r+1,4k+3\}
and
p\gg_{C,d}\delta^{-O_{d,C}(1)}\gg_{C,d}\kappa^{-O_{C,d}(1)}
, then
\iota^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime}_{i})
is strongly
(\kappa,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
.

Since
\Omega_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x})
has an
M
-representation with dimension vector
(r^{\prime}_{k})
for all
\mathbb{x}\in U_{v_{i}}
, by Theorem
C.3
,

|U^{\prime}_{i}|=\sum_{\mathbb{x}\in J^{\prime}(v_{i})}|\Omega_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x})|^{2}\gg_{C,d}\delta^{-O_{C,d}(1)}p^{2(d-r^{\prime}_{k})+D}

and

|\Omega^{\prime}_{i}|=\sum_{\mathbb{x}\in\Omega_{v_{i}}}|\Omega_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{x})|^{2}\gg_{C,d}\delta^{-O_{C,d}(1)}p^{2(d-r^{\prime}_{k})+D}.

By Proposition
3.24
,
there exists
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\psi^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
such that
g^{\prime}(n)\Gamma=g\circ\psi(n)\Gamma
for all
n\in\psi^{-1}(\Omega)
.
So by Lemma
3.28
,
F
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}/p^{s}+C
for some
C\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
\iota^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime}_{i})
is strongly
(\kappa,p)
-irreducible up to degree
s
, if
\kappa\gg_{C,d}\delta^{O_{C,d}(1)}
is chosen to be sufficiently small, then it follows from (
E.3
) that

F^{\prime}(\mathbb{y})\equiv F^{\prime}(\mathbb{y}^{\prime})\mod\mathbb{Z}

for all
\tilde{\mathbb{y}}\in\Omega^{\prime}_{i}
. By the definition of
\Omega^{\prime}_{i}
and
F^{\prime}
, we have that

(E.4)

F(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},y_{i},y_{i+1},\dots,y_{k})\equiv F(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},y^{\prime}_{i},y_{i+1},\dots,y_{k})\mod\mathbb{Z}

for all
1\leq i\leq k
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{k}),(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},y^{\prime}_{i},y_{i+1},\dots,y_{k})\in\Omega
.

Step 3: showing that
F
is a constant on
\Omega
.

If
\Omega=(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
, then (
E.4
) immediately implies that
F\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant and we are done. However, in general this conclusion is not straightforward. In order to show that
F\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega
, we need to change one coordinate at each time carefully.

Fix any
(m_{1},\dots,m_{k}),(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\Omega
with
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\}\cap\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
.

Claim.
There exists

(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\in\Omega
such that

(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},m_{i+1},\dots,m_{k}),(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{k})\in\Omega
for all
0\leq i\leq k
.

Suppose we have chosen
z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1}
for some
1\leq i\leq k
such that for all
0\leq i^{\prime}\leq i-1
, we have that
z_{1},\dots,z_{i^{\prime}},m_{i^{\prime}+2},\dots,m_{k},n_{i^{\prime}+2},\dots,n_{k}
are linearly independent and that

(E.5)

(z_{1},\dots,z_{i^{\prime}},m_{i^{\prime}+1},\dots,m_{k}),(z_{1},\dots,z_{i^{\prime}},n_{i^{\prime}+1},\dots,n_{k})\in\Omega

(there is nothing to prove when
i=1
).
We show that there exists
z_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
z_{1},\dots,z_{i},m_{i+2},\dots,m_{k},n_{i+2},\dots,n_{k}
are linearly independent and that

(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},m_{i+1},\dots,m_{k}),(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{k})\in\Omega.

Let
W
denote the span of
z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},m_{i+2},\dots,m_{k},n_{i+2},\dots,n_{k}
and
W^{\prime}
denote the set of
z_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that

F_{t,j}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},m_{i+1},\dots,m_{k})=F_{t,j}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{k})=0

for all
1\leq t\leq k,1\leq j\leq r_{t}
.
It suffices to show that
|W^{\prime}|>|W|
.

By (
E.1
), the knowledge of linear algebra, and the construction of
F_{t,j}
, it is not hard to see that
\mathcal{J}

admits another nice and independent (but not necessarily standard)
M
-representation
(F^{\prime}_{t,j}\colon 1\leq t\leq k,1\leq j\leq r_{t})
such that

F^{\prime}_{t,j}=F_{t,j}
for all
1\leq t\leq i-1,1\leq j\leq r_{t}
, and that

(E.6)

F^{\prime}_{t,j}(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})=(n_{t}A)\cdot(a^{\prime}_{t,j,1}n_{1}+\dots+a^{\prime}_{t,j,t}n_{t})+u^{\prime}_{t,j}

for some
a^{\prime}_{t,j,t^{\prime}},u^{\prime}_{t,j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
i\leq t\leq k,1\leq j\leq r_{t}
, such that the matrix
\begin{bmatrix}a^{\prime}_{t,1,t}&\dots&a^{\prime}_{t,1,1}\\
\dots&\dots&\dots\\
a^{\prime}_{t,r_{t},t}&\dots&a^{\prime}_{t,r_{t},1}\end{bmatrix}

is of rank
r_{t}
(but is not necessarily in the row echelon form) and that
a^{\prime}_{t,j,i}=0
for all
j\geq 2
. Then
W^{\prime}
can be described as the set of
z_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that

(E.7)

F^{\prime}_{t,j}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},m_{i+1},\dots,m_{k})=F^{\prime}_{t,j}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{k})=0

for all
1\leq t\leq k,1\leq j\leq r_{t}
. We remark that
r_{t}\leq t
.

For convenience denote

\mathbb{w}:=(z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},m_{i+1},\dots,m_{k},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{k}).

For
1\leq t\leq i-1,1\leq j\leq r_{t}
, since

F^{\prime}_{t,j}=F_{t,j}
is independent of
n_{t+1},\dots,n_{k}
, we have (
E.7
) for free because of (
E.5
).
For
i+1\leq t\leq k,1\leq j\leq r_{t}
, suppose that either
j=1,a^{\prime}_{t,1,i}=0
or
j\geq 2
(in which case we still have
a^{\prime}_{t,j,i}=0
). Then
F^{\prime}_{t,j}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
is independent of
x_{i}
.
By (
E.5
), we have that

F^{\prime}_{t,j}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i},y_{i+1},\dots,y_{k})=F^{\prime}_{t,j}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},y_{i},\dots,y_{k})=0

for
y=m
or
n
. So we have (
E.7
) for free.
For
i+1\leq t\leq k,j=1
with
a^{\prime}_{t,1,i}\neq 0
, by (
E.6
), we have that (
E.7
) holds if and only if

(E.8)

a^{\prime}_{t,1,i}(z_{i}A)\cdot m_{t}+P_{t}(\mathbb{w})=a^{\prime}_{t,1,i}(z_{i}A)\cdot n_{t}+P^{\prime}_{t}(\mathbb{w})=0

for some functions
P_{t},P^{\prime}_{t}
. For
t=i,2\leq j\leq r_{t}
, since
a^{\prime}_{i,j,i}=0
, by (
E.6
), we have that (
E.7
) holds if and only if

(E.9)

(z_{i}A)\cdot(a^{\prime}_{i,j,1}z_{1}+\dots+a^{\prime}_{i,j,i-1}z_{i-1})+u^{\prime}_{i,j}=0.

Finally, for
t=i,j=1
, by (
E.6
), we have that (
E.7
) holds if and only if

(E.10)

Q(z_{i}):=(z_{i}A)\cdot(a^{\prime}_{i,1,1}z_{1}+\dots+a^{\prime}_{i,1,i}z_{i})+u^{\prime}_{i,1}=0.

Since

z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},m_{i+1},\dots,m_{k},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{k}
are linearly independent and the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}a^{\prime}_{i,1,i}&\dots&a^{\prime}_{i,1,1}\\
\dots&\dots&\dots\\
a^{\prime}_{i,r_{i},i}&\dots&a^{\prime}_{i,r_{i},1}\end{bmatrix}

is of rank
r_{i}
, it is not hard to see that the following vectors are linearly independent:

\{a^{\prime}_{i,j,1}z_{1}+\dots+a^{\prime}_{i,j,i-1}z_{i-1}\colon 1\leq j\leq r_{i}\}\cup\{m_{t},n_{t}\colon i+1\leq t\leq k\}.

Since
A
is invertible, by (
E.8
), (
E.9
) and (
E.10
), if
a^{\prime}_{i,1,i}=0
, then
W^{\prime}
is an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension at most
r_{i}+2(k-i)
; if
a^{\prime}_{i,1,i}\neq 0
, then
Q
is a non-degenerate quadratic form and
W^{\prime}
is the intersection
V(M)
and an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of co-dimension at most
r_{i}+2(k-i)-1\leq 2k-i-1
. Since
d-2(2k-i-1)\geq 3
, by Corollary
4.14
(ii), in both cases we have that
|W^{\prime}|=p^{d-(r_{i}+2(k-i))}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
.
On the other hand,
|W|\leq p^{2k-i-3}
. Since
d-(r_{i}+2(k-i))>2k-i-3
, there exists
z_{i}\in W^{\prime}\backslash W
.
By induction, we complete the proof of the claim.

Let
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
be given by the claim. By (
E.4
),

\begin{split}&\quad F(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})\equiv F(z_{1},m_{2},\dots,m_{k})\equiv\dots\equiv F(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\\
&\equiv F(z_{1},\dots,z_{k-1},n_{k})\equiv\dots\equiv F(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\mod\mathbb{Z}.\end{split}

For general
(m_{1},\dots,m_{k}),(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\Omega
, similar to the proof of Proposition
4.8
, the number of
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}
such that either
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\}\cap\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}
or
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\}\cap\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}
is nontrivial is at most
O_{k}(p^{d(k-1)+2k-1})
. On the other hand
|\Omega|=p^{dk-r}(1+O_{k}(p^{-1/2}))
by Theorem
C.3
. Since
dk-r>d(k-1)+2k-1
, there exists
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\in\Omega
such that
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\}\cap\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\}\cap\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
. So by the previous case,

F(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})\equiv F(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\equiv F(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\mod\mathbb{Z}.

Therefore
\eta\circ g\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant on
\Omega
. So
\Omega
admits a partially periodic
(\delta,K\delta^{-K})
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity
C
for some function
K
depending only on
C,d
. This completes the proof of Theorem
E.1
.

Remark E.2
.

Motivated by Theorem
E.1
, it is natural to ask if any consistent
M
-set admits a partially periodic Leibman dichotomy. Unfortunately the approach used in Theorem
E.1
can not be applied directly to general consistent
M
-sets. This is because the equation (
E.4
) is insufficient to conclude that
F\mod\mathbb{Z}
is a constant if
\Omega
is not nice. For example, let
\Omega
be the set of
(m_{1},\dots,m_{d},n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{2}
such that
m_{1}=n_{1}
, and let
F(m_{1},\dots,m_{d},n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):=\frac{1}{p}\tau(m_{1})
. Then (
E.4
) holds since
F(m_{1},\dots,m_{d},n_{1},\dots,n_{d})=F(m_{1},\dots,m_{d},n^{\prime}_{1},\dots,n^{\prime}_{d})
for all
(m_{1},\dots,m_{d},n_{1},\dots,n_{d}),(m_{1},\dots,m_{d},n^{\prime}_{1},\dots,n^{\prime}_{d})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. However,
F\mod\mathbb{Z}
is not a constant on
\Omega
.

One possible way to avoid this issue is the do a change of variable under a linear transformation
L\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{t}
for some
t\in\mathbb{N}
to eliminate all the polynomials of degree 1 in the standard
M
-representation of
\Omega
in the initial step. However,
L
in general is not
d
-integral, and thus
L(\Omega)
is no longer an
M
-set. Therefore, one needs to generalize the definition of
M
-sets (as well as all the results relevant to
M
-sets) to a larger class of sets in order to overcome this difficulty. Since Theorem
E.1
is good enough for our purposes, we do not pursuit this improvement.
